NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
1. Executive Summary 
Product Introduction 
Berotralstat is an orally administered inhibitor of active plasma kallikrein for prophylaxis to 
prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 12 years of age and older. 
Berotralstat is a new molecular entity (NME) and not approved for use in any country. The 
dosing regimen for approval consists of a recommended starting dose of 150 mg orally once 
daily and 110 mg orally once daily for patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment or 
for patients with persistent gastrointestinal (GI) adverse reactions. 
Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness 
The recommended regulatory action for this application is Approval of orally administered 
berotralstat 150 mg once daily for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 years of 
age and older. To support this application, the Applicant conducted a single 24-week, adequate, 
well-controlled pivotal efficacy and safety trial in adolescents and adults with type I or II HAE. 
The results from this trial demonstrated evidence of efficacy for both the 110 mg and 150 mg 
doses of berotralstat compared to placebo for the primary endpoint of HAE attack rate over 
24 weeks and the predefined exploratory endpoint of a ш50% reduction in attack rate 
compared to baseline. The study had adequate statistical power to detect a clinically 
meaningful difference between berotralstat treatment versus placebo. Based on the robustness 
of the results and the rarity of the disease, a single pivotal trial is adequate to demonstrate 
substantial evidence of effectiveness. The Division did not deem the results from the first key 
secondary endpoint, the angioedema quality-of-life (AE-QoL) questionnaire score, fit for 
purpose for labeling. Nevertheless the results were not statistically significant versus placebo 
for either treatment group and further secondary endpoints were therefore not tested because 
of the hierarchal analysis. The numerically greater and more clinically significant treatment 
effect in the high-dose arm supports a recommended dose of 150 mg once daily. However, the 
totality of safety and efficacy data also support use of the 110 mg once-daily dose for patients 
who may not tolerate the higher dose due to GI-related adverse reactions or who have 
moderate to severe hepatic impairment.

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Benefit-Risk Assessment 
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment 
Berotralstat is an orally administered inhibitor of active plasma kallikrein for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary 
angioedema (HAE) in patients 12 years of age and older. The recommended regulatory action of this NDA is Approval. 
Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a rare, autosomal dominant, inherited condition characterized by intermittent, unpredictable 
attacks of angioedema. HAE is caused by mutations in the SERPING1 gene, resulting in deficient or dysfunctional C1-esterase 
inhibitor (C1-INH) protein, a serine protease inhibitor. Absence of functional C1-INH leads to dysregulation of the contact system, 
unchecked kallikrein activity, and ultimately widespread release of bradykinin, which increases vascular permeability causing the 
characteristic swelling of acute HAE attacks. Although the exact prevalence is unknown, HAE is estimated to affect 1 in 10,000 to 
50,000 individuals worldwide (approximately 6,000 to 10,000 individuals in the United States) and is categorized as an orphan 
disease. Acute attacks of HAE are potentially life-threatening, particularly in cases of laryngeal edema resulting in airway 
compromise. Attacks at other anatomic sites (e.g., gastrointestinal [GI] tract, genitourinary tract, and skin on the hands, feet, and 
face) can cause disabling pain and significant morbidity. These attacks are highly variable in frequency and location among 
individuals and even within a given individual. Patients with particularly severe and/or frequent attacks may be treated with 
prophylactic therapies to prevent or reduce acute attacks. While the context of available therapies for prophylaxis as well as 
treatment of acute attacks has expanded greatly in the last decade, most therapies require subcutaneous injection or intravenous 
infusion. Danazol, an attenuated androgen, is the only oral therapy approved for HAE prophylaxis; however, safety and tolerability 
issues limit its use. 
The efficacy and safety of berotralstat was evaluated in a single, well-controlled, adequately designed pivotal trial in Type I and II HAE 
patients 12 years of age and older. Patients had a minimum of 2 HAE attacks over a 2-month run-in period. Berotralstat treatment for 
24 weeks versus placebo resulted in statistically significant and clinically meaningful reductions in the rate of HAE attacks. The 110 and 
150 mg doses of berotralstat reduced the rate of HAE attacks by 30% (p=0.024) and 44% (p<0.001) relative to placebo, respectively. 
Efficacy was further supported by additional endpoints such as proportion of patients with at least a 50% reduction in HAE attacks 
compared to baseline. While the study demonstrated a significant treatment benefit in a patient population with frequent attacks who 
are likely to require prophylaxis, the treatment response was modest as few subjects achieved complete elimination of attacks. 
However, an oral therapy provides an additional, convenient option to the treatment armamentarium. 
The safety data submitted with the NDA were sufficient to assess the safety of berotralstat in the proposed HAE population. 
Gastrointestinal-related adverse events and transaminase elevations emerged as the main safety signals. Adverse reactions such 
as abdominal pain, vomiting, diarrhea, dyspepsia and gastroesophageal reflux occurred more frequently, often in a dose-

dependent manner, with berotralstat compared to placebo. However , GI-related events were generally mild-to-moderate severity 
and self- limited , and typica lly did not lead to study drug discontinuation. Adverse events related to transaminase elevations, 
primarily increased ALT, were reported with berotra lstat treatment in the controlled and open label extension studies . Few 
subjects had marked elevations triggering treatment discontinuation, and no potentia l Hy's law cases were identified in the 
program . Transaminase elevations were genera lly transient, asymptomatic , not dose-dependent, and in some cases confounded 
by underlying co-morbidities and prior androgen use. In general , safety data from the open labe l extension study were consistent 
with the placebo contro lled data and support chronic berotralstat use. Although not observed in clinica l trials , QT pro longation is 
an anticipated risk with higher than recommended doses based on the pharmacologic action of the drug. No safety concerns were 
identified that shou ld preclude approva l or require a REMS. 
Efficacy and safety results across various demographic and baseline characteristic subgroups were genera lly consistent with the 
overa ll findings. Given the small number of adolescent subjects, efficacy in this age group is primarily based on extrapo lation from 
efficacy data in adu lts because the pathophysiology of disease is similar irrespective of age. The numerically greater and more 
clinica lly significant treatment effect in the high-dose arm supports a recommended dose of 150 mg once daily. However , the 
tota lity of safety and efficacy data also support use of the 110 mg once-dai ly dose for patients who may not tolerate the higher 
dose due to GI-re lated adverse reactions or who have moderate to severe hepatic impairment . Overall, the risk/benefit profi le for 
berotra lstat in the treatment of a rare, serious disease such as HAE is favorab le.

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
2. Therapeutic Context 
Analysis of Condition 
Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a rare, autosomal dominant, inherited condition characterized 
by intermittent, unpredictable attacks of angioedema. HAE is caused by mutations in the 
SERPING1 gene, resulting in deficiency or dysfunction of C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) protein, 
a serine protease inhibitor. Approximately 85% of patients have type I HAE, characterized by 
low production of normal C1-INH protein; the remaining 15% of patients have type II HAE, 
characterized by normal production and levels of dysfunctional C1-INH. The absence of 
functional C1-INH leads to dysregulation of the contact system, a plasma protease cascade 
initiated by factor XII that activates the proinflammatory kallikrein-kinin system (Figure 1) and 
the procoagulant intrinsic coagulation pathway. Ordinarily, kallikrein activity is regulated by 
C1-INH, but in patients with HAE, kallikrein activity is unchecked, leading to widespread release 
of bradykinin. The resulting elevated level of bradykinin increases vascular permeability, leading 
to the characteristic swelling of acute HAE attacks. 
Figure 1 provides a more detailed representation of how C1-INH functions within the contact, 
complement, and fibrinolytic systems. C1-INH is an endogenous inhibitor of the serine protease 
enzymes Factor XIIa and plasma kallikrein. Factor XIIa converts prekallikrein to active plasma 
kallikrein, which in turn cleaves high-molecular-weight kininogen (HMWK) to form bradykinin 
and cleaved HMWK. Bradykinin is a polypeptide signaling molecule that participates in multiple 
biological processes, including blood coagulation, activation of inflammation, and regulation of 
blood pressure. 
Patients with HAE have uncontrolled plasma kallikrein production and pathologic levels of 
bradykinin. The key triggering events of HAE attacks are not completely understood. Activation 
by bradykinin of bradykinin B2 receptors on the surface of endothelial cells leads to 
vasodilation and decreased blood pressure. Activation of B2 receptors also results in the 
vascular leakage and subsequent tissue swelling characteristic of HAE attacks. Bradykinin is 
converted to des-Arg9 bradykinin in the plasma by carboxypeptidase N. Des-Arg9 bradykinin 
activates bradykinin B1 receptors, thus contributing to symptoms of HAE. Berotralstat is 
designed to block the enzymatic function of plasma kallikrein and so prevent the proteolytic 
conversion of HMWK to bradykinin1.

active site blocking antibody, J Biol Chem, 289(34):23596-23608; and Zuraw, BL, JA Bernstein, DM Lang,

Source: World Allergy Organization Global Hereditary Angioedema Practice Parameter 
Abbreviations: C, complement; C1-INH, C1-esterase inhibitor; HMW-K, high-molecular-weight kininogen 
Although the exact prevalence is unknown, HAE is estimated to affect 1 in 10,000 to 50,000 
individuals worldwide (approximately 6,000 to 10,000 individuals in the United States) and is 
categorized as an orphan disease. Despite its autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, roughly 
one quarter of patients with HAE have spontaneous mutations, and diagnosis is often delayed 
by several years. The age at which attacks begin is variable, but symptoms during infancy are 
exceedingly rare. Based on literature reports, the median age of symptom onset is between 6 
and 11 years. The attack frequency increases after puberty and continues throughout an 
individual’s life. The acute attacks of HAE are potentially life-threatening, particularly in cases of 
laryngeal edema resulting in airway compromise. Attacks at other anatomic sites (e.g., 
gastrointestinal [GI] tract, genitourinary tract, hands, feet, and face) can cause disabling pain 
and significant morbidity. These attacks are highly variable in frequency and location both 
among individuals and within a given individual. If untreated, attacks may occur on average 
every 1 to 2 weeks and typically last 48 to 72 hours. The therapies currently used by patients 
with HAE to manage their disease are listed in Section 2.2. 
The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) convened a Patient-Focused Drug Development public 
meeting for HAE on September 25, 2017, during which FDA staff heard directly from patients, 
families, and caregivers about their experiences with HAE attacks and the life-changing impact 
of available therapies. Approximately 110 patients with HAE and their representatives were 
present at the meeting and a further 52 participated by means of a live webcast. The discussion 
at the meeting focused on two key topics: 1) the disease symptoms and daily impacts that 
matter most to patients and caregivers and 2) patients’ perspectives on current approaches to 
treating HAE and on participating in clinical studies. The Voice of the Patient report highlighted 
the following overarching themes from the discussion that took place during the meeting: 
x Diagnostic delays and lack of awareness of HAE and recognition of signs/symptoms by 
healthcare providers. 
x Unpredictability of attacks. 
x Pain, disability, and disfigurement that result from attacks. 
x Emotional stress that accompanies the condition. 
x Impact on work, family, and social life. 
x Life-changing effect of newer approved treatments.

• 	 Unmet need for treatments that are less invasive, imp rove predictability of attacks, have 
fewer side effects , and ultimately lead to cure . 
• 	 Areas for future researc h include hormona l influe nces on the disease, therapies for 
children , and therapies for HAE types that are less responsive to currently availab le 
therapies. 
• 	 Willingness to participate in clinical trials and natural history studies. 
2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options 
Medications used to treat HAE patients are typically categorized as treatments for acute attacks 
or prophylaxis. Although currently available treatments (Table 1) for routine prophylaxis of 
acute HAE attacks are effective in reducing the ir number and frequency , they do not eliminate 
all attacks in every ind ividual. Whi le fresh frozen plasma and antifibrino lytics (tranexamic acid, 
e-aminocaproic acid) are available for off-label use in HAE, their use has fallen out of favor 
following the availability of effective FDA-approved the rapies .2

3. Regulatory Background 
3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History 
Berotralstat is not approved or marketed in the United States or any foreign country for any 
other indication. 
3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity 
Berotralstat (BCX7353) was deve loped under investigational new drug (IND) application 
135058 , which was opened on June 22, 2017. Prior to opening the IND, the Applicant had 
multiple ex-US clinical studies (comp leted and ongoing) evaluating the use of berotra lstat for 
prevention of HAE attacks . Berotralstat was granted orphan therapy designation on November 
1, 2017, and fast-track designation on August 2, 2018 . Relevant interactions between the 
Agency and App licant to discuss the clinica l development program are summarized below.

Of note, the Applicant has a related IND 142879 for the treatment of acute HAE attacks with 
berotralstat. The higher proposed doses for treatment of acute HAE attacks generated concern 
regarding the potential for significant QT prolongation, particularly if used on top of prophylaxis 
therapy. The FDA communicated these concerns to the Applicant and further stated that 
addressing the issue of QT prolongation was an essential component of the development 
program of berotralstat for the treatment of acute HAE attacks (refer to Meeting Minutes dated 
August 20, 2019). 
4. Significant Issues From Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to the 
Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety 
Office of Scientific Investigations 
The pivotal efficacy and safety trial, Study BCX7352-302 Part 1, in the berotralstat development 
program was a multicenter trial in which each site enrolled a small number of subjects. 
Therefore, the potential for any individual study center to impact the efficacy and safety 
findings was limited. Two clinical sites (Dr. Johnston/Site #033 and Dr. Lumry/Site #035) were 
selected for inspection for Study BCX7352-302 Part 1. Both sites were chosen for having a 
relatively large enrollment and higher response rates on the primary efficacy endpoint 
compared to other sites in the United States. The Office of Scientific Investigations reports for 
these sites found them to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practices and their study data to 
be reliably supportive of the requested indication.

5. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology 
Executive Summary 
The nonclinical program of berotralstat consists of studies of its pharmacology, 
pharmacokinetics (PK), and toxicology. Pharmacology studies showed that berotralstat 
inhibited kallikrein activity across species. PK studies showed that orally administered 
berotralstat is bioavailable in animals and humans. There are no major and unique berotralstat 
metabolites in humans. Toxicology studies evaluated the general toxicity, genetic toxicity, 
carcinogenicity, and reproductive and developmental toxicity of berotralstat. General toxicity 
studies included chronic oral toxicity studies in rats and monkeys (treatment durations of up to 
26 and 39 weeks, respectively). Genetic toxicity was evaluated by means of a standard battery 
of assays. Carcinogenicity studies included studies of oral berotralstat with a traditional 2-year 
bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in transgenic mice. Reproductive and developmental 
26 
Version date: October 12, 2018 
Reference ID: 4711626 

[PDF PAGE 28]
   
 
  
    
     
       
   
    
    
     
   
 
   
    
  
 
    
       
 
   
 
   
  
   
  
    
  
     
 
 
   
   
 
    
  
    
  
NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
studies included teratology studies in rats and rabbits, a fertility and early embryonic 
developmental study in rats, and a peri- and post-natal development (PPND) study in rats. 
Carcinogenicity 
No evidence of tumorigenicity was found in mice or rats. The carcinogenicity potential of 
berotralstat was evaluated in a 2-year traditional bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in B6F1 
Tg.RasH2 mice. Wistar rats (60/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 3, 8 and 20 mg/kg 
berotralstat once daily for 104 weeks. Mice (25/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 8, 
20, and 50 mg/kg berotralstat once daily for 26 weeks. Neither study revealed treatment-
related increases in tumor incidences. 
Genetic Toxicity 
Berotralstat produced negative results in the bacterial reverse gene mutation assay, an in vitro 
chromosomal aberration assay in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, and an in vivo 
micronucleus assay in rats. 
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity 
Berotralstat was nonteratogenic in pregnant rats and rabbits dosed with the drug during the 
organogenesis period. Berotralstat did not affect the fertility or PPND of rats. 
Others 
Toxicity studies indicated that berotralstat can result in phospholipidosis and affect the 
cardiovascular (CV) system of animals. 
5.1.4.1. Phospholipidosis 
Repeat-dose administration of berotralstat can cause phospholipidosis in rats and monkeys. 
Phospholipidosis-related findings included vacuoles in the epithelium and mucosa of the 
intestine, kidney, and choroid plexus (rats); foamy macrophages and histiocytes in the spleen, 
thymus, and intestine; and foamy Kupffer cells in the liver. Phospholipidosis was confirmed by 
electronic microscopic examination of Kupffer cells (myelinosomes) in the liver and the urinary 
level of di-22:6-bis(monoacylglycol) phosphate, a biomarker of phospholipidosis. 
Phospholipidosis is a known drug-storage mechanism. There is no evidence that berotralstat 
causes phospholipidosis in humans. 
5.1.4.2. Cardiovascular Effects 
Berotralstat prolonged the QT interval in monkeys and humans. Berotralstat affected the 
conductance of potassium and sodium channels and the membrane action potential in vitro. 
Berotralstat also affected blood pressure, heart rate, and electrocardiogram (ECG) (i.e., slight 
increases in the RR, PR, QT, and QTc intervals, and in the QRS duration) in monkeys. In humans, 
berotralstat at an oral dose of 450 mg (maximum concentration [Cmax] 191 ng/mL) prolongs the 
QT interval by approximately 16 ms. A thorough QT study of the effect of berotralstat on the QT 
interval in humans has been completed.

Biologics license application (BLA) 125277—KALBITOR (ecallantide), a recombinant

Pharmacology 
Berotralstat decreases vascular permeability and edema in patients with HAE by reducing the 
blood level of bradykinin, a potent vasodilator. Berotralstat decreases the bradykinin level by 
inhibiting kallikrein, a plasma serine protease responsible for bradykinin production. Kallikrein 
cleaves and releases bradykinin from HMWK. Kallikrein is inactivated by C1-INH. Patients with 
HAE exhibit excessive kallikrein activity as a result of C1-INH deficiency or dysfunction. 
Primary Pharmacology 
Berotralstat inhibited plasma kallikrein from animals and humans. The half-maximal inhibitory 
concentration (IC50) was 5.2, 5.7, and 5.4nM for rat, monkey, and human kallikrein (healthy 
subjects), respectively (Study BR-7353-005-REV-01). Kallikrein from patients with HAE was less 
sensitive to inhibition by berotralstat than was that from healthy subjects. This was indicated by 
a three-fold difference in IC50 (15.9 and 5.4nM for kallikrein from patients with HAE and healthy 
subjects, respectively; Study BR-7353-006-REV-01). Berotralstat has an inhibition constant of 
0.44nM for isolated/purified human plasma kallikrein (Study BR-7353-001), but the IC50 of 
kallikrein from patients with HAE is a better indicator of potency because of the lack of purified 
enzymes in vivo. Berotralstat inhibited kallikrein-dependent production of bradykinin (half-
maximal effective concentration [EC50] 5.56±1.2nM, Study BR-7353-004). Berotralstat has little 
activity against other serine proteases—the IC50 values for other serine proteases were at least 
4500-fold higher than that for kallikrein (Study BR-7353-002). 
Secondary Pharmacology 
Berotralstat at very high concentrations caused an increase in coagulation time in vitro. The 
concentration required to produce a doubling of the prothrombin time was about 80μM in 
monkey and pig blood, and greater than 100μM in blood from other species. The prothrombin 
time prolongation at the highest concentration tested (10ϬʅD) was 30%, 67%, 68%, 80%, 86%, 
123%, and 144% in dog, rabbit, rat, human, mouse, pig, and monkey blood, respectively.

Safety Pharmacology 
A full battery of in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology studies of berotralstat was completed. 
The in vitro studies evaluated the effects of berotralstat on membrane channel conductance 
and action potential. In ion channel conductance assays, berotralstat inhibited the currents of 
human ether à-go-go-related gene potassium channels expressed in human embryonic kidney 
293 cells, voltage-gated sodium channels (Nav1.5) in rabbit cardiac Purkinje fibers, and human 
voltage-gated calcium channels (hCav1.2) expressed in Chinese hamster ovary cells (IC50 0.29,

150127.DCS).

Toxicology 
General Toxicology 
Repeat-dose toxicity studies of berotralstat of durations of up to 6 months in rats and 9 months 
in monkeys have been completed to evaluate general toxicity. Studies of durations of up to 
6 months and 13 weeks in rats and monkeys, respectively, were reviewed previously. See the 
nonclinical review by Dr. Luqi Pei on July 19, 2017, in IND 135058 (Document Archiving, 
Reporting, and Regulatory Tracking System [DARRTS] number 4126737). 
This review evaluates two 9-month oral toxicity studies of berotralstat in monkeys (Studies 
8001292 and 806-162). These studies were completed by two different laboratories at different 
times and had no overlap in berotralstat doses. Specifically, Studies 8001292 and 806-162 were 
completed in 2015 and 2016, respectively. The berotralstat doses were 0, 1.0, 2.5, 7.5 and 
20 mg/kg/day in Study 8001292 and 0, 30, 55, and 80 mg/kg/day in Study 806-162. The goal of 
the second study was to identify the target organs of toxicity of berotralstat because the first 
study showed no treatment-related effects. 
Study Title/Number: A 39-Week Study of BCX7353 by Oral Gavage in the Cynomolgus Monkey 
with a 13-Week Recovery Period (#8001292) 
x Monkeys treated orally with up to 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 39 weeks did not 
reveal any significant toxicity. 
x Cynomolgus monkeys (4/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 1, 2.5, 7.5, and 
20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 39 weeks. No treatment-related toxicity was observed in 
the 1 to 7.5 mg/kg/day groups. Half of the monkeys in the 20 mg/kg/day group showed 
mild increases (less than three-fold) in the alanine aminotransferase (ALT) level starting 
from 6 months. One female in the 20 mg/kg/day group showed single-cell necrosis of 
hepatocytes in the liver, but without corresponding changes in the levels of liver

Study Title/Number: A 9-Month Study of BCX7353 by Oral Gavage in the Cynomolgus Monkey 
with a 13-Week Recovery Period (#806-162) 
x The target organs of toxicity of berotralstat in monkeys were the kidney, liver, intestine, 
lung, spleen, lymph nodes, and bone marrow. 
x Cynomolgus monkeys were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 30, 55, and 80 mg/kg/day 
berotralstat for up to 273 days. The mid-dose (MD) and high-dose (HD) groups showed 
the following microscopic changes: degeneration (kidney, tubular), hyperplasia (kidney, 
bone marrow, and spleen), hypertrophy (liver, hepatocellular), vacuolation (adrenal, 
cortical), and foamy macrophages (lung, lymph node, and intestine). The foamy 
macrophages were caused by phospholipidosis. The low-dose (LD) group showed foamy 
macrophages in the intestine and lymph nodes. Although the kidney showed the most 
severe tissue damage (tubular degeneration and hyperplasia), the intestine was the 
most sensitive organ. The effects of berotralstat were partially reversible.

Parameter Major Findings 
Histopathology 
Adequate battery: 
Yes 
Special 
evaluations 
Treatment-related findings were observed in the adrenal glands, bone marrow, 
kidney, liver, lung, lymph nodes, intestine, and spleen. As expected, findings were 
more pronounced in the main study; less-marked effects were apparent in the 
interim and recovery periods. 
Histological findings can be grouped into the following: degeneration (kidney, 
tubular), hyperplasia (kidney, bone marrow and spleen), hypertrophy (liver), 
vacuolation (adrenal), and foamy macrophages (lung, lymph node, and intestine). 
Although the kidney showed the most severe tissue damage (tubular 
degeneration and hyperplasia), the intestine was the most sensitive organ. Only 
the intestine showed foamy macrophages in the LD group. The findings were not 
readily reversible. Most findings remained apparent after a recovery period of 
13 weeks. The foamy macrophages were caused by phospholipidosis. See 
Section 16.3 for the incidence table. 
The study measured BMP levels in urine. BMP is an exploratory biomarker of 
phospholipidosis. There were dose- and treatment duration-dependent increases 
in the urinary BMP level (see the table below). The respective increases in the 
urinary BMP level in the LD, MD, and HD groups at week 39 were 21-, 229-, and 
271-fold in males and 29-, 91-, and 192-fold in females.

5.5.2.2. In Vitro Assays in Mammalian Cells 
Study Title/Number: BCX7353: In Vitro Mammalian Chromosome Aberration Assay in Human 
Peripheral Blood Lymphocytes [AE02WJ.341ICH.BTL] 
Key study findings: 
x Berotralstat tested negative in the chromosomal aberration assay in vitro. 
GLP compliance: Yes 
Test system: Human peripheral blood lymphocytes 
Study is valid: Yes. The selection of berotralstat concentrations was reasonable. The highest 
dose analyzed under each treatment condition produced a reduction in mitotic index (50±5%). 
The criteria for a positive response and valid assay were appropriate. The positive controls 
yielded significant increases in the rate of chromosomal aberrations, as is typical of the assay. 
5.5.2.3. In Vivo Clastogenicity Assay in Rodent (Micronucleus Assay) 
Study Title/Number: BCX7353: In Vivo Mammalian Erythrocyte Micronucleus Assay in Rats 
(AE02WJ.125M0121ICH.BTL) 
Key study findings: 
x Berotralstat tested negative in the in vivo micronucleus assay in rats. 
GLP compliance: Yes 
Test system: Sprague–Dawley rats 
Study is valid: Yes. The dose selection of BCX7353 was appropriate (0, 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg 
single dose by oral gavage). The highest dose was half of the lethal dose in the dose-ranging 
study. The criteria for a positive response and valid assay were appropriate. Bone marrow was 
extracted and 4000 polychromatic erythrocytes per animal were analyzed for the frequency of 
micronuclei. Cytotoxicity was assessed by scoring the number of polychromatic erythrocytes 
and normochromatic erythrocytes in at least the first 500 total erythrocytes for each animal.

Carcinogenicity 
The carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat was evaluated in a 2-year traditional bioassay in 
rats and in a 26-week study in transgenic mice (reports #806-161 and #20088905, respectively). 
Wistar rats (60/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 3, 8 and 20 mg/kg berotralstat 
once daily for 104 weeks. Mice (25/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 8, 20, and 
50 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 26 weeks. No evidence of tumorigenicity was observed in rats or 
mice. 
The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) of the Agency evaluated reports 
of these studies on March 10 and July 10, 2020 (final reports in the original submission and 
addendum to the rat report submitted by the Applicant in SDN 029, respectively). The 
Committee concluded that 1) both studies were adequate, and 2) no evidence of 
carcinogenicity was observed in either species. See the ECAC meeting minutes and the 
addendum thereto completed by Dr. Robeena Ariz on March 16 and July 23, 2020, and the 
nonclinical review and an addendum thereto completed by Dr. Luqi Pei on March 24 and July 
23, 2020, respectively (DARRTS IDs 4576054, 4580256, 4645569, and 4645674, respectively). 
The ECAC twice evaluated the carcinogenicity data of the 2-year rat study. The first evaluation 
was performed on March 10, 2020. The ECAC concluded that: 1) berotralstat at 20 mg/kg/day 
for 2 years increased the incidence of hemangiosarcoma in the mesenteric lymph nodes in male 
rats, and 2) the relevance of the tumor finding in rats to humans is unknown. 
Th ECAC’s conclusion about the carcinogenicity of berotralstat in rats differed from that of the 
Applicant. The Applicant argued that hemangiosarcoma associated with berotralstat treatment 
was a species-specific effect. To support their argument, the Applicant assembled a pathology 
expert working group (EWG) to re-evaluate the microscopic changes in the mesenteric lymph 
nodes in male and female rats. By consensus, the EWG revised the tumor type and incidence. 
The Applicant submitted an amendment to the final report on July 9, 2020 (SDN 029), which 
outlined the EWG’s conclusion. The Applicant stated that new data showed that berotralstat 
39

Observations and Results 
Parameter Major Findings 
Mortality There were no deaths in the study. 
Clinical signs The HD group showed audible breathing in both sexes (incidence, three 
and six male and female rats, respectively). 
Body weight The HD males showed decreases of 5% in bodyweight and 12% in 
bodyweight gain in comparison to the control. This was associated with a 
decrease in food consumption. The females showed no treatment-related 
effects. 
Necropsy findings Mating/fertility index: No treatment-related effects were observed in either 
sex. Mating index was 100% in both sexes in all groups. Both fertility and 
fecundity indices ranged between 96% to 100% in both sexes. There 
were no apparent differences in menstrual cycles and duration in females. 
There were 24 pregnant rats in each dose group. 
Cesarean section data: No treatment-related effects were observed. The 
mean number of corpora lutea was 15.7 to 16.8. The mean number of 
implantation sites was 14.2 to 14.9. The mean number of viable embryos 
was 13.2 to 14.1. The mean preimplantation loss was 6.2% to 11.2%. The 
mean number of postimplantation sites was 4.1% to 9.4%. 
Abbreviation: HD, high dose 
Toxicokinetics 
Blood levels of berotralstat were measured at 4 hours postdosing on treatment day 1 in both 
sexes, day 63 in males, and GD 7 in females in toxicokinetics animals (1.0 mL blood/time point, 
n=4) (Table 6). The plasma berotralstat level increased in a dose-related fashion. A significant 
increase (approximately 2.5-fold) in the blood level between the first and last days of treatment 
was observed in both sexes. There was no sex difference in the plasma berotralstat level.

6. Clinical Pharmacology 
Executive Summary 
This is an original (505(b)(1)) NDA submitted by the applicant, BioCryst Pharmaceuticals 
(BioCryst), on December 3, 2019, seeking marketing approval for ORLADEYO (berotralstat), an 
orally administered plasma kallikrein inhibitor indicated for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE 
in patients 12 years of age and older. The proposed dosage form is the active pharmaceutical 
(b) (4)
ingredient (API)  in a hard gelatin capsule with a product strength of 150 mg berotralstat

(also referred to as BCX7353) per capsule. The proposed dose is 150 mg3 orally once daily (QD) 
w i t h o u t  r e g a r d s t o  f o o d .  H A E  i s  a  s e r i o u s ,  p o t e n t i a l l y  l i f e - t h reatening, genetically determined 
disease characterized clinically by recurrent episodes of angioedema of the skin, pharynx, larynx, 
GI tract, genitals, and extremities. Because there are no oral medications recommended as first-
line prophylactic treatment of HAE, berotralstat was granted or phan drug designation on 
November 1, 2017 and fast track designation on August 2, 2018. 
The NDA for berotralstat is supported by studies conducted by B ioCryst (IND 135058) and 
includes 11 phase-1 clinical pharmacology studies in healthy subjects and subjects with renal and 
hepatic impairment, 2 phase-2 studies, and 1 phase-3 study in s ubjects with HAE. The program 
was also supported by the results of several in vitro human biomaterial studies. The major 
findings for this application are summarized in Section 6.2. 
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology/Division of Inflammation an d Immune Pharmacology and 
Pharmacometrics have reviewed th e clinical pharmacology data su bmitted in support of NDA 
214094 for berotralstat and found them to be acceptable to supp ort approval from a clinical 
pharmacology perspective.

Dose		 The AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau of berotralstat showed a greater than dose-

respectively, with approximately 10% loss in incubations without the cofactor 
NADPH.
	
Following a single dose of 300 mg [14C]-BCX7353, unchanged berotralstat 
accounted for 34% of plasma radioactivity. Eight metabolites were detected,
	
each accounted for <8% of the radioactivity. In vitro and in vivo metabolic
	
pathways include oxidation, oxidative deamination, hydrogenation, 
N-dealkylation, N-acetylation, and N-carbamoylation.
	
Excretion 
Primary After a single dose of 300 mg [14C]-BCX7353, approximately 85.5% of the 
excretion radioactivity administered was excreted over a 50-day collection period; the 
pathways predominant component in urine and feces was unchanged berotralstat, but 
metabolites were also identified in both urine and feces. Overall, 79.4% and 
9.2% of the administered dose was excreted in feces and urine, respectively. 
Intrinsic Factors 
Renal impairment		 The AUC and Cmax of berotralstat were increased by approximately 13 and 47%, 
respectively, in subjects with severe renal impairment compared to healthy 
subjects. This change in exposure does not require dose adjustment of 
berotralstat.

Type of Parameter		 Description 
Hepatic impairment		 There was no change in berotralstat exposure in subjects with mild hepatic 
impairment (Child-Pugh A) compared to healthy subjects. The AUC and Cmax 
were increased by ~80%, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment 
(Child–Pugh B), whereas the Cmax increase in subjects with severe hepatic 
impairment (Child–Pugh C) was 27% (there was no AUC increase), in 
comparison to healthy subjects. Although the exposure increase in subjects 
with severe hepatic impairment was less than that in those with moderate 
impairment, there was a 2.6-fold decrease in protein binding in this 
subpopulation. Additionally, there was a 50% increase in the berotralstat t1/2 in 
subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. In patients with 
moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh B or C, respectively), the 
recommended dose of berotralstat is 110 mg QD. 
Weight		 Slightly higher exposure of berotralstat (~15%) was predicted in adults of 40 to 
60 kg bodyweight and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age, compared to adults of 
60 to 80 kg bodyweight. This increase is not considered sufficiently clinically 
meaningful to warrant dose adjustment in this subpopulation. See the 
pharmacometrics review by Dr. Jing Niu in Section 16.3.2 for further details. 
Extrinsic Factors and Drug Interactions 
PK of		 Berotralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. The berotralstat Cmax and AUC 
berotralstat 	 were increased by 25% and 69%, respectively, with concomitant administration 
affected		 of cyclosporine, a P-gp and BCRP inhibitor. Dose adjustment (110 mg QD) is 
recommended when berotralstat is administered with P-gp and/or BCRP 
inhibitors; this will be communicated to the Applicant during labeling 
interactions Additionally, P-gp inducers (e.g., rifampin, St. John’s wort) may 
decrease berotralstat plasma concentration, leading to reduced efficacy of 
berotralstat. Use of berotralstat with P-gp inducers is not recommended. 
Effect of 	 Berotralstat 150 mg QD is a moderate inhibitor of CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 and a 
berotralstat on 	 weak inhibitor of CYP2C9 and CYP2C19. Berotralstat at 300 mg is an inhibitor 
the PK of other		 of P-gp but not of BCRP. Appropriate monitoring and dose titration are 
drugs		 recommended for concomitant medications with a narrow therapeutic index that 
are predominantly metabolized by CYP2D6 (e.g., thioridazine, pimozide) or 
CYP3A4 (e.g., cyclosporine, fentanyl) or are sensitive P-gp substrates. 
CYP2C9 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the 
Cmax and AUC of tolbutamide were increased by 19% and 73%, respectively. 
CYP2C19 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the 
Cmax and AUC of omeprazole were increased by 21% and 24%, respectively. 
CYP3A4 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the 
Cmax and AUC of midazolam were increased by 45% and 124%, respectively; 
the Cmax and AUC of amlodipine were increased by 45% and 77%, respectively; 
and the Cmax and AUC of danazol were decreased by approximately 20%. 
CYP2D6 substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 150 mg QD, the 
Cmax and AUC of dextromethorphan were increased by 196% and 178%, 
respectively, and the Cmax and AUC of desipramine were increased by 64% and 
87%, respectively. 
P-gp substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 300 mg QD, the Cmax 
and AUC of digoxin were increased by 58% and 48%, respectively. 
BCRP substrates: Following administration of berotralstat 300 mg QD, the Cmax 
and AUC of rosuvastatin were decreased by approximately 20%.

Pharmacologic Effects 
Kallikrein inhibition		 Kallikrein inhibition was evaluated in clinical studies using an ex vivo assay of 
amidolytic cleavage of a kallikrein-specific substrate in pre- and post-dose 
samples. E-R analyses demonstrated a high correlation between ex vivo 
kallikrein inhibition activity and the plasma concentration of berotralstat. The 
berotralstat concentration and kallikrein inhibition relationship in subjects with 
HAE was well described by a sigmoidal Emax model with a predicted EC50 of 
11 ng/mL. See the pharmacometrics review by Dr. Jing Niu in Section 16.3.2 
for further details.

Summary of the Clinical Pharmacology Assessment 
The key issues identified during the clinical pharmacology review are listed in Table 13. 
Table 13. Key Issues Identified in the Review Process 
Review Issue Recommendations and Comments 
Efficacy and 
safety 
General dosing 
instructions 
The pivotal randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 study (302) 
evaluated two doses (110 mg QD1 and 150 mg QD) for a 24-week treatment 
duration. The study met its primary endpoint for both dose levels, with the 110 and 
150 mg berotralstat doses reducing HAE attacks by 30% (p=0.024) and 44% 
(p<0.001), respectively, compared with placebo. 
Key safety issues noted during the review were GI adverse events of diarrhea, 
nausea or vomiting, and abdominal pain; transient benign maculopapular rash; and 
asymptomatic transaminase elevations. In addition, at a dose of 450 mg (i.e., 
three-fold the 150 mg dose), berotralstat showed a mean QTc increase of 15.9 ms 
(90% upper CI bound of 23.5 ms); the observed increase in QTcF was concentration-
dependent. Refer to the clinical and QT-IRT reviews by Dr. Shaz Siddiqui and Dr. 
Girish Bende, respectively, for more details. 
The Applicant recommends a dose of 150 mg QD for the proposed indication. 
However, based on the clinical assessment of benefit/risk, the review team 
recommends a starting dose of 150 mg QD, with an option of reducing the dose to 
110 mg QD if GI-related adverse events persist. 
The Applicant proposed to dose berotralstat without regards to food. Although food 
had no significant impact on the berotralstat PK, the Applicant administered 
berotralstat with food in the phase 3 study to avoid GI-related adverse events. A 
recommendation to administer berotralstat with food will be reflected in the product 
labeling.

In a dedicated single-dose hepatic impairment study (108), the berotralstat AUC and 
Cmax were increased by ~80%, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child– 
Pugh B), whereas the Cmax increase in subjects with severe hepatic impairment 
(Child–Pugh C) subjects was 27% (there was no AUC increase) in comparison to 
healthy subjects. While the exposure increase in subjects with severe hepatic 
impairment was less than that in those with moderate impairment, there was a 
Dosing in 2.6-fold decrease in protein binding in this subpopulation. Additionally, there was a 
patient 50% increase in the berotralstat terminal half-life in subjects with moderate or severe 
subgroups hepatic impairment. At the time of NDA submission, the Applicant did not propose 
(intrinsic dose adjustment in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment. However, 
factors) during the review cycle, the FDA raised concern regarding the concentration-
dependent QTc prolongation potential at high berotralstat exposures in patients with 
moderate or severe hepatic impairment. Thereafter, the Applicant proposed a 
reduced starting dose of 110 mg QD in patients with moderate or severe hepatic 
impairment (Child–Pugh Class B or C). See Section 6.3.2 for further details. No dose 
adjustment is recommended in patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh 
Class A) or renal impairment. 
Berotralstat is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. Cyclosporine, a P-gp and BCRP 
inhibitor, increased the Cmax of berotralstat by 25% and the AUCinf by 69%. The 
Applicant did not propose dose adjustment for this drug interaction. However, a 
recommendation for dose adjustment (110 mg QD) for berotralstat with concomitant 
Dosing in administration of P-gp and/or BCRP inhibitors will be reflected in the product labeling. 
patient Additionally, the Applicant did not address the impact of P-gp inducers on the 
subgroups exposure of berotralstat. The use of P-gp inducers with berotralstat is not 
(extrinsic recommended. See Section 6.3.2 for further details. Berotralstat is a moderate 
factors) inhibitor of CYP2D6, CYP3A4, and CYP2C9 and a weak inhibitor of CYP2C19. It is 
also an inhibitor of P-gp. Appropriate monitoring and dose titration are recommended 
for concomitant medications with sensitive substrates and substrates with a narrow 
therapeutic index that are predominantly metabolized by CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 and 
are sensitive P-gp substrates.

Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics 
6.3.1.1. Pharmacokinetics 
x The PK of berotralstat in patients with HAE is similar to that of healthy subjects. 
x Berotralstat exposure (Cmax and AUC) increases greater than proportionally with dose, 
and steady state is reached within 8 to 12 days. Accumulation is around five-fold at the 
110 and 150 mg doses. 
x The median time to the Cmax (Tmax) of berotralstat when administered with food is 
5 hours (range 1 to 8 hours). No differences in the Cmax and AUC of berotralstat were 
54 
Version date: October 12, 2018 
Reference ID: 4711626 

[PDF PAGE 56]
   
 
  
     
     
    
    
    
     
   
   
   
 
     
       
    
    
     
 
   
  
      
  
  
   
 
    
 
      
 
      
 
      
   
        
NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
observed following administration with a high-fat meal; however, the median Tmax was 
delayed by 3 hours, from 2 hours (fasted) to 5 hours (fed). 
x Plasma protein binding is approximately 99%. After a single dose of radiolabeled 
berotralstat 300 mg, the blood-to-plasma ratio was approximately 0.92. 
x The median elimination half-life of berotralstat was ~93 hours (range 39 to 152 hours). 
x Berotralstat is metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)2D6 and CYP3A4 with low 
turnover in vitro. After a single oral radiolabeled berotralstat 300 mg dose, unchanged 
berotralstat represented 34% of the total plasma radioactivity, with each of the eight 
metabolites accounting for 1.8 to 7.8% of the total radioactivity. 
x	 After a single oral radiolabeled berotralstat 300 mg dose, approximately 9% was 
excreted in urine (3.4% unchanged; range 1.8 to 4.7%) and 79% was excreted in feces. 
x	 Among the factors evaluated in the population PK analyses (bodyweight, age, gender 
and race), only bodyweight was identified as a significant predictor of berotralstat 
clearance and volume of distribution; higher exposure (~15%) was predicted in 40 to 
60 kg adults and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age, compared to 60 to 80 kg adults. 
6.3.1.2. Pharmacodynamics 
Primary and secondary nonclinical PD studies showed that berotralstat is a specific inhibitor of 
human plasma kallikrein activity. Concentration-dependent inhibition of plasma kallikrein, 
measured as a reduction from baseline of specific enzyme activity, was demonstrated after oral 
administration of berotralstat to patients with HAE in the clinical development program. The 
EC50 of kallikrein inhibition measured from a pool of subjects with HAE was 11 ng/mL. A 110 mg 
dose provided a mean Ctau of 4.7-fold the EC50 (Study 203). 
Cardiac Electrophysiology 
The effect of berotralstat was evaluated in thorough QT Study (106), a phase 1, randomized, 
double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, multiple-dose study in healthy subjects. At the 
therapeutic dose of 150 mg QD, berotralstat does not prolong the Fridericia-corrected QT 
(QTcF) interval to a clinically relevant extent. A positive QT prolongation was observed at a dose 
of 450 mg (QD for 14 days) (Table 14). Refer to the QT-IRT review by Dr. Girish Bende for more 
details.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization 
6.3.2.1. General Dosing 
The Applicant’s proposed dose of berotralstat is one 150 mg cap sul e tak en o rally QD  w itho ut 
regard to food. Although the numerically greater treatment effect in the high-dose arm supports 
the recommended dose of 150 mg QD, the totality of the efficacy  and safety data support the 
option for dose reduction to 110 mg QD if GI adverse events (AEs) are observed with the 150 mg 
Q D  d o s e .  R e f e r t o t h e c l i n i c a l  r e v i e w  b y  D r .  S h a z  S i d d i q u i  f o r more details (Section 8). The 
applicant proposed to dose berotralstat without regard to food. Although food had no major 
impact on the berotralstat PK, berotralstat was administered with food in the phase 3 study to 
avoid GI-related AEs. A recommendation to administer berotralstat with food will be reflected in 
the product labeling. 
6.3.2.2. Therapeutic Individualization 
x In patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh B or C, 
respectively), the recommended dose of berotralstat is 110 mg QD. 
x No adjustment of the starting dose of berotralstat is recommended for patients with 
mild hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh A). 
x No adjustment of the starting dose of berotralstat is recommended for patients with 
renal impairment. 
x	 Berotralstat is a substrate for P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein 
(BCRP) and dose adjustment (110 mg QD) is recommended in cases of concomitant 
administration with P-gp and/or BCRP inhibitors. This recommendation will be reflected 
in the product labeling. The use of P-gp inducers with berotralstat is not recommended. 
6.3.2.3. Outstanding Issues 
None. 
Clinical Pharmacology Questions 
Does the Clinical Pharmacology Program Provide Supportive Evidence of Effectiveness? 
The efficacy of berotralstat for the prevention of HAE attacks in patients 12 years of age and 
older with type I or II HAE was demonstrated in Part 1 of the multicenter, randomized, double-
blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group Study 302. Adult and adolescent patients who 
experienced at least two investigator-confirmed attacks within the first 8 weeks of the run-in 
period were randomized at a 1:1:1 ratio (berotralstat 110 mg, berotralstat 150 mg, or placebo 
by oral administration once daily, with food) for the 24-week treatment period (Part 1); 41 
subjects were treated with berotralstat 110 mg QD, 40 subjects with berotralstat 150 mg QD, 
and 40 subjects were treated with placebo QD. Patients discontinued other prophylactic HAE 
medications prior to entering the study; however, all patients were allowed to use rescue 
medications for treatment of breakthrough HAE attacks. A history of laryngeal angioedema 
attacks was reported in 74% of the patients and 75% reported prior use of long-term 
prophylaxis. The median attack rate during the prospective run-in period (baseline attack rate)

Is the Proposed Dosing Regimen Appropriate for the General Patient Population for Which 
the Indication is Being Sought? 
Yes, the proposed dosing regimen is reasonable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The 
basis for the dose selection and findings from phase 2 Study 203 is discussed below. 
Study 203 was a phase 2, randomized, double-blind, placebo-cont rolled, four-part, parallel-
group, dose-response study that evaluated the safety, tolerabil i t y , P K ,  P D ,  a n d  e f f i c a c y  o f 
berotralstat in subjects with HAE over a 28-day dosing period; the doses evaluated were 55 mg 
QD to 300 mg QD. The primary efficacy endpoint was the number o f confirmed HAE attacks. A 
statistical comparison of confirmed attacks per week is shown i n Table 15. Treatment with

Although there was no apparent correlation between the confirmed attack rate and berotralstat 
plasma exposure over the dose range studied (55 to 300 mg), the steady-state berotralstat area 
under the curve over the dosing interval (AUC tau) and maximum plasma concentration over the 
dosing interval (Ctau) were correlated with the confirmed attack rate at the lower d ose range of 
55 to 110 mg. Refer to the pharmacometrics review by Dr. Jing N iu (Section 16.3.2) for further 
details. In addition, the results suggested that maintaining a threshold concentration of at least 
four-fold the EC50 for plasma kallikrein inhibition will result in a clinically meaningful reduction in 
attack rate. The proportions of subjects with an average berotr alstat plasma C tau greater than 
four-fold the EC50 were 0/7, 8/14, 14/14, and 16/16 in the 55, 110, 218, and 300 mg dose groups, 
respectively. A 110 mg QD dose provided a mean C tau of 4.7-fold the EC 50, with a corresponding 
reduction in attack rate of 70% (p<0.001) versus placebo, and a 55 mg QD dose provided a mean 
Ctau of 1.5-fold the EC 50, with no change in attack rate versus placebo (p=0.811). Hence , 110 mg 
QD berotralstat was selected for confirmation of efficacy in the phase 3 study. The higher doses 
(218 mg and 300 mg QD) were not evaluated in the phase 3 study because of the GI AEs observed 
in Study 203. The Applicant noted that berotralstat has nonline ar PK, and a two-fold increase in 
steady-state exposure was observed at 150 mg as compared to 110  mg. Therefore, to evaluate 
the dose-response between 110 mg and 218 mg, 150 mg QD was selected as the second dose in 
the phase 3 study. Note that berotralstat has a long half-life of ~93 hours and as such a QD dosing 
regimen was considered reasonable. Other dosing regimens were n ot evaluated in the clinical 
development plan. 
Is an Alternative Dosing Regimen or Management Strategy Required for Subpopulations 
Based on Intrinsic Patient Factors? 
Yes, in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh B or C), a lower 110 mg 
QD dose of berotralstat is recommended. No dose modification is recommended in patients with 
mild hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh A) or renal impairment. Add itionally, there is no need for 
dose adjustment based on age or bodyweight. See below for further details and the basis of these 
recommendations.

Patients with Hepatic Impairment 
The impact of hepatic impairment on the berotralstat PK was assessed in a single-dose, open-
label study (Study 108) in adult male and female subjects with normal hepatic function and 
varying degrees of hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh A, B, or C). Six subjects in each group 
received a single oral dose of 150 mg berotralstat. 
The PK results are shown in Table 16. Exposure of berotralstat increased in subjects with 
hepatic impairment, but the increase in exposure in subjects with severe hepatic impairment 
was lower than that in those with moderate hepatic impairment. The berotralstat unbound 
fraction was similar in subjects with mild hepatic impairment compared with matched healthy 
subjects with normal hepatic function (1.2), and the median unbound fraction was 
approximately 1.5 and 2.6 in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment, 
respectively. The Applicant noted that there was no physiological basis for moderate hepatic 
disease to have a greater impact on berotralstat disposition than severe disease, and so 
proposed to pool the moderate and severe hepatic impairment groups for comparison with 
healthy matched controls (Table 16). However, the reviewer did not agree with this approach 
because decreased protein binding may impact the pharmacologically active unbound drug in 
patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment.

PK, pharmacokinetics 
Furthermore, based on the pooled PK approach, the Applicant proposed no dose adjustment in 
subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment because higher doses of up to 300 mg QD 
berotralstat were considered to be safe and generally well-tolerated in subjects with HAE 
(Study 203). However, the following issues were noted by the reviewer: 
x	 Although a higher dose of 300 mg QD was evaluated in the phase 2 Study 203 and 
concluded to be generally safe, the study duration was relatively short (28 days) and the 
number of subjects exposed to 300 mg QD was small (N=18), reducing the level of 
support for higher exposure of berotralstat. 
x The berotralstat half-life was increased by ~50% in subjects with moderate or severe 
hepatic impairment. Berotralstat accumulates by around five-fold when dosed to 
steady-state. The accumulation of berotralstat in subjects with moderate or severe 
hepatic impairment is expected to be elevated because of the longer half-life. 
x There was a 1.5- and 2-fold decrease in protein binding in subjects with moderate and 
severe hepatic impairment, respectively, and so the pharmacologically active unbound 
drug concentration would be higher in these patients.

Because berotralstat is proposed for chronic use and associated with a potential QTc risk, an 
information request (IR) was sent to the Applicant (dated June 26, 2020) to consider alternate 
dosing recommendations in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. In response 
to the IR, the applicant simulated the following dosing regimens in subjects with HAE using 
population PK Model 258 (BCX7353-PPK-01-A2): 
x Alternate daily dose of 110 mg QD 
x Alternate dosing regimen 150 mg every other day 
Based on the simulation results and patient compliance considerations, the Applicant proposed 
a reduced dose of 110 mg QD berotralstat in subjects with moderate or severe hepatic 
impairment. This proposed dosing recommendation for subjects with moderate or severe 
hepatic impairment is acceptable. 
Patients with Renal Impairment 
The impact of renal impairment on the berotralstat PK was assessed in a single-dose, open-label 
study (Study 107) in adult male and female subjects with severe renal impairment and matched 
healthy subjects with normal renal function. Seven subjects in the following two groups 
received a single oral dose of 200 mg berotralstat. 
x Group A: normal renal function (creatinine clearance [CLCR΁ шϵϬ mL/min)
	
x Group B: severe renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR]
	
<30 mL/min/1.73 m2; not yet on dialysis) 
Compared to matched healthy subjects, the berotralstat Cmax was approximately 39% higher in 
subjects with severe renal impairment following single-dose administration of 200 mg 
berotralstat; however, the AUC was similar in the two groups. Note that healthy subjects and 
those with severe renal impairment were enrolled using different criteria—CLCR and eGFR, 
respectively. During the NDA review, the Applicant was informed (IR of June 30, 2020) that only 
one criterion; i.e., CLCR or eGFR, should be used for comparison of PK between the two groups. 
The Applicant reanalyzed the study data using CLCR as the criterion. For classification of subjects 
by CLCR, the applicant used the CLCR value, either at screening or baseline, that the respective 
(b) (6)
site had used for classification by MDRD during the study. One subject  in the severe 
renal impairment group had a screening and baseline CLCR of 38 mL/min and was excluded from 
the severe renal impairment group (CLCR <30 L/min). The updated statistical comparison of the 
berotralstat PK between subjects with normal and severe renal impairment using the CLCR 
criterion was provided by the Applicant on July 10, 2020 (Table 17). The Cmax and AUC were 
increased by approximately 47% and 13%, respectively, in subjects with severe renal 
impairment compared to healthy subjects using the CLCR criterion. The half-life and protein 
binding were similar in subjects with severe renal impairment and healthy subjects.

The clinical development program of berotralstat allowed enrollment of HAE patients with 
moderate or mild renal impairment in the clinical trials. Using CLCR to estimate renal function, 
25 of 223 subjects (11.2%) enrolled across the two long-term prophylactic studies (204 and 302) 
who received berotralstat 150 mg QD had mild (60 to <90 mL/min CLCR, n=23) or moderate (30 
to <60 mL/min CLCR, n=2) renal impairment at baseline. In these 25 subjects, the safety profile 
of berotralstat 150 mg QD was generally similar to that of the overall study population or those 
with normal renal function in both studies. 
Overall, considering the above factors, the increased Cmax in subjects with severe renal 
impairment is not expected to cause clinically meaningful QTc prolongation, and no dose 
modification is recommended for patients with severe renal impairment. The PK of berotralstat 
in patients with ESRD requiring hemodialysis has not been determined. 
Covariates (Age, Body Weight) 
Based on population PK analyses of pediatric patients 12 to <18 years of age (simulated 
bodyweight generally 40 to 71 kg), slightly higher exposure (~15%) of berotralstat was 
predicted compared to adults weighing 60 to 80 kg. The higher exposure in this lower 
bodyweight group is not expected to cause meaningful QT prolongation, which is a major safety 
concern correlated with exposure. Therefore, no dose modification is recommended for 
adolescents and adults weighing 40 to 60 kg. See the pharmacometrics review (Section 16.3.2) 
by Jing Niu for further details. 
Are There Clinically Relevant Food–Drug Or Drug–Drug Interactions, and What is the 
Appropriate Management Strategy? 
Effect of Food 
The effect of food on berotralstat exposure was evaluated in two phase 1 studies. Study 
BCX7353-101 was a pilot study in which six subjects received two single doses of berotralstat 
218 mg (250 mg [SN]), in the fasted state and after a high-fat meal. In this study, the API in 
HPMC capsules (an earlier formulation) was used. Subsequently, a second relative 
bioavailability and food-effect study (103; three-period, crossover) evaluated berotralstat

The relative effect of a high-fat meal on the PK of berotralstat is shown in Table 18. In the pilot 
food effect Study 101, berotralstat exposure (AUC) under fed conditions was increased 1.4-fold; 
however, the 90% CIs around the point estimates were large, likely as a result of the small 
number of subjects. In Study 103, although the median Tmax was delayed about 3 hours, food 
did not impact the rate or extent of berotralstat exposure. As berotralstat progressed into the 
phase 3 program, the Applicant decided to administer berotralstat with food because there was 
no effect of food on PK and it was postulated that food may minimize GI AEs of berotralstat. 
Considering this, it is recommended that berotralstat be administered with food in patients 
with HAE. 
Later in clinical development, a change in the phase 3 formulation was made and a higher 
strength capsule (e.g., one 110/150 mg capsule instead of two 55/75 mg capsules) was 
developed as the to-be-marketed (TBM) drug product. The phase 3 formulation and TBM 
formulation are of identical composition (except for the difference in capsule fill weight) and 
were bridged in Study 113. The TBM drug product was bioequivalent to the phase 3 
formulation for the 110 and 150 mg doses in terms of the PK parameters of interest: AUC0-72, 
AUC0-inf, and Cmax. Although a definitive food effect study for the commercial formulation of 
berotralstat was not conducted, the food effect results from Study 103 are considered 
applicable to the TBM drug product because the phase 3 clinical study formulation is of 
composition identical to the TBM formulation and Study 103 evaluated the food effect at a 
dose of 300 mg.

Drug Interactions 
Effects of Coadministered Drugs on the Pharmacokinetics of Berotralstat 
1. Cytochrome P450-Mediated Interactions 
Using in vitro studies of recombinant cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes, the Applicant 
demonstrated that the greatest decrease in ďĞƌŽƚƌĂůƐƚĂƚ ĐŽŶĐĞŶƚƌĂƚŝŽŶ ;Ϯ͘ϳʅDͿ occurred with 
CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 (15.0% and 22.4% decrease, respectively, with ~10% decreases in 
incubations without cofactors). Given the low level of in vitro metabolism, no single enzyme 
contributed more than 25% of berotralstat elimination; therefore, no clinical studies were 
conducted with strong index inhibitors of CYP3A4 and CYP2D6. Similar to the in vitro studies, 
the mass balance Study 104 showed the presence of multiple metabolites at low levels, none of 
which exceeded 8% of drug-related radioactivity. 
2.		 Transporter Interaction Studies 
The in vitro studies showed that berotralstat was a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, but not of 
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, or MATE2-K. 
Study 105 was conducted to assess the drug interaction potential related to transporters. The 
probe inhibitor, cyclosporine (inhibits P-gp, OATP, and BCRP) was used to evaluate a possible 
interaction with berotralstat. As shown in Table 19, cyclosporine increased the exposure of a 
single dose of berotralstat (Cmax by 25% and area under the curve from time zero to infinity by 
69%). The Applicant proposed no dose adjustment with concomitant administration of P-gp and 
BCRP inhibitors. However, the following issues were noted by the reviewer: 
x	 Although a higher dose of 300 mg QD was evaluated in phase 2 Study 203 and 
concluded to be generally safe, the study duration was relatively short (28 days), and 
the number of subjects exposed to 300 mg QD was small (N=18), reducing the level of 
support for higher exposure of berotralstat. 
x	 Limited information from long-term studies is available on the safety of berotralstat 
with P-gp and BCRP inhibitors. According to the information submitted by the Applicant, 
22 of 223 subjects (9.9%) enrolled across the two long-term prophylactic studies (204 
and 302) took concomitant P-gp inhibitors while receiving berotralstat 150 mg QD. In 
seven subjects, the P-gp inhibitor was a short-term antibiotic (azithromycin, 
clarithromycin) or antifungal (fluconazole). Thirteen subjects initiated a concomitant P-
gp inhibitor (atorvastatin, simvastatin, telmisartan, carvedilol, or diltiazem) 
administered chronically with berotralstat 150 mg QD. While no AEs attributable to 
concomitant dosing of a P-gp inhibitor were reported, the relevance of these data is 
unclear because the selectivity/sensitivity of these P-gp inhibitors is unknown and may 
not represent the worst-case scenario. Additionally, this interaction assessment was 
conducted post hoc. Typically, such analyses should be prespecified to avoid effects of 
confounding factors on detecting a specific clinically meaningful change in drug 
exposure. 
x Note that cyclosporine inhibits P-gp as well as BCRP. Further studies using more 
selective inhibitors were not conducted by the Applicant to elucidate the relative 
contribution of each transporter.

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Overall, dose adjustment of berotralstat to 110 mg QD with concomitant administration of P-gp 
and/or BCRP inhibitors is recommended to be included in the prescribing information. 
P-gp inducers (e.g., rifampin, St. John’s wort) may decrease berotralstat plasma concentration, 
leading to reduced efficacy of berotralstat. The Applicant’s clinical development program did 
not address the potential impact of P-gp inducers on the systemic exposure of berotralstat. The 
use of berotralstat with P-gp inducers is not recommended.

The clinical drug interaction potential for berotralstat was evaluated in the following studies: 
x Study 112: Phase 1 drug–drug interaction (DDI) study to evaluate the effect of 
berotralstat on the PK of danazol (CYP3A4), amlodipine (CYP3A4), and desipramine 
(CYP2D6) in healthy subjects.

P-gp substrate (digoxin) and BCRP substrate (rosuvastatin).

interaction study (102) was conducted using the 300 mg dose of berotralstat. This study was 
subsequently repeated with the 150 mg dose; only the results for 150 mg berotralstat are listed 
in Table 19.

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Berotralstat increased the exposure of digoxin by ~50% (Table 19). The increase in exposure of 
digoxin is likely clinically meaningful, given its narrow therapeutic range. Therefore, therapeutic 
drug monitoring and dose titration of digoxin is recommended if administered concomitantly 
with berotralstat. 
CYP2D6 is a polymorphic enzyme, and variations of the gene give rise to alleles resulting in no, 
decreased, normal, or increased enzymatic activity. In Studies 102, 112, and 115, subjects with 
no and increased enzymatic activity, known as poor and ultrarapid metabolizers, respectively, 
were excluded, but subjects with normal and decreased enzymatic activity; i.e., normal and 
intermediate metabolizers, respectively, were eligible for enrollment. The increase in exposure 
of the CYP2D6 substrates, desipramine and dextromethorphan, caused by coadministration 
with berotralstat is not expected to cause variability in exposure to CYP2D6 substrates greater 
than the inherent intersubject variability between poor metabolizers, who have no CYP2D6 
enzymatic activity, and normal or ultrarapid metabolizers. 
Berotralstat is a moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 sensitive substrates; therefore, 
appropriate monitoring and dose titration is recommended in cases of concomitant 
administration of sensitive substrates or those with a narrow therapeutic index that are 
predominantly metabolized by CYP2D6 and CYP3A4. 
What is the Bioanalytical Method Utilized to Quantitate Berotralstat? 
Berotralstat (BCX7353) plasma levels were determined by a validated liquid chromatography 
tandem-mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) assay. The method employs a least-squares linear 
regression analysis with a 1/x2 weighting factor to generate a 1 to 1000 ng/mL range. Protein 
precipitation is performed on a 50 ʅ> ĂůŝƋƵŽƚ ŽĨ ƉůĂƐŵĂ ƵƐŝŶŐ ĂĐŝĚŝĨŝĞĚ ĂĐĞƚŽŶŝƚƌŝůĞ͘ The samples 
are next centrifuged and the supernatants are injected into a high-performance LC-MS/MS 
system. Plasma concentrations are back-calculated from the berotralstat peak area ratios 
compared with those of the calibration standards. The intra-assay precision of the method 
ranged from 2.9% to 7.3% (8.6% at the lower limit of quantification [LLOQ]), and intra-assay 
accuracy (bias) ranged from 88.9% to 110.7% (83.5% at LLOQ). Interassay precision ranged from 
4.0% to 8.5% (12.1% at LLOQ), and interassay accuracy from 92.0% to 102.7% (96.1% at LLOQ). 
Berotralstat in human plasma was found to be stable after four freeze/thaw cycles (о20°C and

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Review Strategy 
Support for the efficacy and safety of berotralstat in the proposed indication is primarily based 
on a single, 24-week multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group 
trial in 120 adult and adolescent patients with type I or II HAE, Study BCX7353-302. The efficacy 
results from Study BCX7353-302 are presented in Section 8.1.1. The FDA biostatistician, Mingyu 
Xi, Ph.D. confirmed the Applicant’s efficacy analyses and generated tables and figures for this 
review. For the evaluation of safety, FDA medical officer Shaz Siddiqi, M.D. analyzed data from 
Study BCX7353-302 Parts 1 and 2, as well as from the open-label extension (OLE) of Phase 2 
study BCX7353-204 using CoreFit Data Analysis, JMP, JMP Clinical, JReview, MAED, Office of 
Computational Science (OCS) Analysis Studio, and the Demographic Tool in the OCS Analysis 
Toolbox. The safety results in Section 8.3 are from the analysis by the medical officer reviewer. 
For brevity, trials/studies may also be referred to in this review by the three-digit study number 
302 or 204. 
The intent-to-treat (ITT) population included all randomized subjects, irrespective of whether 
they received the study drug. This was the primary population used for the efficacy analyses as 
well as for reporting of demographic information and baseline disease characteristics. Data 
were analyzed according to randomized treatment. 
The safety population included all subjects who received at least one dose of study drug. This 
population was used in the assessment and reporting of safety data. 
8. Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation 
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy 
Study BCX7353-302 Protocol 
Trial Design 
Study BCX7353-302 (Figure 5) is a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 
parallel-group, three-part study in adolescent and adult subjects 12 years of age and older with 
type I or II HAE. The schedule of assessments is shown in Table 22. The trial was multinational; 
most centers were in the United States with some in Europe and Canada. The primary objective 
of Part 1 of the study was to determine the safety and efficacy of prophylactic berotralstat 110 
or 150 mg administered orally once daily for 24 weeks compared to placebo in subjects with 
HAE. The primary efficacy analysis (the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing 
in the entire 24-week treatment period) was performed after the last subject completed Part 1. 
The primary objective of parts 2 and 3 of the study was to assess long term safety with chronic 
use of berotralstat. 
To be eligible for randomization for Part 1, subjects had to experience two HAE attacks meeting 
the requirements outlined in the inclusion criteria. These two attacks needed to occur in the 14 
72

ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
to 56 day-long window starting at the date of the screening visit.  The baseline attack rate 
included all qualifying attacks during the run-in period and up to the date of first dose.4 Parts 1, 
2, and 3 were conducted in sequence, with Parts 2 and 3 conducted as continuous rollovers 
from Parts 1 and 2, respectively. 
Part 1 (24-Week Blinded Evaluation of the Efficacy and Safety of Berotralstat) 
The population of the single pivotal study comprised adolescent (12 to 17 years of age) and 
ĂĚƵůƚ ƐƵďũĞĐƚƐ ;шϭϴ years of age). Eligible subjects were equally randomized to one of the 
following three treatment groups: 
x Group 1: Berotralstat 110 mg administered orally once daily for 24 weeks 
x Group 2: Berotralstat 150 mg administered orally once daily for 24 weeks 
x Group 3: Placebo administered orally once daily for 24 weeks

8, 12, 18, and 24. The primary efficacy analysis occurred after the last subject completed their 
week-24 visit and included all data through week 24. 
Part 2 (24-Week Blinded Evaluation of the Safety of Berotralstat) 
Part 2 of the study began with administration of the study drug at the week-24 visit. Subjects in 
Groups 1 and 2 continued to receive the same berotralstat dose to which they were 
randomized in Part 1 of the study in a blinded manner. Subjects originally randomized to 
placebo in Group 3 underwent a second randomization to receive either 110 or 150 mg (1:1) in 
a blinded manner beginning at the week-24 visit through week 48. Study visits during Part 2 
occurred during weeks 26, 28, 32, 36 and 48, with telephone contact at weeks 40 and 44. 
Efficacy and safety assessments continued throughout Part 2, including investigator 
confirmation of HAE. Interim safety analyses were conducted while Part 2 was ongoing. 
Part 3 (48-Week Evaluation of the Safety of Open-Label Berotralstat) 
Part 3 of the study began with the administration of the study drug at the week-48 visit and is 
ongoing. Subjects continued to receive the same berotralstat dose in Part 3 as in Part 2, albeit 
in an open-label manner. Study visits during Part 3 were scheduled to occur during weeks 60, 
72, 84, and 96, with telephone contact at weeks 52, 56, 64, 68, 76, 80, 88, and 92. A final study 
follow-up visit was scheduled approximately 3 weeks following the last administration of the 
study drug (week 99). Investigator confirmation of attacks was not required in Part 3.

Trial Population 
The study population comprised adults (18 years or older) and adolescents (12 to 17 years of 
age) with a confirmed diagnosis of type I or II HAE and a minimum baseline attack rate of two 
investigator-confirmed attacks during the run-in period of 14 to 56 days from the screening 
visit. 
Key Inclusion Criteria 
1.		 Males and nonpregnant, nonlĂĐƚĂƚŝŶŐ ĨĞŵĂůĞƐ шϭϮ years of age. 
2.		 BodyǁĞŝŐŚƚ ŽĨ шϰϬ kg. 
3.		 A confirmed diagnosis of type I or II HAE based on : 
A clinical diagnosis of HAE Type I or Type II, defined as having a C1-INH functional 
level below 50% and a complement 4 (C4) level below the lower limit of the normal 
(LLN) reference range, as assessed during the Screening period. In the absence of a low C4 
value drawn during the intercritical period (ie, subject is not having an HAE attack), one of 
the following was acceptable to confirm the diagnosis of HAE: a SERPING-1 gene mutation 
known or likely to be associated with HAE type I or II assessed during the screening period; 
a confirmed family history of C1-INH deficiency; a C4 redrawn and retested during an attack 
in the screening period with the results below the LLN reference range. For subjects with

ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
4.		 The subject must have had at least two HAE attacks that met all of the requirements below 
during the run-in period of a maximum of 56 days from the screening visit: 
a.		 The attacks were unique, defined as an attack that began at least 48 hours after the end 
of a previous attack. 
b.		 The attacks must have been treated, required medical attention, or been documented 
to cause functional impairment based on information in the e-diary. 
c.		 The attacks included symptoms of swelling. Symptoms of swelling, in addition to visible 
swelling, may include symptoms in the oropharyngeal or abdominal region indicative of 
internal swelling. 
d.		 The attacks were confirmed by the investigator to be HAE attacks. Subjects were 
contacted within 2 business days of the attack to discuss the attack and any queries on 
the data entered in the e-diary, as applicable. 
Key Exclusion Criteria 
1.		 Use of C1-INH for prophylaxis of HAE attacks within the 14 days prior to the screening visit 
or initiation during the study. Use of a C1-INH for treatment of acute attacks was not 
excluded at any time, nor was C1-INH for preprocedural prophylaxis for an 
unplanned/unforeseen procedure. 
2.		 Use of androgens or tranexamic acid for prophylaxis of HAE attacks within the 28 days prior 
to the screening visit or initiation during the study. 
3.		 Use of an angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor within 7 days of the baseline visit or 
planned initiation during the study. 
4.		 Any significant cardiovascular disease or sudden cardiac death. 
5.		 Laboratory abnormalities: Creatinine clearance ч30 mL/min and aspartate aminotransferase 
(AST) or ALT level greater than three-fold the ULN. 
6.		 Prior enrollment in a berotralstat study. 
7.		 Pregnant, planning to become pregnant during the study, or nursing. 
Key Subject Discontinuation Criteria 
A subject was discontinued from the study drug for any of the following reasons, which was 
recorded in the source documents and the electronic case report form: 
1.		 Emergence of any laboratory abnormality or AE that in the judgment of the investigator 
compromised the ability of the subject to continue study-specific procedures or altered the 
subject's benefit-risk profile such that it was considered not to be in the subject’s best 
interest to continue. 
2.		 Confirmed ALT or AST elevation as follows: greater than eight-fold the ULN; greater than 
five-fold the ULN for >2 weeks; greater than three-fold the ULN and a total bilirubin level 
greater than two-fold the ULN; and greater than three-fold the ULN if accompanied by 
fatigue, nausea, vomiting, right upper quadrant pain and tenderness, fever, rash and/or 
eosinophilia (>5%). 
3.		 Subject noncompliance with the study drug or the study protocol. 
4.		 A QTcF of >500 ms (confirmed on repeat ECG testing).
