regularly scheduled visits and procedures through week 24. 
Reviewer comments: The Applicant’s target population and overall study design was consistent 
with HAE programs for other approved products for prophylaxis. A similar patient population, 
primary endpoint, and treatment period to assess efficacy (26 weeks) were used in the recent 
lanadelumab program. 
Although HAE is a rare disease, the development program for HAE prophylaxis is established 
and the primary clinical endpoint of HAE attacks is a meaningful, established endpoint. The 
randomized, controlled treatment period of 26 weeks is acceptable for this program.  Most sites 
in the pivotal trial were located in the United States; therefore, the data should be reflective of 
the U.S. population of patients with HAE. 
The inclusion, exclusion, and subject discontinuation criteria were also acceptable and 
consistent with prior studies of approved HAE products. Long-term prophylaxis of HAE is an 
individualized decision made between the patient and physician taking into account disease 
activity, frequency of attacks, quality of life, health-care resources, and ability to achieve 
adequate control with on-demand therapy. However, based on the minimum attack frequency, 
the eligibility criteria captured a target population likely to require prophylactic therapy. 
Study Treatments and Blinding 
x Berotralstat 110 mg orally once daily 
x Berotralstat 150 mg orally once daily 
x Placebo orally once daily 
The study drug was blinded to both the subjects and study staff. With the exception of the 
week-2 and week-26 visits, subjects were instructed to bring all drug kits (including unused and 
used bottles) and e-diaries with them to each study visit. Compliance and adherence were 
reviewed at these visits. During study drug administration, subjects were to record in the e-
diary the times of day the study drug was taken on a daily basis. Real-time access to e-diary 
entries for subjects was available via a website, and the investigator (or designee) proactively 
assessed compliance. 
Concomitant Medications 
Allowed 
Therapies for coexisting conditions, treatment for acute HAE attacks (including C1-INH for 
acute-attack therapy, but not for long-term prophylaxis), treatments for short-term HAE 
prophylaxis, and therapies for any AEs.

Prohibited 
Long-term prophylaxis for HAE (e.g., C1-INH, androgens, antifibrinolytics), angiotensin-

Study Endpoints 
Primary Efficacy Endpoint 
The primary endpoint for Part 1 is the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing 
in the entire 24-week treatment period, defined as day 1 to the time of the first dose of study 
drug in Part 2 (day 1 to day 168). 
Details of acute attacks of angioedema were recorded in an electronic diary (e-diary) beginning
	
at screening and continuing through week 48. Subjects were required to complete the HAE 
attack e-diary daily, noting whether or not symptoms of an HAE attack were experienced in the
	
previous 24 hours. Subjects were required to complete the e-diary irrespective of the presence 
of HAE symptoms. If a subject did report an attack, additional details about the attack were 
required to be entered into the e-diary, including the start and stop times, symptoms,
	
anatomical location of swelling (if applicable), severity, treatment(s) administered and the 
times of administration, and whether additional medical care was sought for the attack. Attack
	
severity was assessed by the subject as negligible, mild, moderate, or severe. Attacks were 
treated in accordance with the subject’s normal standard of care. Medications for angioedema
	
attacks were not provided by the Applicant.
	
For all attacks that occurred from screening through the end of Part 1, the investigator (or 
designee) reviewed the e-diary record of the attack. Subjects were contacted within
	
approximately 2 business days of the end of the attack to discuss its clinical characteristics, any 
questions the investigator had on the entered data or to gain additional information not
	
included in the e-diary that the investigator deemed important for clinical evaluation of the 
event. This information, in conjunction with the e-diary record, was used by the investigator to 
verify or reject the event recorded in the e-diary as an HAE attack. Investigators used their 
judgment to confirm or reject a reported event as an HAE attack. All investigator-confirmed
	
attacks of HAE must have included symptoms of swelling irrespective of treatment. In addition
	
to visible swelling, symptoms in the oropharyngeal or abdominal regions indicative of internal
	
swelling were also included. Investigator-confirmed attacks were entered into the AE database
	
as mild, moderate, or severe. Safety and tolerability were evaluated by assessments of AEs, 
laboratory parameters (clinical chemistry, hematology, and urinalysis), vital signs, ECGs, and
	
physical examinations. 
Secondary Efficacy Endpoint
	
Three secondary efficacy endpoints were specified in the Statistical Analysis Plan:
	
x Change from baseline in the Angioedema Quality of Life Questionnaire (AE-QoL) total 
score at week 24. 
x Number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms through 24 weeks.

Quality-of-life data were obtained using the Angioedema Quality of Life Questionnaire (AE-QoL) 
and Treatment Satisfaction Questionnaire for Medications (TSQM)5 at baseline and 
subsequently at weeks 4, 8, 12, 18, and 24 (Table 22). 
The AE-QoL is an AE-ƐƉĞĐŝĮĐ͕ ƋƵĂůŝƚǇ-of-life questionnaire used in the clinical setting and 
captured in some prior HAE clinical trials. The AE-YŽ> ĐŽŶƐŝƐƚƐ ŽĨ ϭϳ ƋƵĞƐƚŝŽŶƐ ǁŝƚŚ ĮǀĞ ĂŶƐǁĞƌƐ 
each and has a recall period of 4 weeks. A total score can be computed over all questions, as in 
Study 302 Part 1, but it is also possible to apply the AE-YŽ> ĂƐ Ă ƉƌŽĮůĞ ŝŶƐƚƌƵŵĞŶƚ ďǇ ŐƌŽƵƉŝŶŐ 
the questions into four domains (Functioning, Fatigue/Mood, Fears/Shame, and Nutrition) and 
calculating the individual domain scores. These are transformed to a linear 0 to 100 scale, with 
higher scores indicative of greater QoL impairment (0 best, 100 worst). Therefore, based on the 
scoring algorithm a decrease in score should correspond to improvement in QoL (Section 16.4). 
The 14-item TSQM is used in clinical practice to assess patients' satisfaction with medication. It 
comprises scores on four scales: side effects, effectiveness, convenience, and global 
satisfaction. 
Exploratory Efficacy Endpoints 
Three exploratory efficacy endpoints were specified in the Statistical Analysis Plan: 
x Number and proportion of subjects with no attacks over 24 weeks. 
x Use of HAE attack medications over 24 weeks. 
x The proportion of responders to study drug, defined as at least a 50% relative reduction 
in the rate of investigator-confirmed attacks. 
Reviewer comments: A reduction in the frequency of acute HAE attacks is a clinically meaningful 
outcome for HAE patients and therefore acceptable as a primary endpoint for an HAE clinical 
trial intended to demonstrate efficacy for a product indicated for prophylactic treatment. 
Investigator-confirmed attacks (rather than patient-reported attacks) have been used as the 
primary efficacy endpoint in other HAE programs to ensure that only swelling attacks and not 
prodromal symptoms or pre-emptive rescue medication use are counted as a distinct HAE 
attack. Secondary endpoints primarily assessed outcomes related to HAE attack reduction. 
Endpoints derived from the AE-QoL instrument are not considered well-defined and reliable 
assessments of HAE-related quality of life for use by the clinical division in HAE prophylaxis trials 
or fit for purpose for labeling. While its use in recurrent angioedema patients has been 
described in the literature, this includes patients with recurrent histamine or bradykinin-
mediated angioedema. Furthermore, the recall period is relativ ely long at 4 weeks, and the tool 
contains domains related to nutrition and mood that may not be specific to HAE. Within person

Safety Assessments 
Safety was evaluated by assessments of AEs, laboratory parameters (clinical chemistry, 
hematology, urinalysis, coagulation, drug screen, and additional tests), vital signs, ECGs, and 
physical examinations according to the schedule in Table 22. Furthermore, should a pregnant 
woman have received blinded study drug, the outcome of the pregnancy was to be followed. 
Statistical Analysis Plan 
Statistical Methods 
The efficacy analyses were conducted on the ITT population, defined as all randomized 
subjects, regardless of whether study drug was administered. The primary endpoint was the 
rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing in the entire treatment period of Part 
1, expressed as attacks per month (1 month was defined as 28 days). Comparisons of the rate 
of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks between each berotralstat dose and placebo during the 
entire dosing period for Part 1 was conducted using a Poisson regression model. The number of 
investigator-confirmed attacks was included as the dependent variable, the treatment was 
included as a fixed effect, the baseline attack rate was included as a covariate, and the 
logarithm of duration on treatment was included as an offset variable. The estimated monthly 
rate of attacks for each treatment group and the treatment differences expressed as attack rate 
ratios (berotralstat over placebo rate ratio) and their associated 95% confidence intervals (CIs) 
were obtained from the Poisson regression model. 
Analyses of Secondary Endpoints for Part 1 
The AE-QoL total score change from baseline was analyzed using a mixed model for repeated 
measures with fixed effects for treatment, baseline attack rate, baseline AE-QoL, visit, a visit-
by-treatment interaction, and a random effect for subject. The estimated treatment difference 
for each berotralstat dose versus placebo at each visit, the 95% CI, and the associated p-value 
were calculated. Primary inferences were drawn from treatment differences for the changes 
from baseline estimated from the mixed model for repeated measures model at week 24. 
The number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms through week 24 were 
computed. The proportion of days with angioedema symptoms through week 24 was evaluated 
using an analysis of covariance model with baseline attack rate as a covariate and treatment 
included as a fixed effect. The estimated treatment difference comparing each active treatment 
to placebo, the 95% CI, and the associated p-value were calculated. 
For investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during the effective treatment period, between-
treatment comparisons were performed using a Poisson regression model similar to that used 
for the primary endpoint analysis. 
The Statistical Analysis Plan specified that for all analyses using the Poisson regression model, 
goodness of fit will be evaluated by examining the dispersion parameter. In addition, the

significantly greater than zero, the negative binomial model was to be considered primary. 
Multiplicity Control 
A combination of hierarchical testing and the Hochberg step-up procedure was used to control 
the Type I error rate for testing the primary and secondary endpoints in Part 1. Four endpoints 
were evaluated, for each of which two potential doses were tested against placebo. The four 
endpoints were tested in a hierarchical fashion, and the two doses were tested using the 
Hochberg step-up procedure at each level of the hierarchy to which both doses progress. 
The first endpoint to be tested was the primary endpoint, the rate of investigator-confirmed 
HAE attacks during dosing in the entire 24-week treatment period of Part 1. Using the Hochberg 
step-up procedure, each of the two ĚŽƐĞƐ ǁĂƐ ƚĞƐƚĞĚ Ăƚ ƚŚĞ ɲсϬ͘Ϭϱ ůĞǀĞů͕ ĐŽŵƉĂƌŝŶŐ ĂĐƚŝǀĞ 
treatment to placebo. If the maximum of the two p-values was <0.05, the null hypotheses of no 
difference between the rate of attacks for subjects on active and placebo treatment was 
rejected for both doses and testing proceeded to the next endpoint in the hierarchy with

The first, second, third, and fourth endpoints in the hierarchy are: 
1.		 The rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing in the entire 24-week 
treatment period (day 1 to day 168). 
2.		 Change from baseline in the total AE-QoL score at week 24. 
3.		 Number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms through 24 weeks. 
4.		 Rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing in the effective treatment period 
(beginning on day 8 through 24 weeks). 
The process described above was continued for each endpoint in the hierarchy until either all 
four endpoints had been tested or testing was stopped due to nonrejection of the null 
hypotheses for both doses for endpoints earlier in the hierarchy. At each level of the hierarchy, 
the Hochberg step-up procedure was used to control the Type I error rate if two doses were

Missing Data Sensitivity Analysis 
Missing data sensitivity analyses were conducted for the analysis of the primary endpoint using 
three methods: 
1.		 A missing data sensitivity analysis was conducted in which observed data post-study drug 
discontinuation were included in the attack-rate determination. 
2.		 A missing data sensitivity analysis was conducted in which observed data post-study drug 
discontinuation were used in combination with imputed data for subjects who discontinued 
study drug prior to the end of Part 1 and did not continue to provide attack information. 
3.		 A tipping-point analysis was conducted in which observed data post-study drug 
discontinuation were combined with imputed data for subjects who discontinue study drug 
prior to the end of Part 1 and did not continue to provide attack information by varying 
assumptions for missingness. 
The analyses of safety data for Parts 1, 2, and 3 and of effectiveness data for Parts 2 and 3 were 
primarily descriptive. 
Protocol Amendments 
The Applicant amended the protocol once, making the following key changes: 
Amendment 1 (October 11, 2018) 
x Removed participating study centers in the Asia–Pacific region. 
x Added objectives and endpoints for Study 302 Part 3. 
x Clarified the diagnosis of HAE, which allowed patients with C1-INH function ш50% but 
lower than the lower limit of normal (LLN) to be included in study. 
x Provide updated information on the berotralstat clinical program. 
Study BCX7353-302 Part 1 Results 
Data Quality and Integrity 
The Applicant conducted clinical studies according to procedures that incorporate the ethical 
principles of good clinical practices (GCP). BioCryst also undertook a GCP audit program. Audits 
(b) (4)
were performed by auditors from  who operated independently of the study 
monitors. The audits within the clinical program were aimed at study documentation, 
investigator sites, and clinical study reports. A total of 10 site audits was performed through 
April 10, 2019 and audit certificates were provided. No issues were identified. 
Compliance with GCP 
The Applicant states that the study was conducted in accordance with GCP as described in the 
guidelines of the ICH. The study protocol, amendments, informed consent, and other necessary 
documents were reviewed and approved by an independent ethics committee or institutional 
review board before initiation of the study. Written informed consent and assent, where 
applicable, were obtained from each subject before participating in the study.

Subject Disposition 
Overall, a total of 160 subjects were screened, 121 subjects were randomized (ITT population), 
and 120 of these subjects (99%) were treated (safety population). Most screening failures were 
caused by normal C1-INH and/or C4 results and an insufficient number of baseline HAE attacks 
(b) (6)
during the run-in period. Subject  was randomized to placebo but not treated due to 
withdrawal of consent; this subject was included in the ITT population but not the safety 
population. A total of 108 subjects (89%) completed study drug dosing in Part 1: 37 subjects 
who received berotralstat 110 mg (90%), 37 who received berotralstat 150 mg (93%), and 34 
who received placebo (85%). No subject was lost to follow up. Table 23 shows the subject 
disposition.

Although these major protocol deviations were unlikely to have affected the overall quality or 
interpretation of the study data, two of the four patients continued in the trial despite receiving 
adjunct and prohibited HAE therapies that may have improved their treatment response. In 
view of the small total sample size, there was concern this could be significant. However, an 
efficacy analysis of the ITT population yielded robust results whether these two patients were 
included or excluded from the ITT population. Numerous other minor protocol deviations 
occurred; these were related to the timing of study visits, timing of procedures, and one ECG 
assessment that showed a PR interval longer than recommended for entry. The extended PR 
interval was deemed not to be clinically meaningful by the site principal investigator and 
cardiologist. 
Demographic Characteristics 
The demographic characteristics of the subjects are shown in Table 24, and were generally 
similar across treatment arms. Overall, the study enrolled more females than males. The 
proportion of females was higher in the berotralstat 110 mg group (73%) than in the 
berotralstat 150 mg group (57%). Relatively small proportions of the subjects were in the 
youngest (12 to 17 ǇĞĂƌƐͿ ĂŶĚ ŽůĚĞƐƚ ;шϲϱ years) age groups. The majority of subjects (93% 
across treatment arms) were white. Most subjects (72%) were from North America with over 
60% of that population from the United States. The remainder were from various European 
countries. Also, there was a total of six adolescent patients enrolled, which is relatively few, 
even for this orphan population. However, this may be expected because symptoms tend to 
present for the first time in puberty and patients are typically diagnosed several years after 
initial presentation. 
Reviewer comment: Although HAE affects males and females equally, the literature reports a 
clear female predominance, most likely because women tend to be more symptomatic than men 
as a result of hormonal factors such as puberty, contraception, and pregnancy. Therefore, the 
predominantly female study population does not raise concerns about the generalizability of the 
study but is merely a reflection of the most symptomatic patients, who are most likely to use 
and benefit from prophylactic treatment. All ethnicities and races are affected by types I and II 
HAE; however, most of the extant epidemiologic and genetic data are derived from European

proportion of nonwhite patients in this study , while relatively small, is adequate for such a rare 
disease. The proportion of adolescent subjects in this study is small {N=6}; however , the 
pathophysiology of the disease is unaffected by age.

Other Baseline Chara cteri stics (e.g ., Disease Chara cteri stics, Important Concomitant Drug s) 
The baseline d isease characteris t ics were generally similar across treat ment groups (Table 25). 
The vast m ajor ity of pat ients had HAE diagnosis confirmed by a low C4 and <50% f unctiona l 
level of Cl -i nhib itor (88%). The Appl icant d id not provi de data to categori ze subjects as having 
85 
Version date: October 12, 2018 
Reference ID 4711626 

[PDF PAGE 87]
    
 
 
  
     
     
      
      
   
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
    
       
      
 
  
         
           
        
         
     
        
          
        
NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
type I or II HAE; however, low C4 and low functional C1-INH are pathognomonic for types I and 
II HAE. Type III HAE is exceedingly rare and characterized by normal complement studies. Based 
on prevalence data, the vast majority of subjects (~85%) are expected to have type I disease. 
Further, phenotypically types I and II HAE are indistinguishable and respond similarly to HAE 
treatments. SERPING-1 gene mutations were only evaluated if a patient had a normal C4 level 
and despite a clinical history suggestive of type I or II HAE. The time between screening and first 
dose of study drug was similar between treatment groups. The mean baseline attack rate per 
month was also similar among the treatment groups, with an average of approximately three 
attacks per month. Subjects reported the primary location of previous HAE attacks as a 
combination of abdominal and peripheral attacks. All of the treatment groups had on average 
about two laryngeal attacks per year. The study population reflects a population with 
predominantly moderate-to-severe HAE and a fairly equal disease burden among treatment 
groups in terms of the frequency of attacks and attack location; including laryngeal 
involvement. Patients were most likely to be on prior prophylactic therapy with androgens, 
followed by C1 inhibitor replacement. This was consistent in all three treatment groups. 
Overall, the study population was representative of a population of patients with HAE for which 
long-term prophylaxis with an oral kallikrein inhibitor would be appropriate. 
Reviewer comment: The baseline attack rate was calculated from the start of the run-in period 
to the date of first dose of study drug. Once subjects experienced 2 or more attacks during the 
14 to 56-day run-in period, they were randomized into the study, but may not have received the 
first dose of study drug right away. Some subjects had a longer time interval between the end of 
run-in and first dose of study drug (up to 16 days); given the variability of disease, these subjects 
may not have experienced any further attacks during this time period. Because the baseline 
attack rate was calculated from the run-in period to the first day of study drug, some subjects 
had a baseline attack rate < 1 attack per month; this scenario was most apparent in subjects 
with a long run-in period and lengthier delay between run-in and start of study drug.

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use 
The rate of treatment compliance was high and fairly equal across all treatment groups, with an 
average total mean compliance rate of 98% (Table 26). Subjects were not allowed to take 
concomitant prophylactic HAE medications; however, they were allowed rescue medications 
for an HAE attack that occurred during the study. Rescue medications were not supplied by the 
Applicant.

The tota l number of investigator-confirmed attacks was lower in the 150 mg group than the 
110mg dose, and berotra lstat-treated subjects had fewer tota l attacks than subjects treated 
with placebo. Numerica lly, the proportion of subjects with one or more HAE attacks requiring 
treatment with rescue medication was lowest in the 150 mg group. Notably, however, very few 
subjects in the study achieved comp lete elimination of attacks during the treatment period: 
one (2%), three (8%), and two (5%) subjects in the 110 mg, 150 mg, and placebo groups, 
respective ly. 
Analysis of the HAE attacks during the 24-week treatment period confirmed that the primary 
endpoint was met for both dose leve ls, with the 110 and 150 mg doses reducing the HAE attack 
rate by 30% (p=0 .024) and 44% (p<0.001), respectively, relative to placebo. The negative 
binomia l regression analysis-estimated attack rate per 28 days was 1.65 for berotralstat 110 mg

Note: Statistical analysis was based on a negative binomial regression model. The number of investigator-confirmed attacks was 
included as the dependent variable, the treatment was included as a fixed effect, baseline investigator-confirmed attack rate was 
included as a covariate, and the logarithm of duration on treatment was included as an offset variable. 
The Applicant reported that 10 to 15% of subject-reported HAE attacks were not confirmed by 
the investigator either due to absence of swelling symptoms or investigator judgment. 
However, rejection rates were similar across treatment groups, and the Applicant’s 
supplementary analysis of subject-reported attacks showed that the rate ratios and p-values 
were similar to those from the primary endpoint analysis. 
In an ad hoc analysis of the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks rated as moderate or 
severe by the subject during Part 1, the 110 and 150 mg berotralstat doses reduced the rate of 
moderate and severe HAE attacks by 10% (p=0.608) and 40% (p=0.018), respectively, compared 
with placebo. 
Dose/Dose Response 
Subjects receiving the 150 mg dose had a moderately higher reduction in the HAE attack rate 
compared to those receiving the 110 mg dose. Attack rate ratio between 150 mg and 110 mg  
doses is 0.80 with 95% CI (0.58, 1.10). The dose response is preserved in the evaluation of 
secondary and exploratory endpoints, which also demonstrate numerically better outcomes 
with the 150 mg dose compared to the 110 mg dose. Within the development program, there 
were more treatment discontinuations due to lack of perceived efficacy in the 110 mg group 
compared to the 150 mg group, albeit this was mainly seen in the open label studies. Overall, 
the evidence supports the Applicant’s recommended dose of 150 mg once daily. 
Durability of Response 
Figure 6 presents the mean investigator-confirmed attack rate by month. Berotralstat reduced 
the attack rate in month 1 and the effect was stable over the 6-month duration of Part 1.

Persistence of Effect 
Patients in this study continued the study drug in an open-label fashion in Part 3. Because the 
effect of berotralstat was not evaluated after cessation of study drug, no data are available to 
analyze the persistence of its effect. 
Efficacy Results—Secondary or Exploratory Clinical Outcome Assessment (Patient-Reported 
Outcome) Endpoints 
Change from Baseline in AE-QoL Total Score at Week 24 
Table 29 shows the analysis of the secondary endpoint of AE-QoL score at week 24. The least-
squares mean (LSM) differences from placebo in AE-QoL total score were о2.8 (95% CI о10.1, 
4.5; p=0.453) and о4.9 (95% CI о12.2, 2.4; p=0.188) for the berotralstat 110 and 150 mg groups, 
respectively. The treatment effect of berotralstat compared to placebo was not statistically 
significant for either dose group. Given that the AE-QoL total score did not significantly improve 
with berotralstat treatment, the individual components and domains were not analyzed. 
Because this endpoint failed to reach statistical significance and the Applicant did not seek a 
labeling claim for AE-QoL, the Division of Clinical Outcome Assessments was not consulted to 
evaluate this tool during the NDA review.

Rate of Investigator-Confirmed HAE Attacks During Dosing in the Effective Treatment Period 
Table 31 shows the analysis of the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during dosing in 
the effective treatment period. The rate of investigator-confirmed attacks for the effective 
treatment period is an indication of the efficacy of active treatment after berotralstat had 
reached steady-state concentration. The reduction in attack rate relative to the placebo group 
was 30% (95% CI 4, 49; nominal p=0.026) and 47% (95% CI 26, 62; nominal p<0.001) for the 
berotralstat 110 and 150 mg groups, respectively.

Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trials 
Exploratory Efficacy Analysis 
One of the exploratory efficacy endpoints was the proportion of responders to study drug, 
defined as at least a 50% relative reduction in the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks 
during treatment compared with the baseline attack rate. The analysis of this endpoint is 
presented in Table 32. For this exploratory endpoint, 58% of patients receiving 150 mg 
berotralstat and 51% of those receiving 110 mg berotralstat ŚĂĚ Ă шϱϬй ƌĞĚƵĐƚŝŽŶ ŝŶ ƚŚĞŝƌ , 
attack rate compared to baseline versus 25% of patients receiving placebo. 
The post hoc analyses of the proportion of responders to study drug, defined as at least 70% 
and 90% relative reductions in the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during treatment 
compared with the baseline attack rate, were also considered. The analyses of these endpoints 
are also listed in Table 32. 
In post hoc analyses, 50% and 23% of patients receiving 150 mg berotralstat, and 27% and 10% 
of those receiving 110 mg berotralstat͕ ŚĂĚ Ă шϳϬй Žƌ шϵϬй ƌĞĚƵĐƚŝŽŶ ŝŶ , ĂƚƚĂĐŬ ƌĂƚĞ 
compared to baseline versus 15% and 8% of patients receiving placebo, respectively. While 
beroltrastat demonstrated significant improvements in investigator-confirmed attack rates 
compared to placebo, these responses suggest that very few subjects experienced 
complete/near complete elimination of attacks. These responder analysis results should be 
interpreted with caution because the  reduction of attack rate relative to the baseline rate 
could be potentially highly variable due to the limiteseline observation period.

Subgroup Analysis 
To examine whether the treatment effects vary by baseline demographic factors, such as age, 
gender , race, or region, we conducted subgroup analyses on the primary endpoint for these 
four factors. These subgroup analyses used the same analysis mode l used in the overa ll 
analyses. 
Figure 7 presents the subgroup analysis results of primary endpoint investigator-confirmed HAE 
attack rate comparing the 110 mg group to the placebo group . Figure 8 presents the subgroup 
analysis results comparing the 150 mg group to the placebo group. Subgroup analyses showed 
that the treatment effect was consistent with the overall effect across all categories , with the 
exception of 3 subgroups whose sample sizes were too small to allow adequate interpretation 
(adolescents [n=G], elderly [n=9] , and other race [n=8]). Due to the small number of subjects in 
those subgroups, mode l based analysis could not be comp leted or the results were not reliab le. 
We left fields of these subgroups blank on the graphs and only reported the number of subjects 
in each group. Because HAE is caused by genetic mutations leading to low levels of functiona l 
Cl-I NH, the pathophysiology of the disease is the same irrespective of age. Even though the 
number of adolescents evaluated in the pivota l trial was sma ll, efficacy in ado lescents is 
expected to be similar to that in adu lts and may be extrapo lated from the adult data.

Missing Data Sensitivity Analysis 
The Applicant conducted three missing data sensitivity analyses on the primary endpoint. The 
first missing data sensitivity analysis included observed data post-treatment discontinuation. 
The analysis was supportive of the primary endpoint for both dose levels, with the 110 and 
150 mg doses reducing the HAE attack rate by 31% (95% CI 6, 49; p=0.020) and 42% (95% CI 20, 
58; p<0.001), respectively, versus placebo. The negative binomial analysis-estimated attack rate 
per 28 days was 1.60 for berotralstat 110 mg subjects, 1.34 for berotralstat 150 mg subjects, 
and 2.31 for placebo subjects over the 24-week Part 1 dosing period. 
The second missing data sensitivity analysis included observed data post-treatment 
discontinuation and imputing missing data post-treatment discontinuation. The analysis was 
supportive of the primary endpoint for both dose levels, with the 110 and 150 mg doses 
reducing the HAE attack rate by 31% (95% CI 5, 50; p=0.023) and 35% (95% CI: 9, 53; p=0.011), 
respectively, versus placebo. The negative binomial analysis-estimated attack rate per 28 days 
95 
Version date: October 12, 2018 
Reference ID: 4711626 

[PDF PAGE 97]
   
 
  
   
  
         
       
     
      
  
  
    
 
 
 
  
      
  
    
       
   
    
      
  
      
 
     
  
  
   
     
       
  
    
   
    
  
  
NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
was 1.64 for berotralstat 110 mg subjects, 1.55 for berotralstat 150 mg subjects, and 2.37 for 
placebo subjects over the 24-week Part 1 dosing period. 
The third missing data sensitivity analysis was the tipping point analysis on the primary 
endpoint. The analysis showed that three and two-fold increase in expected attack rates for the 
110 and 150 mg doses (attack rates of 5.0 and 2.6, respectively) would be needed for the 
missing data to alter the result of the analysis. The expected attack rate at week 24 for the 100 
and 150 mg groups was 1.65 and 1.31, respectively. Therefore, it is highly unlikely that the 
conclusion would be altered by the missing data. 
Overall, the missing data sensitivity analyses of both doses of berotralstat were supportive of 
the robustness of the outcomes of the primary analysis. 
Statistical Issues 
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness 
The effectiveness of berotralstat was studied in a single ongoing Study 302 based on its Part 1 
results. The study met its primary endpoint; the 110 and 150 mg doses of berotralstat reducing the 
rate of HAE attacks by 30% (p=0.024) and 44% (p<0.001) relative to placebo, respectively. 
The effectiveness of berotralstat was also evaluated in terms of the secondary endpoints of 
change from baseline in the AE-QoL, number and proportion of days with angioedema 
symptoms, and rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during the effective treatment 
period. Testing of the first secondary endpoint, a patient-reported outcome in the hierarchy, 
failed for both doses, halting formal efficacy evaluation on the remaining secondary endpoints. 
Of note, in an exploratory analysis, the trend favoring berotralstat in the proportion of patients 
with at least a 50% reduction in HAE attacks compared to baseline lends support to the primary 
findings. 
The study achieved clinical and statistical significance on the primary endpoint. Endpoints 
derived from similar data (e.g., investigator confirmed attack rate during the effective 
treatment period, proportion of days with angioedema symptoms in Part 1) also achieved 
nominal clinical and statistical significance. There were no statistically significant secondary 
endpoints after adjusting for multiple testing using the Hochberg procedure. Considering the 
rarity of the disease, the current unmet need and the robustness of the primary endpoint 
analysis, the statistical reviewer concludes that substantial evidence of effectiveness was met. 
Multiplicity Control 
The study hypothesis testing combined hierarchical testing and the Hochberg step-up 
procedure. Testing of primary and secondary endpoints proceeded in hierarchical fashion, and 
two doses at each level were tested using the Hochberg step-up procedure. Table 33 
summarizes the results of the hierarchical testing. Note that there were no statistically 
significant secondary endpoints after adjusting for multiple testing.

Estimand 
The primary analyses were conducted on the ITT population but included only observed on-
treatment data. This is an on-treatment estimand according to the ICH E9 R1 guidance. The 
Applicant also conducted an analysis of all collected data, including the data collected after 
treatment discontinuation. The results based on this treatment-policy estimand were 
consistent with those based on the on-treatment estimand. 
Missing Data and Sensitivity Analysis 
Table 34 presents the number of subjects who completed study treatment in Part 1. One 
subject in the 110 mg group, two subjects in the 150 mg group, and four subjects in the placebo 
group discontinued the study treatment early. Of them, one in the 110 mg group, one in the 
150 mg group, and two in the placebo group were due to lack of efficacy.

The Applicant conducted a missing data sensitivity analysis using three approaches. The first 
approach used all collected data including the data after treatment discontinuation but without 
imputation, the second approach used the same data as in the first approach plus imputation 
for the remaining missing data, and the third approach used all collected data plus imputed 
data based on the tipping point analysis method. All three analyses supported the conclusion 
from the primary analysis. 
The sensitivity analyses of both doses of berotralstat were supportive of the robustness of the 
primary analysis outcomes. 
Overall, the statistical reviewer of this NDA concludes that the efficacy of both doses of 
berotralstat was demonstrated. The statistical reviewer’s recommended regulatory action for 
this application is approval. 
Review of Safety 
Safety Review Approach 
All clinical studies conducted as part of the berotralstat development program were evaluated 
for safety; however, given the short exposure periods and absence of additional safety findings 
in the phase 1 and shorter 2 studies, this safety review focuses on Part 1 of the 24-week 
placebo-controlled phase 3 trial, Study 302. Supportive data for safety were derived from the 
analysis of interim data from Part 2 of Studies 302 and 204, discussed in Section 8.3.4, 
Additional Safety Assessments and Section 8.3.7, Special Safety Studies, respectively. The 
review tools used to conduct independent reviewer analyses were MAED, JMP Clinical, JMP, 
JReview, OCS Analysis Studio, and the Demographic Tool in the OCS Analysis Toolbox.

Adequacy of the Safety Database 
A total of 15 clinical studies have been completed in the berotralstat HAE clinical development 
program and 6 studies are ongoing. As of April 22, 2020, a cumulative total of 1104 subjects 
have been enrolled in berotralstat studies. Among them, 1030 subjects received berotralstat 
and 165 subjects received placebo, with 91 placebo subjects also receiving berotralstat in 
crossover studies. 
Study 302 included 120 adult and adolescent patients with HAE, 81 of whom received at least 
one dose of berotralstat during the controlled treatment period. A total of 227 subjects 
received open-label berotralstat in the OLE Study 204. A total of 16 adolescent patients in the 
two trials were exposed to the study drug. 
Overall, the safety database is of sufficient size and duration for a rare disease such as HAE to 
assess the safety of the proposed dose of berotralstat when taken chronically. Of note, the 
safety database exceeds the Division’s recommendation for 1 year exposure in at least 100 
subjects. Study 302 Part 3 and the OLE Study 204 are ongoing to provide further long-term 
safety data.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments 
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality 
Overall, no data quality issues were identified in the review of this NDA. OSI audits of sites 33 
and 35 from Study 302 did not reveal any issues related to data integrity. As noted earlier in this 
review, our initial review of the ADaM datasets for pivotal Study 302 Part 1, showed that the 
record sequence numbers for the ADVS and ADLB datasets were missing. An IR was sent on 
February 12, 2020 to resubmit the ADVS and ADLB datasets for Study 302 Part 1, Study 204, the 
NDA analysis, and the ISS with the appropriate sequence number identifiers included. A data 
analysis was performed on these new datasets, which were received on February 20, 2020. 
Since the record sequence numbers did not change the numerical and non-numerical data, the 
analysis was identical to the safety reports generated previously, and did not impact the 
integrity of the data. 
Categorization of Adverse Events 
The definitions of AEs and SAEs in the protocols were consistent with 21 CFR 312.32. Reports of 
all AEs and SAEs, regardless of investigator attribution, were collected from the time of signing 
of informed consent to the last study visit. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were 
defined as any AE that increased in severity or that was newly developed at or after the first 
dose of study drug through the final follow-up visit. AEs were coded using the Medical 
Dictionary for Regulatory Activities version 19.1. 
All AEs were assessed (graded) for severity and classified using the DMID criteria for grading AEs 
(published November 2007). Any AEs not covered by the DMID criteria were assessed and 
classified into one of four clearly defined categories as follows: mild (1), moderate (2), severe 
(3), and life-threatening (4). The Applicant’s coding of verbatim terms to preferred terms (PTs) 
was appropriate. The investigator or medically qualified designee also reviewed each AE to 
make a determination of its relationship to the study drug using the following categories: not 
related, unlikely, possibly related, probably related, and definitely related. 
Routine Clinical Tests 
Laboratory tests were performed at time points specified in the schedule of assessments for 
Studies 302. Only clinically significant abnormalities (as determined by the investigator) in 
laboratory values, physical examination findings, or vital signs were reported as AEs. 
Safety 
Controlled study drug exposure is shown in Table 36. Drug exposure was fairly uniform across 
all three treatment groups, with an average mean exposure of 159 days for all subjects. The 
total exposure period for Part 1 of Study 302 was 168 days (24 weeks). There was adequate 
drug exposure during the study to determine the treatment effect and perform a safety 
analysis.

steroid treatment, and persistence of the rash despite stopping the study drug 3 months prior.

agrees with the Applicant's assessment that myeloma was likely already present in early stages 
at start of study entry and therefore un-related to berotralstat exposure. 
Significant Adverse Events 
Ten subjects reported 11 TEAEs of grade 3 or 4 severity (Tab le 40), severa l of which were

events occurred in the placebo group , and on ly one event (back pain) occurred in the 150 mg 
group. Other than abdomina l pain, all severe AEs were single occurrences . No new safety 
signals were identified based on the analysis of severe TEAEs.

Laboratory Findings 
Routine clinical testing included hematology, serum chemistry with LFTs, and urina lysis. The 
results of LFTs are discussed in detail below. Otherwise , there was no significant clinica l 
difference between the berotralstat and placebo grou ps in laboratory parameters , including 
coagulation tests, during the study. 
Liver Function Tests 
Table 43, Table 44, and Table 45 list the maximum ALT, AST and bili rubin elevations, 
respective ly, experienced by subjects within each treatment arm. Of note , pat ients with 
AST/ ALT >3x ULN were excluded from t he study; the refor e, most subjects had normal 
transaminase and bilirubin values at baseline. Few subjects experienced transa minase or 
bilirubin elevation; however , one subject in the 150 mg berotralstat group had marked increase 
in ALT that prompted discontinuatio n of study drug as described ear lier in this review. A single 
subject had a maximum AST elevation >3x ULN, and no subjects had bilirubin levels >2x ULN. 
There were no potential Hy's law cases in th is study or the deve lopment prog ram as a who le.

The App licant asserted that a history of androgen use was a risk factor for elevated liver 
enzyme levels in the phase 2 and 3 studies. Table 46 shows maximum ALT elevation by prior 
androgen use (yes/ no). Although in the 110 mg berotra lstat group , the rate of ALT elevation in 
patients who had and had not used androgens was 2.4% and 4.9%, respective ly, no such effect 
was seen in the 150 mg group. Patients in the placebo group with prior androgen use also 
tended to have a higher incidence of an elevated ALT level compared to those who had never 
taken androgens. Given the sma ll samp le size, it is unclear if the combination of berotralstat 
and prior androgen use had an additive effect or if the risk of increased ALT was present with 
prior androgen use alone. This potentia l relationship was evaluated further in safety 
assessments from Study 204 (Section 8.3.7.1 , Table 57).

ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Additional Safety Assessments from Study 302, Part 2 
In addition to the 6 month placebo-controlled safety data from Part 1, the NDA submission 
included interim safety data from 4 months of Study 302, Part 2. No new safety signals were 
identified during the review of Part 2 interim data. Similar to Part 1, GI adverse reactions such 
as abdominal pain, vomiting and diarrhea were common and occurred more frequently with 
the higher 150 mg dose than the lower 110 mg dose. No significant elevations in transaminases 
or bilirubin were reported. There were 31 study drug discontinuations, mostly occurring due to 
subject withdrawal for reasons unrelated to study drug or perceived lack of efficacy. Four 
subjects in the 150 mg group discontinued due to an adverse event (palpitations/tachycardia, 
abdominal pain, abdominal pain/diarrhea, and anal incontinence). One subject in the 110 mg 
group discontinued due to nausea. There were two SAEs: obesity in the 110 mg group and a 
medical observation in the 150 mg group. The medical observation SAE was due to 
hospitalization for a routine thyroid cancer screening procedure using radiolabeled iodine in a 
subject with a history of treated thyroid papillary carcinoma and hypothyroidism. Neither event 
appeared related to the study drug. No AESIs occurred in Part 2 of Study 302. 
Vital Signs 
Overall, no clinically meaningful changes within or between treatment groups were observed 
for pulse rate, respiratory rate, or mean blood pressure. 
Electrocardiograms 
Among all treatment groups, one subject in the placebo group had a clinically significant 
abnormal ECG finding. This subject developed sinus bradycardia with first degree 
atrioventricular block, which was reported as an AE. The Applicant conducted a thorough QT 
study, which is summarized below. 
QT 
A thorough QT study (Study BCX7353-106) was conducted in healthy volunteers evaluating 
berotralstat doses of 150 mg and 450 mg QD for 14 days and reviewed by the Interdisciplinary 
Review Team (IRT) for Cardiac Safety Studies. Berotralstat caused a dose and concentration-
dependent increase in the QT interval over the range of doses evaluated; however, at the peak 
concentrations with the recommended dose of 150 mg QD, the QT interval was not prolonged 
to any clinically relevant extent at Day 14. At a dose of 450 mg QD (Day 14), exposures were ~3-
fold higher than achieved at the recommended 150 mg dose, and the mean QTc increased 
significantly (refer to the IRT review by Dr. Girish Bende and Clinical Pharmacology review in 
this document for details). 
Reviewer comment: Because the QT prolongation observed with berotralstat is dose and 
concentration dependent and berotralstat exhibits nonlinear PK, administering even twice the 
recommended dose carries a potential risk of clinically significant QTc prolongation. Of note, 
this safety issue has been discussed with the Applicant during previous interactions for the acute

HAE attack development program which proposes to administer higher doses of berotralstat. In 
light of an ongoing development program for acute attacks, Sections 1 (Limitation of Use 
statement), 2 (Dosage and Administration), 5 (Warnings and Precautions) and 12 
(Pharmacodynamics and Pharmacokinetics) of the PI and the PPI will be revised to ensure that 
prescribers are aware of this potential risk and patients do not take higher or additional doses. 
Immunogenicity 
Immunogenicity studies were not required or performed as part of this study. 
Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues 
Rash was designated a priori as an adverse event of special interest. Reports of skin disorders 
were rare and occurred as single events: pruritis, acne, dry skin, alopecia, petechiae, purpura 
and echymosis. One subject treated with 150 mg berotralstat experienced mild petechiae, 
which was assessed to be caused by mechanical irritation. The purpuric rash was discussed 
under treatment discontinuations due to TEAEs, but was not felt to be related to study drug. 
Although gastrointestinal adverse events were common and a few cases of transaminase 
elevation occurred, no hepatobiliary adverse events were reported with berotralstat treatment 
in Part 1 of Study 302. 
Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups 
The OCS Analysis Toolbox DM Tool and OCS Analysis studio risk assessment tools were used to 
analyze safety in Study 302 by the following demographic subgroups: sex, age, race, ethnicity, 
and region. This review did not identify a meaningful difference in overall TEAEs between 
berotralstat and placebo within any subgroup. However, the relatively low frequency of other 
SAEs and adverse dropouts in addition to the small number of subjects within certain subgroups 
made it difficult to draw any further conclusions regarding an imbalance in safety based on 
demographic or disease characteristics. Study 302 only enrolled six adolescent subjects. 
Because the Applicant is seeking an indication for patients 12 years of age and older, safety 
signals in the 12 to 17-year age group were analyzed in more detail (Table 47).

The majority of adolescent subjects were white similar to the general safety population. 
Although small in number, there was adequate representation across the age spectrum with 
half of the subjects being 12 years of age. There were no SAEs or TEAEs leading to treatment 
discontinuation. Infections and GI disorders were the most common TEAEs, all of which were 
grade 1 or 2 severity. The types of infection were typical for this age group. Despite the small 
numbers, there was no apparent difference in the safety profile of berotralstat in adolescent 
subjects compared to adults that would raise concern or alter the benefit-risk assessment in 
this age group. 
Specific Safety Studies: BCX7353-204 
Trial Design 
This is an ongoing two-arm open-label phase 2 study designed to evaluate the long-term safety 
of prophylactic treatment with daily oral berotralstat at two dose levels in subjects with HAE. 
The study is also evaluating the long-term effectiveness and impact on QoL or general well-
being of subjects who receive berotralstat prophylactic treatment, and the subject’s satisfaction 
with the medication. Target enrollment is 475 subjects with approximately 250 subjects from 
the United States. The study population includes adults (18 years and older) and adolescents 
(12 to 17 years of age) with a confirmed diagnosis of type I or II HAE who are either naïve or 
have received berotralstat in another study. Both 110 mg QD and 150 mg QD doses of 
berotralstat were initially being evaluated; however, following the completion of Study 302 Part 
1, all subjects are receiving berotralstat 150 mg QD. 
Screening visits are not required for previous berotralstat study participants if the last dose of 
study drug was <84 days prior. Subjects rolling over from another berotralstat study without 
treatment interruption or with <14 days of study drug interruption could have final study visit 
assessments for the parent study and baseline assessments for Study 204 performed at the 
same visit. The same values may be used for both assessments when possible.

discontinues development of the product for the prevention of angioedema attacks, whichever
	
occurs first. Telephone contact will occur at weeks 16, 20, 28, 32, 40, 44, 52, 56, 64, 68, 76, 80, 
88, and 92.

Key Exclusion Criteria 
1.		 Clinically significant abnormal ECG including but not limited to a QTcF >470 ms for 
women, a QTcF >450 ms for men, a PR interval >220 ms (both sexes), or ventricular 
and/or atrial premature contractions that are more frequent than occasional, and/or as 
couplets or higher in grouping. 
2.		 Known family history of sudden cardiac death. 
3.		 Calculated CLCR ŽĨ чϯϬ mL/min or AST or ALT value at least three-fold the ULN reference 
range at screening or at the last available visit prior to enrollment. 
4.		 Pregnancy or breast feeding or planned pregnancy during the study period. 
5.		 Use of any of the following prohibited medications: 
a.		 Angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors within 7 days of the baseline visit or 
planned initiation during the study (potential for exacerbation of HAE). 
b.		 Investigational drug exposure, other than berotralstat, within 30 days prior to the 
screening visit (or baseline if no screening visit) or initiation during the study. 
c.		 Use of a medication that is transported by P-gp and has a narrow therapeutic range 
within 7 days of the baseline visit or planned initiation during the study. For the 
purposes of this protocol, these are: aliskiren, digoxin, posaconazole, and talinolol. 
d.		 Use of a concomitant medication that is metabolized by CYP2D6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, 
or CYP3A4 and has a narrow therapeutic range, within 7 days of the baseline visit or 
planned initiation during the study, including those known to prolong the QT 
interval. 
Reviewer comment: The protocol initially included only subjects from prior berotralstat studies 
(version 6), however, was later amended (version 7) to allow enrollment of naïve patients. 
There was no minimum attack rate requirement for entry into the study; thus, this study 
population could potentially have less severe disease than the subjects in Study 302. 
Subject Removal Criteria 
Subjects were permanently withdrawn from the study drug if any of the following 
circumstances occurred: 
1.		 Emergence of any laboratory abnormality or AE that in the judgment of the investigator 
compromised the ability of the subject to continue study-specific procedures or it was 
considered not to be in the subject’s best interest as a result of an altered benefit-risk 
profile.

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
2.		 Recurrence of any significant treatment-emergent AST or ALT elevations or 
abnormalities indicative of significant hepatic toxicity.
	
3.		 Subsequent determination that inclusion/exclusion criteria were not met. 
4.		 Intercurrent illness or emergence of a new illness/medical condition that, in the 
judgment of the investigator, affected assessment of clinical status to a significant 
degree. 
5.		 Subject noncompliance with study drug or the protocol. 
6.		 The subject had a QTcF >500 ms (confirmed on repeat testing) or other significant QTcF 
changes. 
7.		 Subjects with a study drug-related grade 4 rash according to the DMID criteria. 
Study Treatments and Blinding 
Study treatment was not blinded. All subjects received open-label berotralstat capsules for oral 
administration at a dose of 110 or 150 mg QD. Subjects initially allocated to the 110 mg QD 
group received a daily dose as 110 mg capsules (one capsule per day). Subjects initially 
allocated to the 150 mg QD treatment group received a daily dose supplied as 50 mg capsules 
(three capsules per day) or 150 mg capsules (one capsule per day). Based on the results of Part 
1 of Study 302, all subjects will be transitioned to berotralstat 150 mg QD. 
Primary Endpoints 
The primary objective of the study is to assess the long-term safety of berotralstat. The safety 
endpoints are as follows: 
x	 The proportion of subjects who discontinue berotralstat as a result of a TEAE. 
x	 The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent serious adverse events (SAEs). 
x	 The proportion of subjects with TEAEs. 
x	 The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent grade 3 or 4 AEs. 
x	 The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent, treatment-related AE consistent 
with a drug rash. 
x	 The proportion of subjects with treatment-emergent grade 3 or 4 laboratory 
abnormalities. 
Secondary and Other Endpoints 
Efficacy endpoints include: 
x Number and rate of HAE attacks. 
x Durability of response (trend in attack rate over time). 
x Number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms. 
x Patient-reported outcomes (HAE disease-specific AE-QoL questionnaire scores and 
TSQM global satisfaction scores). 
x Number of attacks requiring attack medication. 
x Discontinuations because of lack of efficacy (through week 48 only). 
x Severity of attacks.

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Reviewer comment: The results of this open label, uncontrolled study primarily serve as 
supportive evidence of safety. Although the study captured efficacy outcomes, we did not rely 
on the results to provide evidence of effectiveness for approval, and thus the efficacy results 
will not be covered in this review. Safety results are discussed below. 
8.3.7.1. Study BCX7353-204 Results 
This section will focus on the safety results from the 18-month interim analysis period from the 
date of first subject enrollment (February 27, 2018) to the data cutoff date (August 20, 2019). 
As mentioned above, subjects were transitioned from the 110 mg to the higher 150 mg dose 
following data analysis from Part 1 of Study 302. Between June 11, 2019 and August 20, 2019 
(data cutoff), 22 of the 100 subjects initially allocated to the 110 mg group were transitioned to 
berotralstat 150 mg. However, all safety analyses were conducted based on the subject’s initial 
treatment dose assignment. As very few TEAEs occurred in subjects after crossing over to the 
higher dose given the short time period between dose escalation and data cutoff, this did not 
have a major impact on the conduct of the safety analyses. TEAEs which occurred in subjects 
after the transition to the 150 mg dose are discussed under Adverse Events. beginning on 11 
June 2019. 
Disposition 
Overall, a total of 254 subjects were screened and 227 were allocated to receive study drug— 
100 subjects to berotralstat 110 mg and 127 to berotralstat 150 mg (Table 49). 
The most common reason for screening failure was failure to meet the clinical diagnosis of HAE 
type I or II. Subjects were enrolled at 49 sites in 22 countries, mostly in Europe. There was a 
fairly high rate of early discontinuation of study drug (59 subjects [26%]). The most common 
reasons for discontinuation of study drug overall and prior to week 48 were perceived lack of 
efficacy (n=28/227 [12.3%]) and laboratory abnormalities or AEs (n=19/227 [8.4%]), 
respectively. Patients who stopped the study drug as a result of a perceived lack of efficacy 
were more likely to be in the 110 mg berotralstat group than the 150 mg berotralstat group (17 
versus 11 subjects, respectively). In contrast, patients who stopped the study drug because of 
an AE or laboratory abnormality were more likely to be in the 150 mg berotralstat group than 
the 110 mg berotralstat group (13 versus 6 subjects, respectively).

Baseline Disease Characteristics 
Table 51 lists the baseline characteristics of subj ects in Study 204. The majority of subjects 
(66.5%) were enro lled direct ly into this study without having participated in a prior berotra lstat 
study , and the proportion of subjects who had participated in a previous berotra lstat study was 
higher in the 150 mg group (48%) than the 110 mg group (15%). The mean age of symptom 
onset and age at diagnosis is consistent with the epidemio logy of the disease where symptoms 
common ly manifest with increasing frequency and severity around puberty and patients are 
diagnosed within the next few years after. A combination of abdominal and peripheral attacks 
being the most common locations is also consistent with the usual disease presentation . 
Patients in the 150mg treatment group had more laryngea l attacks within the past year and 
therefore this group may have had a greater disease burden at the start of the study.

Berotralstat Exposure 
Table 52 shows exposure to berotralstat in Study 204. The mean tota l exposure to study drug 
was 283 days. For subjects in the berotralstat 150 mg group, the mean time exposed to study 
drug (307 days) was greater than that in the berotra lstat 110 mg group (253 days). This 
discrepancy is noted by the App licant to be due to early preferential dosing allocation to 
150 mg. A substantia l number (49%) of subjects in the 150 mg group received 337 to 504 days 
of exposure , equiva lent to 1 to almost 2 years of therapy .

Adverse Events 
Rollover Subjects 
A tota l of three subjects reported 5 TEAEs after switching from berotra lstat 110 to 150 mg. 
However , in the tab les below, these TEAEs are attributed to the 110 mg dose group because 
subjects were analyzed according to the initia l treatment group allocation. One subject 
deve loped tendinitis , another deve loped a leg fracture and the third subject had three episodes 
of HAE attacks. The HAE attacks and the leg fracture were identified as SAEs; however , none of 
these five TEAEs appear related to study drug. None of the remaining 19 patients who 
transitioned from the 110 mg to the 150 mg dose reported TEAEs following the dose escalation. 
Therefore , analysis of the data by this reviewer did not revea l any difference in the safety

Deaths 
No deaths were reported in the OLE Study 204. 
Nonfatal SAEs 
Serious nonfatal AEs that occurred during the OLE are shown in Table 53. Any SAE which 
occurred in 2 or more subjects is listed , as well as any single event within a SOC which was 
previously identified as having a potentia l safety signa l in the pivotal study. For example, 
hepatobiliary disorder was still included despite only one event , due to the known GI safety 
prof ile of the drug. Most treatment-emergent SAEs were attacks of HAE (12 subjects [5.3%]), all 
of which required hospita lization. The 110 mg group had 10 subjects with HAE attacks with the 
remaining 2 in the 150 mg group, which was consistent with efficacy resu lts favoring the 
150 mg dose . GI SAEs were few-abdomina l pain, elevated liver enzymes, and an abnormal 
liver function test (each in one subject) (Table 53). Other significant SAEs that may be relevant 
but occurred infrequent ly were two episodes of pneumonia and one of biliary colic, all in the 
150 mg group. There was no clustering of SAEs in any other system organ class.

Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events 
The TEAEs with a frequency of ~10% in either of the berotra lstat groups or overa ll are listed in 
Table 55 . The safety profile was sim ilar to that of Study 302, with most AEs in the infections and 
GI SOCs. Most infections were mi ld and transient episodes of upper respiratory tract infections 
and nasopharyngitis. Although diarrhea was a more common AE in the 150 mg group , 
abdominal pain was more common in the 110 mg group . Therefore , a clear dose-response 
relationship for overall GI side effects could not be established. Similar to Study 302 Part 1, high 
rates of GI-related AEs did not dissuade most subjects from continuing the study drug . Most of 
these AEs were of mild-to-moderate severity; only four subjects had a grade 3 or 4 GI TEAE. 
Consistent with the efficacy resu lts from Study 302 Part 1, more HAE attacks occurred in the 
110 mg versus the 150 mg dose group .

Laboratory Findings 
LFTs 
As in Study 302, LFT elevations were observed in the OLE Study 204. Table 56 shows the 
maximum ALT elevation by group. ALT elevations greater than three-fold the ULN occurred 
more frequently in the 110 mg group (9%) versus the 150 mg group (4.7%). While there was no 
clear dose-response, the greatest ALT elevation (>10× ULN) occurred in the 150 mg group, as 
was the case in Study 302 Part 1.

The Applicant asserted that a history of prior androgen use was a risk factor for the 
deve lopment of elevated liver enzymes in their phase 2 and 3 studies. Although not evident in 
Study 302 Part 1, the relationship between ALT elevation with berotra lstat treatment and 
history of androgen use for HAE prophylaxis is more apparent in Study 204. Elevations >3x ULN 
only occurred in subjects who reported prior androgen therapy; however, there were 
insufficient data provided to determine the impact of other factors such as dose and duration 
of prior androgen use, time since cessation of androgen therapy, and transaminase elevation 
with androgen therapy to fully characterize this potential risk. Therefore , in the absence of 
additiona l data and a placebo contro l comparator , it is difficult to interpret this data into 
meaningful information that could be included in the product label to guide prescribers . 
Reviewer comment: Although androgen therapy is clearly associated with a higher risk of 
hepatocyte injury, there is no known mechanism by which prior androgen-exposed patients 
would be more susceptible to the hepatotoxic effects of berotralstat. A literature search was 
performed, and no similar relationship was found in the lanadelumab program or with other 
currently approved HAE therapeutic agents. 
120-Day Safety Update 
The Applicant also submitted a 120-day safety update report with a data cutoff at December 1, 
2019. This provided integrated safety data from all three parts of Study 302 (first patient 
enrolled March 14, 2018) and OL Study 204 (first patient enro lled February 27, 2018) . Most 
common TEAEs observed in the integrated Phase 2/ 3 studies remained consistent with what 
was reported in the pivotal tria l, particular ly mild to moderate gastrointestinal adverse events 
such as abdominal pain and diarrhea. No new safety concerns were identified in laboratory 
safety monitoring, with the exception of a single grade 3 AE of transient leukocytosis. No TEAEs

of arrhythmia or QT prolongation were reported. Overall, there is no evidence of any increasing 
or new toxicity with chronic use of berotralstat. 
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development 
In Study 302, one subject in the 110 mg group developed multiple myeloma, which was 
deemed unlikely to be related to study treatment, and one subject in the placebo group 
developed a leiomyoma. In Study 204, one subject in the 150 mg group developed acute 
myelocytic leukemia. Although rare events such as malignancy are unlikely to appear in a 
clinical development program of this size, berotralstat is not anticipated to have 
immunomodulatory effects that increase the risk of cancer in humans. Potential tumor and 
cancer risks to humans based on non-clinical studies were discussed in discussed in 
Section 5.1.1. 
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy 
All pregnancy tests administered during Part 1 of Study 302 were negative. Three pregnancies

(b) (6)
were reported for the OLE Study 204: Subjects , and a partner pregnancy 
for Subject . In both female subjects, study drug was stopped, and the estimated 
maximum drug exposure was 28 days. One female subject had an uncomplicated delivery of a 
healthy infant and the other had a delivery date of May 21, 2020. The male subject whose 
partner had become pregnant continued study drug and his partner delivered a healthy infant. 
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth 
The safety profile in adolescent patients 12 to 17 years of age enrolled in Study 302 and OLE 
Study 204 was similar to that in adults. Berotralstat is not expected to impact growth. Although 
the consequences of long-term suppression of plasma kallikrein are not fully known, the fact 
that congenital deficiency of prekallikrein is largely asymptomatic is reassuring. 
Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound 
Based on the mechanism of action, berotralstat is not expected to have any potential for drug 
abuse or dependence. 
Safety in the Postmarket Setting 
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarketing Experience 
There is no postmarketing experience with berotralstat. 
Expectations on Safety in the Postmarketing Setting 
Because Studies 204 and 302 excluded patients with certain levels of pre-existing LFT 
abnormalities, it is possible that patients with HAE with underlying hepatic dysfunction may 
experience more pronounced side effects not observed in clinical trials. However, the dosage 
125

recommendation for patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment will be 110 mg 
(Section 6.3.3). Overall, the patient population evaluated in Study 302 as well as Study 204 are 
reasonably representative of the HAE patients who are expected to take berotralstat in the 
postmarket setting. Although the clinical trial safety database is small due to the rarity of this 
disease, the safety profile observed in the clinical development program may be generalized to 
the target population. 
Integrated Assessment of Safety 
The safety data submitted with the NDA were sufficient to assess the safety of berotralstat in 
the proposed HAE population. The safety information for berotralstat is primarily derived from 
the pivotal, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial BCX7353-302, Part 1. Additional support for long-
term safety comes from the OLE Study BCX7353-204. Study 302 included 121 adult and 
adolescent HAE subjects, 81 of whom received at least one dose of berotralstat and of these 40 
received the proposed recommended starting dose of 150 mg once daily. A total of 227 
subjects (100 in the 110 mg group and 127 in the 150 mg group) received open-label 
berotralstat in the OLE Study 204. No deaths occurred in the berotralstat development 
program. SAEs were single events, only one of which occurred with berotralstat (in Study 302) 
and appeared unrelated to treatment. Gastrointestinal-related adverse events and 
transaminase elevations emerged as the main safety signals. In Part 1 of Study 302, TEAEs such 
as abdominal pain, vomiting, and diarrhea occurred more frequently in a dose-dependent 
manner with berotralstat compared to placebo. Dyspepsia and gastroesophageal reflux events 
occurred with greater frequency in the 110 mg dose group and led to one treatment 
discontinuation. That said, GI-related TEAEs were generally mild-to-moderate severity and self-
limited, and typically did not lead to study drug discontinuation. TEAEs related to transaminase 
elevations, primarily increased ALT, were reported with berotralstat treatment in the controlled 
and OLE studies. No potential Hy’s law cases were identified in the program. In Study 302, only 
one subject had AST/ALT >3× ULN; this subject was in the 150 mg group and had ALT >10× ULN, 
which led to treatment discontinuation. In OLE Study 204, there was no clear dose-response 
with regard to AST/ALT elevations >3× ULN; however, again, one subject in the 150 mg group 
experienced ALT >10× ULN. Overall, though, transaminase elevations were generally transient, 
asymptomatic, not dose-dependent, and in some cases confounded by underlying 
comorbidities and prior androgen use. In general, safety data from OLE study 204 was 
consistent with the placebo controlled data and supports chronic berotralstat use. Although not 
observed in clinical trials with the 150 mg dose, berotralstat prolongs the QT interval in a dose 
and concentration dependent manner based on data from the thorough QT study. Therefore, 
based on the pharmacologic action, QT prolongation is an anticipated risk with higher than 
recommended doses of 150 mg once daily. Overall, the berotralstat safety profile for the 
treatment of a rare, serious disease such as HAE is favorable. 
Conclusions and Recommendations 
The recommended regulatory action from both the clinical and statistical perspective is 
Approval. The berotralstat development program for HAE prophylaxis consisted of a single 
pivotal efficacy and safety trial, BCX7353-302, Part 1 that was adequately conducted and well-

greater and more clinically significant treatment effect in the higher dose arm supports a 
recommended starting dose of 150 mg once daily, with dose adjustments to 110 mg once daily 
in patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment and for patients with persistent GI-

The safety data submitted with the NDA were sufficient to assess the safety of berotralstat in 
the proposed HAE population. Gastrointestinal-related adverse events and transaminase

diarrhea, dyspepsia and gastroesophageal reflux occurred more frequently, often in a dose-

pharmacologic action of the drug. Efficacy and safety results across various demographic and 
baseline characteristic subgroups, including adolescents, were generally consistent with the 
overall findings. Overall, the risk/benefit profile for berotralstat in the treatment of a rare, 
serious disease such as HAE is favorable.

14. Division Director (DPACC) Comments 
This NDA provides for berotralstat as a prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 
years of age and older. Berotralstat is an NME that inhibits plasma kallikrein. The tradename is 
Orladeyo. HAE is a rare disease with variable attacks of swelling/edema that can potentially be 
life threatening. In patients with HAE, deficient or dysfunctional C1-INH leads to increased 
kallikrein activity and release of bradykinin which leads to the characteristic swelling of acute 
HAE attacks. Berotralstat decreases plasma kallikrein activity to control excess bradykinin 
generation in patients with HAE. 
The efficacy and safety of berotralstat was evaluated in a single 24-week, randomized, double-
blind, placebo controlled, clinical trial in 120 patients with HAE 12 years of age and older (Study 
302). While Study 302 had several parts, the focus of the review was on Part 1, the 24-week 
131 
Version date: October 12, 2018 
Reference ID: 4711626 

[PDF PAGE 133]
    
 
 
  
   
    
    
      
    
    
    
  
      
    
  
   
        
    
     
    
   
     
   
   
     
  
   
       
 
   
     
      
    
  
       
   
  
     
   
   
      
   
 
NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
placebo-controlled period. Two dose levels were evaluated: 110 mg once daily and 150 mg 
once daily. 
The primary endpoint was the rate of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks during the 24-week 
treatment period. HAE attacks are an established and meaningful efficacy endpoint for HAE 
programs. Results from this pivotal trial showed a significant reduction in HAE attacks over the 
24 week treatment period for both doses of berotralstat compared to placebo. The HAE attack 
rate per 28 days was 1.7, 1.3, and 2.4 for berotralstat 110 mg, berotralstat 150 mg, and placebo 
respectively with a rate ratio of 0.70 (95% CI 0.51, 0.95) [berotralstat 110 mg] and 0.56 (95% CI 
0.41, 0.77) [berotralstat 150 mg] relative to placebo.  Secondary endpoints were generally 
supportive of efficacy. While efficacy was demonstrated for both berotralstat doses, there was 
a numerically greater treatment effect with the 150 mg dose. In addition, there were 
numerically greater proportions of patients with 50%, 75%, and 90% reductions from baseline 
of investigator confirmed attack rates with the 150 mg dose compared to the 110 mg dose of 
berotralstat. Based upon these results, the sponsor has proposed the 150 mg dose of 
berotralstat. Overall, this single adequate and well-controlled trial is sufficient to provide 
substantial evidence of efficacy. Efficacy was demonstrated for each dose group of berotralstat 
and the results for the 150 mg group were statistically persuasive. 
The safety data were derived primarily from the single pivotal trial, Study 302, with some 
supportive data from the open-label Study 204. While the size of the safety database is small, 
this is not unusual for a rare disease program and overall the safety database is acceptable for 
this indication. Review of the safety data showed that GI adverse reactions (e.g. abdominal 
pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea) were the most commonly reported adverse reactions and 
there was a dose response demonstrated. There were no deaths. SAEs were limited and didn’t 
identify a particular safety signal. Elevated transaminases were also noted, but no cases of Hy’s 
law were identified. QT prolongation with doses higher than 150 mg were identified in the 
thorough QT study. Given the nonlinear PK for berotralstat, information regarding potential for 
QT prolongation with higher than approved doses will be included in the product labeling. 
The sponsor has proposed an indication in patients down to 12 years of age. There were only 
six adolescents in Study 302. Review of the efficacy and safety data for these six adolescents is 
consistent with the findings in adult patients. An additional 10 adolescents were evaluated in 
Study 204. No unique safety signals were identified. The pathophysiology of HAE is the same 
irrespective of age and efficacy in adolescents is expected to be similar to that in adults and 
may be extrapolated from the adult data. 
Overall, the submitted data support a favorable benefit risk profile for berotralstat for the 
prevention of HAE attacks in patients 12 years of age and older.  All disciplines recommend 
approval and I agree with approval of this application. Berotralstat provides a new oral 
treatment option for patients with HAE. Labeling has been agreed to by the FDA and the 
Applicant. The recommended dosing regimen is 150 mg once daily. A 110 mg daily dose is 
recommended for patients with moderate-to-severe hepatic impairment or for patients with 
persistent GI adverse reactions.

15. Office Director (OII) Comments 
I concur with the recommendation from the Division of Pulmonology, Allergy and Critical Care 
to approve berotralstat, an orally administered inhibitor of active plasma kallikrein, for 
prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 12 years of age and 
older. This new molecular entity represents a new prophylaxis option for patients with HAE. 
The efficacy of berotralstat for this rare disorder was demonstrated in a single double-blind, 
placebo-controlled trial. Both doses evaluated, 110 mg and 150 mg administered daily, 
significantly reduced investigator-confirmed HAE attack rates compared to placebo through 
24 weeks of treatment. The numerically greater treatment effect supports a recommended 
starting dose of 150 mg, with use of 110 mg recommended for patients with moderate-to-
severe hepatic impairment and for patients with persistent gastrointestinal-related adverse 
reactions. 
Berotralstat was generally well tolerated with gastrointestinal-related adverse reactions and 
transaminase elevations as the main safety findings. Prolongation of the QT interval is an 
expected finding at doses above 150 mg based on the pharmacology of the drug. Product 
labeling adequately conveys the safety risks associated with use of berotralstat; a REMS will not 
be required. 
The pathophysiology of HAE is similar in adolescents and adults. Although the number of 
adolescent subjects enrolled in clinical trials was small, efficacy and safety results were similar 
to those in adults, supporting inclusion of pediatric patients aged 12 years of age and older in 
the indicated population.
