Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology 
Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology 
16.2.1.1. Integrated Summary and Safety Evaluation 
The nonclinical program of berotralstat consists of studies of pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, 
and toxicology. The toxicology program consists of studies of general toxicity (chronic oral 
toxicity studies in rats and monkeys), genetic toxicity (standard battery of testing assays), 
carcinogenicity (oral traditional 2-year bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in a transgenic 
mouse model), and reproductive and developmental toxicity (teratology studies in rats and 
rabbits, a fertility and early embryofetal developmental study in rats, and a peri- and post-natal 
development [PPND] study in rats). 
16.2.1.2. Pharmacology 
Berotralstat decreases vascular permeability and edema in patients with hereditary 
angioedema (HAE) by reducing the blood level of bradykinin, a potent vasodilatory agent. 
Berotralstat decreases the bradykinin level by inhibiting kallikrein (inhibition constant 0.44nM), 
which cleaves and releases bradykinin from high-molecular-weight kininogen. The half-maximal 
inhibitory concentration for kallikrein activity in activated human, monkey, and rat plasma is 
5.4, 5.2, and 7.2nM, respectively. 
Berotralstat inhibited currents of K+, Na+, and Ca++ channels in vitro and prolonged QT intervals 
in monkeys and humans. It also affected duration and amplitude of action potential in vitro. 
The effect of berotralstat on QT has been thoroughly investigated in clinical studies. See the 
section on effects on the Cardiovascular System. 
16.2.1.3. Pharmacokinetics and Toxicokinetics 
Berotralstat was rapidly absorbed after oral administration to animals. The plasma drug level 
generally increased in proportion to the administered dose. There were no significant 
difference in plasma drug level between sexes. Oral bioavailability was 33% and 45% in rats and 
134 
Version date: October 12, 2018 
Reference ID: 4711626 

[PDF PAGE 136]
    
 
 
  
     
       
   
   
 
  
     
  
  
    
    
   
  
     
   
  
      
    
  
    
     
   
   
     
      
     
  
  
  
 
 
 
 
   
    
NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
monkeys, respectively. The half-life was 18.5, 22.7, and 93 hours in rats, monkeys, and humans, 
respectively. The protein-binding capacity was high in humans, rats, and mice (98.7 to 99.8%) 
but low in monkeys and rabbits (74.1% to 81.9%). The tissues showing the highest maximum 
concentrations of radioactivity were the liver, kidney medulla, spleen, adrenal glands, kidneys, 
lungs, and pituitary gland. 
Metabolism of berotralstat was low across species. About 61% of orally administered 
berotralstat was excreted unchanged in rats. Less than 10% of berotralstat was metabolized 
when incubated with human cryopreserved hepatocytes for 360 minutes. No unique or major 
human metabolites were observed. 
Bioaccumulation of berotralstat occurred in rats, but not in monkeys. In rats, the berotralstat 
area under the curve from time zero to 24 hours (AUC0-24) in week 26 was approximately three-
fold that in week 1. The monkeys showed no apparent difference in AUC0-24 between weeks 1 
and 39. For example, the respective mean AUC0-24 at 20 mg/kg/day in weeks 1 and 26 was 3190 
and 9710 ng.h/mL in rats and 3310 and 3110 ng.h/mL in monkeys. 
16.2.1.4. Toxicology 
A comprehensive toxicology program was conducted to evaluate the general toxicity, genetic 
toxicity, carcinogenicity, and reproductive and developmental toxicity of berotralstat. General 
toxicity studies included chronic oral toxicity studies for up to 6 and 9 months in rats and 
monkeys, respectively. Evaluation of the genetic and reproductive toxicity potential involved 
standard batteries of tests. The carcinogenicity studies comprised a standard 2-year bioassay 
and a study in a transgenic mouse model. A comprehensive evaluation of the effect of 
berotralstat on growth and development in juvenile animals has not been completed. 
Evaluation of the general toxicity of berotralstat included chronic toxicity studies for up to 
6 months in rats and 9 months in monkeys. These studies showed that the toxicity of 
berotralstat was dependent on the dose and treatment duration. The berotralstat doses used in 
the chronic toxicity studies were up to 20 and 80 mg/kg/day in rats and monkeys, respectively. 
Higher doses of berotralstat were used in the shorter-term studies (up to 75 and 
100 mg/kg/day in rats and monkeys, respectively). The target organs of toxicity were the 
kidney, liver, intestine, and others ().

Mortality 
Dose- and treatment duration-dependent deaths occurred in rats, but not in monkeys. Deaths 
ŝŶ ƌĂƚƐ ŽĐĐƵƌƌĞĚ Ăƚ шϳϱϬ mg/kg after single doses of 150 mg/kg/day for 7 days (AUC0-24 
58,000 ng.h/mL), and 75 mg/kg/day for 4 weeks (AUC0-24 40,500 ng.h/mL). No deaths occurred 
at a single dose of 450 mg/kg (AUC0-24 11,900 ng.h/mL), 25 mg/kg/day for 4 weeks (AUC0-24 
11,200 ng.h/mL), or 20 mg/kg/day for 26 weeks (AUC0-24 16,000 ng.h/mL). No deaths were 
observed in monkeys at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day for 28 days (AUC0-24 16,000 ng.h/mL). 
Studies in Rats 
The repeat-dose studies in Wistar Han rats involved oral (gavage) administration of berotralstat 
for 4, 13, and 26 weeks. The berotralstat dose was up to 75, 20, and 20 mg/kg/day in the 4-, 
13-, and 26-week studies, respectively. The results identified the following as the target organs 
of berotralstat toxicity: liver, kidney, and intestine. 
In a 4-week study (#806-131), rats (10 and 5/sex/dose in the main study and recovery period, 
respectively) were dosed with 0 (water), 5, 10, 25, and 75 mg/kg/day once daily for 28 days. 
The recovery period was 2 weeks. Additional rats (three and nine of each sex in the vehicle and 
treatment groups, respectively) were included in the toxicokinetics (TK) analysis. The mean 
AUC0-24, day 28, male/female was 1310, 3830, 20,200, and 130,000 ng.h/mL in the low-dose (LD), 
mid-low-dose, mid-high-dose, and high-dose (HD) groups, respectively. The HD rats showed 
audible breathing and piloerection, decreased bodyweight (9% to 16%), clinical chemistry 
changes (decreased mean corpuscular volume (MCV) and mean corpuscular hemoglobin (MCH) 
and an increased number of neutrophils, aspartate aminotransferase [AST] level, and alanine 
aminotransferase [ALT] level), and increased liver weight. The microscopic changes included 
vacuolation/foamy macrophages in multiple organs (the adrenal glands [cortical], brain 
[choroid plexus], liver, lung, lymph nodes [mesenteric], ovaries, pituitary glands, salivary glands, 
and intestine); tubular regeneration in the kidney; and minimal degeneration/necrosis and 
hypertrophy of the bile ducts. Most of the microscopic changes were confined to the HD group, 
but liver and bile duct changes were observed in all of the groups. The findings in the LD groups 
were of low incidence and minimal severity. 
In a 13-week study (#806-144), rats (15 and 5/sex/dose in the main study and recovery period, 
respectively) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 
26 weeks. The recovery period was 6 weeks. Additional rats (12/sex/dose) were included in the 
TK analysis. The respective mean AUC0-24, day 90 in the LD, MD, and HD groups was 318, 1890, and 
6330 ng.h/mL in males; and 476, 3480, and 16,000 ng.h/mL in females. The MD and HD groups 
showed minimal to mild bile duct hypertrophy in males and females (incidence 10 MD males, 
14 HD males, 15 MD females, and 15 HD females). The HD group showed bile duct hyperplasia 
(6 females only) and vacuolated macrophages (13 and 15 males and females, respectively). The 
no observable adverse effects level [NOAEL] of the study was 2.5 mg/kg/day, corresponding to 
a mean AUC0-24 of 397 ng.h/mL. 
In a 26-week study (#8001291), rats (15 and 5/sex/dose in the main study and recovery section, 
respectively) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 1.0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for

26 weeks. Additional rats (nine/sex/dose) were included in the TK analysis. The recovery period 
was 13 weeks in duration. The mean AUC0-24, day 182, male/female was 56.9, 297, 2030, and 
9710 ng.h/mL in the LD, mid-low-dose, mid-high-dose, and HD groups, respectively. 
Microscopic changes were observed in the hepatobiliary system, lymph nodes (mesenteric), 
spleen, intestine (jejunum and duodenum), and nasal cavity in the шϳ͘ϱ mg/kg/day groups. 
Most of the changes were vacuolation in the mucosa and foamy histiocytes. The bile duct 
showed minimal-to-moderate hyperplasia. Changes in the nasal cavity were believed to be 
related to gastric reflux and irritation. The NOAEL of the study was 2.5 mg/kg/day, 
corresponding to a mean AUC0-24 of 297 ng.h/mL. 
Studies in Monkeys 
Studies completed in nonhuman primates involved oral (gavage) administration of berotralstat 
for 4, 13, and 39 weeks in cynomolgus monkeys. In the 4-week study (Study 806-132), monkeys 
(five and two/sex/dose in the control, MD, and HD groups in the main study and recovery 
period, respectively; and three/sex in the MD main study) were dosed with 0, 10, 30, and 
100 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 28 days. The mean AUC0-24, day 28, male/female was 1350, 5320, and 
146,000 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. The HD group showed emesis and 
watery feces; changes in electrocardiogram parameters; increases in lipase (>7-fold), AST 
(~3-fold), and ALT (6- to 10-fold) levels, increases in the organ weight of the kidney and liver; 
and microscopic changes. Microscopic changes were observed in the kidney (basophilic tubules 
and tubular degeneration/regeneration), liver (diffused fatty changes and increased 
cytoplasmic collagen and hepatocellular hypertrophy), intestine (histiocytic infiltration), and 
spleen (follicular lymphoid hyperplasia). No significant findings were observed in the recovery 
animals after the 3-week recovery period. 
In a 13-week study (Study 806-145), monkeys (four and two/sex/dose in main study and 
recovery sections, respectively) were dosed with 0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 
91 days. The recovery period was 6 weeks. The mean AUC0-24, day 91, male/female was 278, 930, and 
3350 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. No treatment-related findings were 
observed in any berotralstat treatment groups. The NOAEL was 20 mg/kg/day. 
In a 39-week study (Study 8001292), monkeys (four and two/sex/dose in the main study and 
recovery section, respectively) were dosed with 0, 2.5, 7.5, and 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat for 
273 days. The recovery period was 13 weeks. The mean AUC0-24, day 273, male/female was 240, 559, 
and 2880 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. No treatment-related findings 
were observed in any of the berotralstat treatment groups. The NOAEL was 20 mg/kg/day. 
In another 39-week study (#806-162), monkeys (6, 4, 4, and 6/sex in the C, LD, MD, and HD 
groups, respectively) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 30, 55, and 80 mg/kg/day berotralstat 
for 273 days. Additional monkeys were included for a 13-week interim analysis 
(three/sex/dose) and recovery (two/sex/dose in the control and HD groups only). The mean 
AUC0-24, day 273, male/female was 3950, 7780, and 13,500 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, 
respectively. The MD and HD groups showed decreases (up to 38%) in fibrinogen concentration, 
increases in AST and ALT levels (up to 4- and 8.7-fold, respectively) and a decrease in albumin

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
Targets of Berotralstat Toxicity 
Kidney 
The kidneys are target organs of berotralstat toxicity in both rats and monkeys. Dose-
dependent increases in the incidence of tubular degeneration and regeneration were observed 
at 75 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 4-week rat study and at ш55 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 
39-week study in monkeys. The finding was absent at 20 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 26-week 
study in rats and at 30 mg/kg/day berotralstat in the 39-week study in monkeys. 
Phospholipidosis 
Phospholipidosis was a consistent finding in rats and monkeys (Section 5.1). Phospholipidosis, 
however, was not observed in humans in clinical trials (refer to the end-of-phase 2 internal 
meeting held on October 25, 2017). 
Cardiovascular System 
Berotralstat caused dose- and plasma-concentration-dependent increases in the QT interval in 
monkeys and humans. See Section 5.1 and the interdisciplinary review team review completed 
by Dr. Girish Bende et al. on April 15, 2020 (DARRTS ID 4592746). 
Safety Margins 
Animal toxicity studies generally provided safety margins of greater than one for relevant 
findings in general toxicity studies on a plasma drug AUC basis (Table 61). Some safety margins 
were smaller than 1 (i.e., 0.1 to 0.8), but these were generally irrelevant, being  attributable to 
phospholipidosis, which was absent in humans. The safety margins for reproductive and 
developmental toxicity and carcinogenicity are discussed in the labeling review.

Reproductive Toxicity 
A standard battery of tests for reproductive and developmental toxicity of berotralstat did not 
reveal any significant treatment effect (Table 62). The battery included a fertility and early 
embryonic developmental study in rats, embryofetal development (EFD) studies in rats and 
rabbits (one each), and a PPND toxicity study in rats. None of the studies showed findings of 
significant concern. Berotralstat was nonteratogenic under the test conditions and did not 
affect fertility or PPND in rats.

(GD) 7. Males were evaluated for fertility and sperm quality (day 63). Females were evaluated 
for fertility and uterine contents on GD 13. The HD group showed increases in the incidence of 
audible breathing in both sexes (incidence three and six male and female rats, respectively). 
The HD males showed a statistically significant decrease in bodyweight at the end of treatment 
(5%, p<0.05) and a decrease in food consumption (up to 14%, p<0.01). The mean plasma drug 
level was 835 and 795 ng/mL in males and females at the last day of treatment (on treatment 
day 63 and GD 7, respectively). No treatment-related effects were observed in any of the 
fertility parameters in either sex and in the sperm analysis in males. Berotralstat did not affect 
fertility in male or female rats under the test conditions. 
In an EFD study in rats (Study #9000644), pregnant dams (21 to 23/dose) were dosed by oral 
gavage with 0, 10, 25, and 75 mg/kg/day berotralstat during the organogenesis period (GD 6 to 
17). They were euthanized on GD 21 for examination of uterine contents. Four (of twenty-
three) HD dams showed audible breathing. The HD group showed a statistically significant 
decrease in mean bodyweight gain (34%, p<0.01). No treatment-related effects were observed 
in any of the fetal structural parameters evaluated. The mean AUC on GD 17 was 3510, 12,400, 
and 24,600 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively. Berotralstat was 
nonteratogenic in Sprague–Dawley rats under the test conditions. 
In an EFD study (Study #9000815), pregnant New Zealand white rabbit does were dosed by oral 
gavage with 0, 20, 50, and 100 mg/kg/day berotralstat during the organogenesis period (GD 7 
to 19). They were euthanized on GD 29 for the examination of uterine contents. The mean 
plasma AUC on day 19 was 880, 2350, and 4410 ng.h/mL in the LD, MD, and HD groups, 
respectively. The HD group showed statistically significant decreases in bodyweight gain and 
food consumption (100% and 32%, respectively; p<0.05). The HD group had abortions and early 
terminations (two rabbits each). These findings were not considered of concern because they 
were associated with significant maternal toxicity (i.e., significant decreases in bodyweight). No 
treatment-related effects were observed in any other fetal development parameters. 
Berotralstat can cross the placenta; the drug was present in fetal blood at up to 11% of the 
concentration in maternal blood. Berotralstat was nonteratogenic in New Zealand white rabbits 
under the test conditions. 
In a PPND study in rats (Study #806-157), dams (F0) were dosed by oral gavage with 0, 10, 25, 
and 45 mg/kg/day berotralstat during pregnancy and lactation (i.e., from GD 6 to lactation day 
[LAD] 20). The respective mean plasma berotralstat concentration in the LD, MD, and HD dams 
was 46.2, 183.3, and 312.8 on GD 6 and 63.8, 360.3, and 699 ng/mL on LAD 14. Dams gave 
births to and nurtured pups (F1) naturally. The berotralstat concentration in pup plasma on LAD 
14 was <5% of the maternal maximum plasma concentration (Cmax). Pups grew and matured 
naturally and were assessed for growth and behavioral development parameters (e.g., motor 
activity, learning, and memory) while growing. The F1 mated at the age of 10 weeks and 
pregnant females were euthanized on GD 13 for the examination of uterine contents. Both 
dams and pups (F0 and F1, respectively) in the HD group showed lower bodyweight gain during 
the lactation period (up to 7.8%, p<0.01). The dams also showed a statistically significant 
decrease in food consumption (up to 10%). Therefore, berotralstat did not affect PPND 
parameters in rats under the test conditions. The NOAEL was 25 mg/kg/day.

Juvenile Animal Study 
No comprehensive toxicity study in juvenile animals has been completed to evaluate the effect 
of berotralstat on growth and development. The Applicant states that they evaluated the effect 
of berotralstat on growth and development in a PPND study in rats (#806-157). This review 
disagrees with the Applicant based on the limitation of the design of the PPND study. 
Specifically, pups received limited exposure through milk during the lactation period and no 
drug exposure postweaning. During the lactation period, the plasma berotralstat level in pups 
was less than 5% that in maternal blood. Such limited exposure to berotralstat cannot fully 
evaluate its effect on the growth and development of rat pups. 
The lack of a juvenile animal study does not preclude approval for the current indication. The 
Applicant stated that the animals used in their toxicity program were comparable to adolescent 
humans. Specifically, dosing in the 26-week study in rats was initiated at 10 weeks of age. The 
39-week toxicity studies in monkeys commenced dosing at a median age of 2 years 10 months 
to 3 years 3 months. The human ages equivalent to those of the rats and monkeys are 
approximately 14 and 12 years, respectively. The Applicant should consider a well-designed 
study to fully evaluate the effect of berotralstat on growth and development in juvenile animals 
if they intend to develop berotralstat for younger pediatric patients in the future.

There are no safety concerns regarding impurities in the drug product. Six impurities were 
identified and discussed in the new drug application—

3 The NOAEL in rat and monkey 13-week toxicity studies was 20 mg/kg 
4 Based on a 150 mg dose in a 60 kg patient 
Abbreviations: BCX, berotralstat; NOAEL, no observable adverse effect level 
The nonclinical review team consulted the chemistry review team regarding the structural 
alerts of the impurities and the Applicant’s safety assessment (via email dated May 8, 2020). 
Dr. Lawrence Perez found no structural alerts of potential genotoxicity for any of the three 
impurities (source, email on May 8, 2020). Further, these compounds have groups present in 
berotralstat and are considered nonmutagenic per the International Council for Harmonisation-
M7 guidance. Because these compounds were present at the proposed levels in repeat-dose 
toxicity studies in rats and monkeys, these impurities are considered qualified. 
Conclusion 
The Applicant has conducted adequate nonclinical characterization and safety evaluation of 
berotralstat. Findings in the general toxicity studies of berotralstat in animals have been 
adequately monitored and addressed in clinical trials. Genetic toxicity, carcinogenicity, and 
reproductive and developmental toxicity studies identified no safety signal of concern. The 
reviewer recommends approval of the drug from the nonclinical perspective.

Data

missing date/time, PK without dosing, and outliers. The final Nonlinear Mixed-Effects Modeling 
data file for analysis contained 10,437 PK observations from 771 subjects. Table 66 provides 
summary statistics of the baseline demographic covariates in the analysis dataset.

Base Model 
The base model for berotralstat was a linear three-compartment model with first-order 
absorption, an absorption lag time, and linear elimination (Figure 10). An effect of dose on 
relative bioavailability was included in the base model to address the nonlinear PK of 
berotralstat. Interindividual variability was modelled assuming a log-normal distribution for 
patient-level random effects. Residual variability was tested as additive, proportional, or both 
on the dependent variable. The stochastic approximation/expectation maximization (SAEM) 
method was used for parameter estimation, and the relative standard error (RSE) was used for 
hypothesis testing, with a forward-addition RSE of 0.510 (p=0.05) and a backward-elimination 
RSE of 0.388 (p=0.005). The SAEM was used because it can handle complex models with many 
parameters. 
Figure 10. Diagram of the Base Model 
Source: Revised from Figure 9 of the Applicant’s Population PK report (BCX7353-PPK-01-A2 Addendum). 
Covariate Analysis 
Clinical judgment, physiologic relevance, and mechanistic plausibility were used to determine 
which covariates should be tested with the various PK parameters. Additionally, the collinearity 
of covariates was assessed to ensure that no collinear covariates were added to the model. The 
effects of the covariate parameters body weight, age, gender, and ethnicity on apparent oral 
clearance (CL/F) and apparent central volume (Vc/F); bilirubin on CL/F; and food effect on the 
absorption rate (Ka) were tested in the covariate analysis. Continuous covariates were 
146

NDA/BLA Multidisciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 214094 
ORLADEYO (berotralstat) 
evaluated using a power function and categorical covariates were parameterized as a fractional 
change. 
Final Model 
The final model (Model 258) was a three-compartment model with first-order absorption with 
an absorption lag time and linear elimination. The effect of between-subject variability on 
clearance, volume of distribution, Ka, and absorption lag time was evaluated. A between-study 
random effect on clearance was included. Bodyweight was the only covariate for clearance and 
volume. An effect of dose on relative bioavailability was included to address the nonlinear PK of 
berotralstat (higher dose predicting higher bioavailability). Residual variability was modeled by 
a combined additive and proportional component. 
The parameter estimates for the final model are listed in Table 67, and the goodness-of-fit plots 
in Figure 11. Selected visual predictive check plots for the final model are shown in Figure 12. 
Figure 13 shows that inclusion of bodyweight in the final model accounted for the trend 
between the random effects of berotralstat PK parameters and bodyweight in the base model.

Reviewer’s comments: In general, the Applicant’s population PK analysis is considered 
acceptable to support the labeling claims in Section 12.3 of the prescribing information. The 
Applicant’s analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified. 
The SAEM method was used by the Applicant for parameter estimation. Other estimation 
methods were evaluated by the reviewer, but the model crashed using the first-order 
conditional estimation and first-order conditional estimation I methods. This might explain why 
the SAEM method was selected by the Applicant. 
The reviewer noted that bilirubin and food effect were identified as significant covariates on CL 
and absorption parameters (Ka and bioavailability for dose in compartment 1) by statistical 
testing; however, both were removed before model finalization as not clinically significant. 
Assessment of clinical significance should be conducted after model development, and a 
covariate’s effect on berotralstat exposure should be interpreted in the context of the known 
exposure–response relationships for efficacy and safety. Although the reviewer does not fully 
agree with the Applicant’s covariate analysis, this does not change the conclusion of no clinically 
relevant covariates and no recommended dose adjustment for covariates. 
Bodyweight is a major factor that influences the exposure of berotralstat. In Table 68, the 
median bodyweight in the pooled dataset is 75.1 kg, within the 60 to 80 kg bodyweight group 
(low-weight adults) in Table 66. Higher exposure (~15%) was predicted in adults of 40 to 60 kg 
and adolescents 12 to 18 years of age compared to adults of 60 to 80 kg. The higher exposure in 
lighter patients is not expected to cause meaningful QT prolongation, which is a major safety 
concern; therefore, no dose adjustment is recommended. Furthermore, berotralstat was 
effective in subjects of high bodyweight and showed no substantial safety signal in subjects with 
low bodyweight (Section 16.3.2), supporting the recommendation of no dose adjustment.

BCX7353-203. The effect of berotralstat on the target enzyme, plasma kallikrein, was evaluated 
by comparing specific amidolytic activity in plasma samples taken after drug administration to 
that in baseline samples. Exposure–response analyses demonstrated a high correlation 
between ex vivo kallikrein inhibition activity and the plasma berotralstat concentration, which

concentrations of the 110 and 150 mg doses were 45 and 70 ng/mL; therefore, both reached 
the plateau for kallikrein inhibition.

Because bodyweight is a significant covariate that influences the exposure of berotralstat, the 
potential impact of bodyweight on efficacy was examined by summarizing the investigator-
confirmed attack rates by bodyweight category using the data collected in Part 1 of Study 302. 
The mean attack rate varied among the three bodyweight categories: 1.793, 1.810, and 2.219 
for berotralstat 110 mg; 1.799, 2.022, and 1.251 for berotralstat 150 mg; and 2.484, 2.015, and 
2.901 for placebo low-, middle-, and high-weight subjects, respectively (Table 69). The 110 and 
150 mg doses demonstrated effectiveness in high-weight subjects. The variability in efficacy 
among bodyweight groups may be attributable to the small sample size of each bodyweight 
tertile, and/or the nonstratification of baseline disease severity by bodyweight tertile.

16.3.2.2. Exposure-Safety Relationship 
Exposure-Electrocardiogram Interval Relationship (QT-Integrated Review Team Analysis) 
Based on the analyses by the QT-IRT, berotra lstat caused a dose- and concentration-dependent 
increase in the QT interva l at the doses (150 and 450 mg) evaluated. However , the QT interva l 
was not pro longed to any clinically relevant extent at the peak concentrations for the 
recommended dose of 150 mg once dai ly, and "'1.5-fold higher exposure than the 
recommended 150 mg dose . This covers the exposure increase ("'15%) predicted for subjects of 
lower bodyweight (underweight adults or ado lescents in Table 68). As a result, the higher 
exposure in lighter patients is not expected to cause meaningfu l QT prolongation , and no dose 
adjustment is recommended. Refer to the QT-Integrated Review Team review (Reference ID, 
4592746) for details . 
Applicant's Analysis 
An analysis of treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) by dose leve l was conducted using 
the data collected in Part 1 of Study BCX7353-302. No substantia l safety findings were 
identified in the 110 and 150 mg groups . The TEAE analysis identified on ly a slightly higher 
frequency of gastrointestina l (Gl)-re lated TEAEs for the higher doses: 150 mg (50.0%) >110 mg 
(41.5%) >placebo (35.9%). No clear dose-dependent trend was identified for other TEAEs. 
The potentia l effect of bodyweight on safety was evaluated by summarizing TEAEs by 
bodyweight category using the data collected in the integrated phase 2/3 prophy lactic long­
term studies (Studies 204 and 302). Overall, there were no differences in the percentage of 
subjects who experienced TEAEs based on baseline bodyweight terti le: no bodyweight-based

NDA No. 214094      Pharmacology/Toxicology Review    Reviewer: Luqi Pei, Ph.D. 
LABELING RECOMMENDATION
 
alterations 
This review recommends the following text for nonclinical sections of the ORLADEYO 
(berotralstat) capsule label. The nonclinical sections include the following: USE in 
SPECIFIC POPULATION (8.1 and 8.2), CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY (12.1), and 
NONCLINICAL TOXICOLOGY (13.1).  
8.1 Pregnancy 
Risk Summary 
There are insufficient data in pregnant women to inform drug-related risks 
with ORLADEYO use in pregnancy. Based on animal reproduction studies, no 
evidence of structural 
(b) (4) was observed when berotralstat was 
administered orally to pregnant rats and rabbits during organogenesis at doses up 
to approximately 10 and 2 times, respectively, the maximum recommended human 
daily dose (MRHDD) in adults on an AUC basis [see Data]. 
The background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage for the indicated 
population is unknown. In the U.S. general population, the estimated background 
risk of major birth defects and miscarriage in clinically recognized pregnancies is 2­
4% and 15-20%, respectively. 
Data 
Animal Data 
In animal reproduction studies, oral administration of berotralstat to pregnant rats 
and rabbits during the period of organogenesis did not cause fetal structural 
alterations. The berotralstat dose in rats and rabbits was up to approximately 10 
and 2 times, respectively, the MRHDD in adults (on an AUC basis at maternal 
doses of 75 and 100 mg/kg/day, respectively). In a pre- and postnatal development 
study in rats, oral administration of berotralstat to pregnant rats during the period of 
organogenesis until delivery at doses up to 45 mg/kg/day (approximately 2 times of 
the MRHD on a mg/m 2 basis) did not cause fetal structural alterations. Berotralstat 
concentrations in the fetal blood was approximately 5 – 11% of the maternal blood. 
8.2 Lactation 
Risk Summary 
There are no data on the presence of berotralstat in human milk, its effects on the 
breastfed infant, or its effects on milk production. However, when a drug is present 
in animal milk, it is likely that the drug will be present in human milk. Low levels of 
berotralstat were detected in the plasma of rat pups when dams were dosed with 
the drug orally during the lactation period. The berotralstat concentration in the pup 
plasma was approximately 2% of the maternal plasma (see Data). 
The developmental and health benefits of breastfeeding should be considered 
along with the mother’s clinical need for ORLADEYO and any potential adverse 
Reference ID: 4646388 
2 

[PDF PAGE 165]
NDA No. 214094 Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Reviewer: Luqi Pei. Ph.D. 
effects on the breastfed infant from ORLADEYO or from the underlying maternal 
condition. 
Data 
Animal Data 
In a pre- and postnatal development study in rats, berotralstat was administered to 
dams during the pregnancy and lactation periods at doses up to 45 mg/kg/day 
(approximately 2 times of the MRHD on a mg/m2 basis). Berotralstat was detected 
in the plasma of pups during the lactation period. The berotralstat concentration in 
the pup plasma was approximately 2% of the maternal plasma. Both dams and 
pups at 45 mg/kg/day showed statistically significant decreases in body weight 
gain (p < 0.05). No treatment-related effects were observed at 25 mg/kg/day 
(approximately equal to the MRHD on a mg/m2 basis). 
12.1 Mechanism of Action 
Berotralstat is a p lasma kallikrein inhibitor that binds plasma kallikrein and inhibits 
its proteolytic activity. Plasma kallikrein is a protease that cleaves high-molecular­
weight-kininogen (HMWK) to generate cleaved HMWK (cHMWK) and bradykinin, a 
potent vasodilator that increases vascular permeability resulting in swelling and 
pain associated with HAE. In patients with HAE due to C1-inhibitor (C1-INH) 
deficiency or dysfunction, normal regulation of plasma kallikrein activity is not 
present, which leads to uncontrolled increases in plasma kallikrein activity and 
results in angioedema attacks. Berotralstat decreases plasma kallikrein activity to 
control excess bradykinin generation in patients with HAE. 
13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility 
Carcinogenesis

NDA No. 214094      Pharmacology/Toxicology Review    Reviewer: Luqi Pei, Ph.D. 
LABELING REVIEW
 
Edits to the nonclinical information in the ORLADEYO (berotralstat) label proposed by 
the Applicant are recommended. The nonclinical information is described in the 
following sections: 8.1 Pregnancy, 8.2 Lactation, 12.1 Mechanism of Action, and 13.1 
Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility. Edits are made to ensure that the 
to-be-approved label reflects the Agency’s most current thinking.  
1. INTRODUCTION 
This review evaluates the nonclinical information of berotralstat in the ORLADEYO label 
proposed by BioCryst on December 3, 2019 and July 9, 2020 (SDNs 0001 and 0029, 
respectively). Specifically, the review evaluates the nonclinical information in the 
following subsections of the label: 8.1, 8.2, 12.1, and 13.1. The review recommends 
major edits, including both the text and dose ratios between animals and humans.  
Berotralstat is indicated for the prevention of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 
12 years of age and older. The proposed clinical dosing regimen is 150-mg berotralstat 
once daily.  The mean AUC0-24 at 150-mg/day berotralstat in patients is 2540 ng.h/mL. 
This review consists of 8 sections. Section 1 provides background information relevant 
to the current review. Section 2 discusses dose ratios between animals and humans in 
the nonclinical sections. Section 3 discusses methods and rationale used for this 
review. Sections 4 - 7 evaluate subsections of the proposed labeling, rationale for the 
recommended editing, and line editing. Section 8 provides a summary of overall 
recommendations. A clean copy of the recommended text of the nonclinical sections of 
the ORLADEYO label is presented in the LABELING RECOMMENDATION section 
which is located at the beginning of the document. 
2. EXPOSURE MULTIPLES 
This review derives exposure multiples between animals and humans on an AUC and 
mg/m2/day basis. Exposure multiples based on AUCs are preferred. When AUC data is 
unavailable, exposure multiples are calculated on a mg/m 2/day basis [human equivalent 
dose (Human HED)]. The following exposures in humans are used to calculate the 
exposure multiples: AUC 0-24 = 2540 ng.h/mL and Human HED = 92.5 mg/m 2/day. Both 
parameters are based on a maximum recommended human daily dose (MRHDD) of 
150 mg. A body weight of 60-kg was used to calculate the Human HED [150 mg/day/60 
kg x 37 (km) = 92.5 mg/m 2/day]. Briefly, all but one exposure multiple (i.e., 45 
mg/kg/day dose in rats) were derived on an AUC basis (Table 1).

exposure ratio (ratHED / humanHED) = 270/92.5 = 2.4. 
3. METHODS OF EVALUATION 
The review uses product labels of drugs approved for the same indication and similar 
mechanism of actions as references to maintain consistency across approved labels. 
Specifically, the review references to the label for KALBITOR, FIRAZYR, and 
TAKHZYRO (BLA 125277, NDA 022150, and BLA 761090, respectively) for 
consistency. These products were approved for the treatment of acute attacks or 
prophylaxis of HAE. The current application proposed to prevent HAE attacks from 
occurring.  See Section 6 (Table 2). 
Contents of the proposed labeling are generally acceptable, but the review finds it 
necessary to edit the proposed text. The edits are made to ensure that the to-be­
approved label reflects the Agency’s current thinking. Highlights indicate recommended 
edits. Underlines indicates addition.  Strikeouts indicate deletion.

are made to the Risk Summary section. The Animal Data section is rewritten as 
indicated below.

present, which leads to uncontrolled increases in plasma kallikrein activity and 
results in angioedema attacks. Berotralstat decreases plasma kallikrein activity to 
control excess bradykinin generation in patients with HAE.

Addendum to Pharmacology/Toxicology Review 
Completed by Dr. Luqi Pei in NDA 214094 on March 24, 2020 (DARRTS 4580256) 
Date:	 July 23, 2020 
NDA No.:	 214094 
Submission No.:	 SDNs 0001 and 29 
Submission date:	 December 3, 2019 and July 9, 2020 
Drug Name:	 Berotralstat 
Reviewer:	 Luqi Pei, PhD 
Subject: 	 Re-evaluation of tumors in the mesenteric lymph nodes in 
male rats in 2-year oral carcinogenicity study of berotralstat 
(Study 801-161) 
This document is an addendum to a nonclinical review completed by Dr. Luqi Pei on 
March 24, 2020 in NDA 214094 (DARRTS ID 4580256). The addendum documents the 
Agency’s re-evaluation of tumor findings in the mesenteric lymph nodes in males in a 2­
year oral carcinogenicity study of berotralstat in rats (Study 806-161). The re-evaluation 
was prompted by the Applicant’s recent submission of an amendment to the final report 
of Study 806-161 (SDN 029). The final report of the study was submitted to the original 
NDA on December 3, 2019 (SDN 0001).  
Dr. Luqi Pei of the Division of Pharmacology and Toxicology for Immunology and 
Inflammation (DPT-II) completed a review of the final report of Study 806-161 on March 
24, 2020 (DARRTS ID 4580256). The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee 
(Exec CAC) evaluated the findings of the study (as reported in the original study report) 
on March 10, 2020. The Committee concluded that berotralstat tested positive for 
carcinogenicity. The conclusion was based on the dose-dependent increase in the 
incidence of hemangiosarcoma in the lymph nodes in male rats. See Exec CAC Meeting 
minutes dated March 16, 2020 (DARRTS ID 4576064). 
BioCryst submitted an amendment to the final report of Study 806-161 on July 9, 2020 
(NDA 214094, eCTD 029). The amendment revised neoplastic findings in the vasculature, 
particularly the mesenteric lymph node in male rats. Table 1 presents the tumor incidence 
in the mesenteric lymph nodes in male rats. 
Table 1: Tumor Findings in the Mesenteric Lymph Node in Males (Study 806-161)

Briefly, the amendment revised both tumor type and incidence in this tissue. The tumor 
type changed from malignant hemangiosarcoma to benign hemangioma. The tumor 
incidence was decreased by approximately half. These differences resulted in a change 
in carcinogenic potential of berotralstat: from positive to negative, according to the current 
evaluation criteria. That is, the original report shows that berotralstat tested positive for 
carcinogenicity while the amended report showed that berotralstat tested negative.  
The revision of the tumor type and incidence was the result of a pathology expert working 
group (EWG) assembled by BioCryst to re-evaluate neoplastic findings in the mesenteric 
lymph nodes. The re-evaluation was prompted by a difference in interpretation between 
the Agency and BioCryst regarding the relevance of the tumor findings to humans, based 
on data presented in the original study report.  
As discussed previously, the Exec CAC concluded that berotralstat tested positive 
because berotralstat at 20 mg/kg/day caused a statistically significant increase in the 
incidence of hemangiosarcoma (Ref. DARRTS ID 4576064). The Committee also 
concluded that the relevance of the finding to humans is unknown. However, BioCryst 
has maintained their position that the findings of hemangiosarcoma in rats is a species-
specific effect. The Agency’s position was conveyed to BioCryst in the Filing 
Communication dated February 14, 2020 and was briefly discussed at the mid-cycle 
meeting with the Applicant. 
BioCryst assembled the EWG to re-evaluate the slides with tumor findings. The EWG 
conveyed on April 28, and May 12, 2020 via videoconference. The group reviewed “H&E 
stained digitized slides of selected tissue sections of proliferative vascular findings in 
mesenteric lymph nodes and all diagnosed hemangiomas and hemangiosarcomas from 
other tissues as reported”. Members of the EWG included Drs. Paul W. Snyder (Chair), 
Jerry Hardisty (reviewing pathologist), Joshua Decker (Study Pathologist), Kenneth 
Schaffer, Andrew Suttie, and Samuel Cohen. The EWG issued its report on June 11, 
2020 (p. 2401 – 2427 of the amended study report). Table 2 summarizes the diagnosis 
of pathological findings in the original report and the EWG.  
The Exec CAC evaluated the findings of the EWG via email communications on July 10, 
2020. Based on the new re-adjudication provided in the amended study report, the 
Committee concluded that berotralstat is negative for tumorigenicity in male rats in Study 
806-161. See the Addendum to the March 16, 2020 Exec CAC minutes completed on 
July 23, 2020 (DARRTS ID 4645569).

Summary 
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in 
Wistar Han [Crl:WI(Han)] rats and one in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous (transgenic) mice. 
These studies were intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of ORLADEYO (BCX7353), 
when administered orally by gavage at appropriate drug levels for 104 weeks in rats and 26 
weeks in mice. 
Rat Study: . Two hundred and forty Wistar Han [Crl:WI(Han)]  rats of each sex were 
randomly assigned to the treated and vehicle control groups in equal size of 60 rats per 
group. The dose levels for treated groups were 3, 8 and 20 mg/kg/day. The rats in the 
vehicle control group received the vehicle(deionized water). 
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in 
mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in male or female rats. The 
pairwise comparisons did not show any statistically significant differences in mortality 
between the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups in male or female rats. 
Tumor analysis: 
1.	 For male rats, the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive dose 
response relationship in incidences of the Hemangiosarcoma, Multicentric (P­
value=0.0018<0.005), Hemangiosarcoma in Lymph Node, Mesenteric (P­
value=0.0001<0.005) and combined tumors of Hemangiosarcoma and Hemangioma in 
whole body (P-value=0.0044<0.005). The pairwise comparison of the 
Hemangiosarcoma in Lymph Node, Mesenteric (P-value=0.0049<0.01) between the 
vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group for male rats is significant at 0.01 
level of significance. The pairwise comparison of the Hemangiosarcoma, Multicentric 
(p-value=0.0166) and) between the vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group 
for male rats is marginally significant at 0.01 level of significance. However, this tumor 
type for male rats is definitely significant at 0.05 level of significance in the joint test 
used by the ECAC but with the more appropriate levels of significance recommended 
in Lin and Rahman (2018, 2019). 
2.	 For female rats,  the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive 
dose response relationship in incidence of the Sarcoma, endometrial stromal, Uterus 
with cervix (P-value=0.0226<0.025).

Mouse Study: One hundred and twenty five hemizygous CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous 
(transgenic) mice of each sex were randomly assigned to the treated, vehicle and positive 
control group in equal size of 25 mice per group. The dose levels for treated groups were 8, 
20, and 50 mg/kg/day for males and females. The mice in the vehicle control group received 
the vehicle (Reverse Osmosis Deionized (RODI) Water). The study was designed to 
continue for up to 26 weeks for both sexes, however in accordance with study termination 
criteria, all surviving mice were sacrificed during Week 27. The mice in the positive control 
group received 75 mg/kg N-Nitrosomethylurea (NMU) in citrate-buffered saline (pH 4.5) 
once on Day 1 via intraperitoneal injection. 
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in 
mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in either male or female mice. 
The pairwise comparisons did not show any statistically significant differences in mortality 
between the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups in either male or female 
mice. 
Tumor analysis: 
1.	 For male mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the 
positive control showed a statistically significant increase in incidence of Lymphoma, 
Hemolymphoreticula (P-value<0.001), Papilloma, Skin (P-value<0.001), Papilloma, 
Stomach (P-value<0.001) and Squamous cell carcinoma, stomach (P-

1. Background 
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in 
Wistar Han [Crl:WI(Han)] rats and one in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous (transgenic) mice. 
These studies were intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of ORLADEYO (BCX7353), 
when administered orally by gavage at appropriate drug levels for 104 weeks in rats and 26 
weeks in mice. Results of this review have been discussed with the reviewing pharmacologist 
Dr. Luqi Pei. This review analyzed the SAS data sets of these studies received from the 
sponsor on December 3, 2019 via NDA214094/0000. 
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component of the 
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor 
incidence rate as the dose increases. 
2. Rat Study 
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in males and one in females. In each of 
these two experiments there were four treated groups and one vehicle control group. Three 
hundred Wistar Han [Crl:WI(Han)]  rats of each sex were randomly assigned to the treated 
and vehicle control groups in equal size of 60 rats per group. The dose levels for treated 
groups were 3, 8, 20 and 60/40 mg/kg/day. The rats in the vehicle control group received 
the vehicle(deionized water). The study for the rats was designed to continue for up to 104 
weeks. In accordance with study termination criteria, all surviving male rats were sacrificed 
during Week 105. 
Animals in 60/40 mg/kg/day group were dosed at 60 mg/kg/day from Week 1-17; from 
Week 18-28 animals were dosed at 40 mg/kg/day. Pharm/Tox reviewer Dr. Pei requested to 
drop the 60/40 mg/kg/day group due to high death rate. We do not include the 60/40 
mg/kg/day group in our analysis.

2.1. Sponsor's analyses 
2.1.1. Survival analysis 
Intercurrent mortality data was analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product-limit method. An 
overall test comparing all groups was conducted using a log-rank test. If this overall test 
was significant (p<0.05) and there were more than two groups, then a follow up analysis 
was done where each treatment group was compared to the control group using a log-rank 
test. Results of all pair-wise comparisons were reported at the 0.05 and 0.01 significance 
levels. All endpoints were analyzed using two-tailed tests. 
Sponsor’s findings: Sponsor’s analysis showed  the numbers (percents) of survival were 43 
(71.67%), 39 (65%), 42 (70%), and 33 (55%) in vehicle control, 3 mg/kg/day, 8 mg/kg/day 
and 20 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively in males and 35 (58.33%), 36 (60%), 37 
(61.67%), and 28 (46.67%) in vehicle control, 3 mg/kg/day, 8 mg/kg/day and 20 mg/kg/day 
dose groups, respectively in females. 
Morbidity and mortalities occurred in the male and females at 60 mg/kg/day, causing the 
dose level to be lowered to 40 mg/kg/day on Day 120. However, even though the dose was 
lowered, the morbidity and mortality continued in this group. Consequently, the animals in 
this group were euthanized on Day 191 (Week 28). 
The sponsor concluded that no significant differences in the survival rate were noted in 
males or females at 3 or 8 mg/kg/day when compared to the control group. The survival 
rate for males and females at 20 mg/kg/day was notably less than the survival rate in the 
control groups, although the difference was not statistically significant. Decreased survival 
in the males and females at 20 mg/kg/day was considered BCX7353-related. 
2.1.2. Tumor data analysis 
Reference ID: 4617511 

[PDF PAGE 187]
                                                       
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
             
           
           
 
 
              
           
               
         
               
                 
             
                
NDA214094                                                                       Page 10 of 45 
Tumor incidence data were analyzed using both survival adjusted and unadjusted tests. The 
unadjusted tests were based on the incidence and number of sites examined for each tumor 
type. The Cochran-Armitage trend test was calculated and Fisher’s exact test14 was used 
to compare each treatment group with the control group. The survival adjusted test was 
conducted according to the prevalence/mortality methods described by Peto et al. 
Evaluation criteria (pvalues of significance) were applied differently for rare tumors 
(background rate of 1% or less) and common tumors (background rate greater than 1%) 
Sponsor’s findings: The sponsor concluded that the hemangiosarcoma, malignant, 
Multicentric neoplasm was significant in both the trend test and the pairwise comparison 
between the vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group for male rats and the 
Sarcoma, endometrial stromal, malignant in Uterus with cervix was significant in both the 
trend test and the pairwise comparison between the vehicle control group and the 20 
mg/kg/day group for female rats. The Sarcoma, undifferentiated, malignant in Skin/skin, 
subcutis was significant in the pairwise comparison between the vehicle control group and 
the 20 mg/kg/day group for male rats. 
2.2. Reviewer's analyses 
To verify sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing 
pharmacologist, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses. Data 
used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on December 3, 
2019, via NDA214094/0000. 
2.2.1. Survival analysis 
The survival distributions of animals in all five groups were estimated by the Kaplan-Meier
product limit method. The dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival
distributions were tested for the vehicle controls, low, medium and high dose groups using the
Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in
Tables 7 and 8 in the appendix for males and females, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves
for survival rate are given in Figures 1 and 2 in the appendix for males and females,
respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals,
are given in Tables 9, and 10 in the appendix for males and females, respectively.

Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers (percents) of survival 
were 43 (71.67%), 39 (65%), 42 (70%), and 33 (55%) in vehicle control, 3 mg/kg/day, 8 
mg/kg/day and 20 mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively in males and 35 (58.33%), 36 (60%), 
37 (61.67%), and 28 (46.67%) in vehicle control, 3 mg/kg/day, 8 mg/kg/day and 20 
mg/kg/day dose groups, respectively in females. 
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in 
mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in male or female rats. The 
pairwise comparisons did not show any statistically significant differences in mortality 
between the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups in male or female rats. 
2.2.2. Tumor data analysis 
The tumor data were analyzed for the positive dose response relationships and the positive 
pairwise comparison increases between each of the treated groups with control group. Both 
the dose response relationship tests and pairwise comparisons were performed using the 
Poly-K method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and Bieler and Williams 
(1993). In this method an animal that lives the full study period ( wmax ) or dies before the 
terminal sacrifice but develops the tumor type being tested gets a score of sh =1. An animal 
k
 

wh  

that dies at week without a tumor before the end of the study gets a score of =
wh sh  
< 1. The adjusted group size is defined as Σ sh . As an interpretation, an animal with score sh 
=1 can be considered as a whole animal while an animal with score sh < 1 can be considered 
as a partial animal. The adjusted group size Σ sh is equal to N (the original group size) if all 
animals live up to the end of the study or if each animal that dies before the terminal sacrifice 
develops at least one tumor, otherwise the adjusted group size is less than N. These adjusted 
group sizes were then used for the dose response relationship (or the pairwise) tests using the 
Cochran-Armitage test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value 
of k, which depends on the tumor incidence pattern with the increased dose. For long term 104 
week standard rat and mouse studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature. Hence, this 
reviewer used k=3 for the analysis of this data. For the calculation of p-values the exact 
permutation method was used. The tumor rates and the p-values for the positive dose 
response relationship tests and pairwise comparisons are listed in Tables 11 and 12 in the 
appendix for male and female rats, respectively.

Adjustment for multiple testing: For the chronic study in rats, the adjustment of multiple 
testing of the dose response relationship for a submission with one chronic rat study and 
one transgenic mouse study, the more recently revised draft (January, 2013) FDA guidance 
for the carcinogenicity studies suggests the use of test levels α =0.005 for common tumors 
and α=0.025 for rare tumors for the chronic rat study. For pairwise comparisonsfor the 
chronic rat study in the above type of submission with one chronic rat study and one 
transgenic mouse study, the same guidance document suggests the use of test levels α 
=0.01 for common tumors and α =0.05 for rare tumors for the chronic rat study. 
It should be noted that the FDA guidance for multiple testing for dose response relationship 
is based on a publication by Lin and Rahman (1998). In this work the authors investigated 
the use of this rule for Peto analysis. However, in a later work Rahman and Lin (2008) 
showed that this rule for multiple testing for dose response relationship is also suitable for 
Poly-K tests. 
Reviewer’s findings: The tumor types in Tables 2 and 3 below showed p-values less than or 
equal to 0.05 in the trend tests and the tests for pairwise comparisons between the vehicle 
and the treated groups for male and female rats, respectively. Based on the above criterion 
for multiple testing adjustment, we make the folloing statistical conclusions: 
1.	 For male rats, the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive dose 
response relationship in incidences of the Hemangiosarcoma, Multicentric (P­
value=0.0018<0.005), Hemangiosarcoma in Lymph Node, Mesenteric (P­
value=0.0001<0.005) and combined tumors of Hemangiosarcoma and Hemangioma in 
whole body (P-value=0.0044<0.005). The pairwise comparison of the 
Hemangiosarcoma in Lymph Node, Mesenteric (P-value=0.0049<0.01) between the 
vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group for male rats is significant at 0.01 
level of significance. The pairwise comparison of the Hemangiosarcoma, Multicentric 
(p-value=0.0166) and) between the vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group 
for male rats is marginally significant at 0.01 level of significance. However, this tumor 
type for male rats is definitely significant at 0.05 level of significance in the joint test 
used by the ECAC but with the more appropriate levels of significance recommended 
in Lin and Rahman (2018, 2019). 
2.	 For female rats,  the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive

3. Mouse Study 
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in males and one in females. In each of 
these two experiments there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group, and one 
positive control group. One hundred and twenty five hemizygous CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 
hemizygous (transgenic) mice of each sex were randomly assigned to the treated, vehicle 
and positive control group in equal size of 25 mice per group. The dose levels for treated 
groups were 8, 20, and 50 mg/kg/day for males and females. The mice in the vehicle control 
group received the vehicle (Reverse Osmosis Deionized (RODI) Water). The study was 
designed to continue for up to 26 weeks for both sexes, however in accordance with study 
termination criteria, all surviving mice were sacrificed during Week 27. The mice in the 
positive control group received 75 mg/kg N-Nitrosomethylurea (NMU) in citrate-buffered 
saline (pH 4.5) once on Day 1 via intraperitoneal injection.

3.1. Sponsor's analyses 
3.1.1. Survival analysis 
The sponsor used the same survival analysis methods used for the rats study in this mouse 
study. 
Sponsor’s findings: The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers (percents) of death 
were 1 (4%), 1 (4%), 1 (4%), 3 (12%), and 17 (68%) in male mice, and 0 (0%), 1 (4%), 3 
(12%), 2 (8%), and 25 (100%) in female mice in vehicol control, low, medium, high dose 
groups and positive control group, respectively. 
Reference ID: 4617511 

[PDF PAGE 192]
                                                       
 
 
  
 
 
 
             
           
            
 
 
              
            
              
              
                 
                
              
              
             
            
                       
NDA214094                                                                       Page 15 of 45 
The sponsor concluded that there were no statistically significant differences in survival for 
any BCX7353-treated groups in either sex relative to control groups. 
3.1.2. Tumor data analysis 
The sponsor used the same tumor data analysis methods used for the rat study in this 
mouse study 
Sponsor’s findings: The sponsor concluded that there were no statistically significant 
findings among males or females for tumor incidence. 
3.2. Reviewer's analyses 
To verify sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing 
pharmacologist, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses. Data 
used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on December 3, 
2019, via NDA214094/0000. 
3.2.1. Survival analysis 
The survival distributions of three treated groups, one vehical control group, and one positive 
control group were estimated using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. The dose 
response relationship in survival was tested using the likelihood ratio test and the homogeneity 
of survival distributions was tested using the log-rank test. The Kaplan-Meier curves for 
survival rates are given in Figures 3 and 4 in the appendix for male and female mice, 
respectively. The intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables 13 and 14 in the appendix for 
male and female mice, respectively. Results of the tests for dose response relationship and 
homogeneity of survivals among the vehicle control and three treated groups are given in 
Tables 15 and 16 in the appendix for male and female mice, respectively. 
Reviewer’s findings: This reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers (percents) of death 
were 1 (4%), 1 (4%), 1 (4%), 3 (12%), and 17 (68%) in male mice, and 0 (0%), 1 (4%), 3 (12%), 2 
Reference ID: 4617511 

[PDF PAGE 193]
                                                       
                
   
            
               
            
                
 
  
              
            
                   
   
  
   
 
   
 
 
  
 
  
  
NDA214094                                                                       Page 16 of 45 
(8%), and 25 (100%) in female mice in vehicol control, low, medium, high dose groups and 
positive control group, respectively. 
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in 
mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in either male or female mice. 
The pairwise comparisons did not show any statistically significant differences in mortality 
between the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups in either male or female 
mice. 
3.2.2. Tumor data analysis 
The reviewer used the same tumor data analysis methods for the rat study in this mouse 
study. 
The tumor rates and the p-values for the positive dose response relationship tests and 
pairwise comparisons between vehicle control and three treated groups, and between vehicle 
control and positive control are listed in Tables 17, 18, 19, and 20 in the appendix for male and 
female mice, respectively. 
Adjustment for multiple testing: For the adjustment of multiple testing of dose response 
relationship for the transgenic mouse study in a submission with one chronic rat study and 
one transgenic mouse study, the more recently revised draft (January, 2013) FDA guidance 
for the carcinogenicity studies suggests the use of test levels α =0.05 for both common 
tumors and rare tumors for the mouse study. For pairwise, the same guidance document 
suggests the use of test levels α =0.05 for both common tumors and rare tumors for the 
mouse study. 
It should be noted that the FDA guidance for multiple testing for dose response relationship 
is based on a publication by Lin and Rahman (1998). In this work the authors investigated 
the use of this rule for Peto analysis. However, in a later work Rahman and Lin (2008) 
showed that this rule for multiple testing for dose response relationship is also suitable for 
Poly-K tests. 
Reviewer’s findings: The tumor types in Tables 5 and 6 below showed p-values less than or

groups for male mice and female mice, respectively.

4.	 Conclusion 
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in 
Wistar Han [Crl:WI(Han)] rats and one in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous (transgenic) mice. 
These studies were intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of ORLADEYO (BCX7353), 
when administered orally by gavage at appropriate drug levels for 104 weeks in rats and 26 
weeks in mice. 
Rat Study: . Two hundred and forty Wistar Han [Crl:WI(Han)]  rats of each sex were 
randomly assigned to the treated and vehicle control groups in equal size of 60 rats per 
group. The dose levels for treated groups were 3, 8 and 20 and mg/kg/day. The rats in the 
vehicle control group received the vehicle(deionized water). 
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in 
mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in male or female rats. The 
pairwise comparisons did not show any statistically significant differences in mortality 
between the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups in male or female rats. 
Tumor analysis: 
1.	 For male rats, the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive dose 
response relationship in incidences of the Hemangiosarcoma, Multicentric (P­
value=0.0018<0.005), Hemangiosarcoma in Lymph Node, Mesenteric (P­
value=0.0001<0.005) and combined tumors of Hemangiosarcoma and Hemangioma in 
whole body (P-value=0.0044<0.005). The pairwise comparison of the 
Hemangiosarcoma in Lymph Node, Mesenteric (P-value=0.0049<0.01) between the 
vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group for male rats is significant at 0.01 
level of significance. The pairwise comparison of the Hemangiosarcoma, Multicentric 
(p-value=0.0166) and) between the vehicle control group and the 20 mg/kg/day group 
for male rats is marginally significant at 0.01 level of significance. However, this tumor 
type for male rats is definitely significant at 0.05 level of significance in the joint test 
used by the ECAC but with the more appropriate levels of significance recommended 
in Lin and Rahman (2018, 2019). 
2.	 For female rats,  the tumor data analysis showed a statistically significant positive 
dose response relationship in incidence of the Sarcoma, endometrial stromal, Uterus 
Reference ID: 4617511 

[PDF PAGE 197]
                                                       
 
 
 
 
            
               
            
                
 
 
  
                                                                                                               
                                                                                                          
NDA214094	                                                                       Page 20 of 45 
with cervix (P-value=0.0226<0.025). 
Mouse Study: One hundred and twenty five hemizygous CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous 
(transgenic) mice of each sex were randomly assigned to the treated, vehicle and positive 
control group in equal size of 25 mice per group. The dose levels for treated groups were 8, 
20, and 50 mg/kg/day for males and females. The mice in the vehicle control group received 
the vehicle (Reverse Osmosis Deionized (RODI) Water). The study was designed to 
continue for up to 26 weeks for both sexes, however in accordance with study termination 
criteria, all surviving mice were sacrificed during Week 27. The mice in the positive control 
group received 75 mg/kg N-Nitrosomethylurea (NMU) in citrate-buffered saline (pH 4.5) 
once on Day 1 via intraperitoneal injection. 
The survival analyses didn’t show any statistically significant dose response relationship in 
mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in either male or female mice. 
The pairwise comparisons did not show any statistically significant differences in mortality 
between the vehicle control group and each of the treated groups in either male or female 
mice. 
Tumor analysis: 
1.	 For male mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the 
positive control showed a statistically significant increase in incidence of Lymphoma, 
Hemolymphoreticula (P-value<0.001), Papilloma, Skin (P-value<0.001), Papilloma, 
Stomach (P-value<0.001) and Squamous cell carcinoma, stomach (P­
value=0.0073<0.05) 
2.	 For female mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the 
positive control showed statistically significant increases in incidence of Lymphoma, 
Hemolymphoreticula (P-value<0.001), Papilloma, Skin (P-value<0.001), Papilloma, 
Stomach (P-value<0.001) and Combined tumor of Papilloma and Carcinoma, Skin 
(P-value<0.001)

NDA #214094 Reviewer: Luqi Pei, PhD 
1 Executive Summary 
1.1 Introduction 
This nonclinical review evaluates the carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat 
(BCX7353) in animal studies. Berotralstat is a kallikrein inhibitor being developed 
as a therapy for the prevention of hereditary angioedema (HAE) attacks in adult 
and adolescent patients. The carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat was 
evaluated in a 2-year traditional bioassay in rats and a 26-week alternative animal 
model in mice (Reports 806-161 and 20088905, respectively). Results showed that 
berotralstat was carcinogenic in rats, but not in mice. 
1.2 Brief Discussion of Nonclinical Findings 
The carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat was evaluated in a 2-year traditional 
bioassay in Wistar rats and a 26-week study in B6F1 Tg.rasH2 mice. Rats 
(60/sex/dose) were dosed by oral gavage with 0 (vehicle: deionized water), 3, 8, 
and 20-mg/kg berotralstat once daily for 104 weeks. Mice (25/sex/dose) were 
dosed by oral gavage 0 (vehicle: deionized water), 8, 20, and 50-mg/kg berotralstat 
once daily for 26 weeks. The male rats showed evidence of tumorigenicity as 
discussed below. No evidence of tumorigenicity was found in mice or female rats. 
The male rats showed statistically significant, dose-related increases in the 
incidence of hemangiosarcomas (HS) in the mesenteric lymph nodes (Table 1). In 
pairwise comparisons between the high dose (HD) and controls (C), only the 
increase in the HS incidence in the mesenteric lymph nodes reached statistical 
significance (p < 0.01); test for HS in the whole body reached a marginal 
significance (p=0.017); test for the HS and hemangioma in combination in the 
whole body did not reach statistical significance (p=0.041). The trend analyses for 
all the above tumor findings were significant (p < 0.005). 
Table 1: Incidence of Hemangiosarcoma in Male Rats 
Tumor, organ Sex 
0 
Incidence (n = 60) 
3 8 20 
p value 
Pairwise a Trend 
Hemangiosarcoma (HS), total M 2 2 4 9 (15.0%) 0.0166 0.002
 Mesenteric lymph node M 1 1 2 9 (15.0%) 0.0049 0.0001
 Skin/adipose tissues M 1 1 2 0 - b -
HS + hemangioma, total M 3 2 4 9 (15.0%) 0.041 0.004 
a. Pairwise comparison between HD and C groups. 
b. (-), not calculated. 
It is a common practice in the evaluation of tumorigenesis to conduct statistical 
analysis of the HS and hemangioma in the whole body because these tumors are 
generally present in multiple organs and have similar mechanism of tumorigenesis. 
Berotralstat induced HS specifically in the mesenteric lymph node in males. The 
organ and tumor specificity of berotralstat may render it more appropriate to 
conduct a tissue specific analysis of tumorigenesis. 
4 
Reference ID: 4580256 

[PDF PAGE 228]
  
 
    
  
 
 
    
 
 
   
    
 
 
NDA #214094 Reviewer: Luqi Pei, PhD 
The mechanism of tumorigenesis for berotralstat remains unclear. The applicant 
claims that the mechanism for berotralstat-induced tumorigenesis in rats is not 
relevant for humans. However, the Division doesn’t agree with this claim at this 
point based on the available data. 
The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (Exec. CAC) of the Center 
evaluated the above findings in a meeting held on March 10, 2020 [see meeting 
minutes, DARRTS ID# 2576054]. The Committee concluded the following: 
1. Both rat and mouse studies were adequate to evaluate the carcinogenicity 
potential of berotralstat. 
2. Berotralstat tested positive for evidence of carcinogenicity in male rats. The 
20-mg/kg/day berotralstat dose for 2-years caused statistically significant 
increases in the incidence of hemangiosarcomas in the mesenteric lymph 
node in males. No increases in the incidence of this tumor were observed at 
≤ 8 mg/kg/day. Relevance of the tumorigenicity data in rats to humans is 
unknown. 
3. Berotralstat tested negative for tumorigenicity in both sexes in mice and 
females in rats.

2.7 Regulatory Background 
BioCryst Pharmaceuticals filed NDA 214094 seeking market registration of 
berotralstat for the prevention of HAE in adult and adolescent patients on 
December 3, 2019. The development program of the application was completed 
in IND 135038.  
The carcinogenicity potential of berotralstat was evaluated in a 2-year traditional 
bioassay in rats and a 26-week study in B6F1 Tg.RasH2 mice (Reports #806-161 
and #20088905, respectively). The Agency concurred with the dose selection of 
the protocol of the 26-week study in transgenic mice on December 14, 2017. The 
Applicant did not seek the Agency’s input on the dose selection of the rat study 
protocol.

4 Pharmacology 
Berotralstat is a small molecule kallikrein inhibitor. Kallikrein is a plasma enzyme 
responsible for the production of bradykinin, a vasodilatory agent. Kallikrein cleaves and 
releases bradykinin from high-molecular-weight keninogen. Bradykinin binds to the 
6
 
Reference ID: 4580256 

[PDF PAGE 230]
  
   
   
    
  
   
 
   
   
     
  
    
   
   
 
 
 
 
NDA #214094 Reviewer: Luqi Pei, PhD 
bradykinin B2 receptor and results in dilation of blood vessel, fluid leakage, and edema.  
Berotralstat inhibited purified plasma human kallikrein (IC50 = 5.83 nM). 
5 Pharmacokinetics/ADME/Toxicokinetics 
5.1 PK/ADME 
Berotralstat was quickly absorbed after oral administration in animals. Plasma drug levels 
increased generally in proportion to the administered doses. There were no significant 
differences in plasma drug levels between sexes. The half-life was 18.5 and 22.7 hr in 
rats and monkeys, respectively. The oral bioavailability was 33% and 45% in rats and 
monkeys, respectively. The protein binding capacity was high in humans, rats, and mice 
(98.7-99.8%) but lower in monkeys and rabbits (74.1-81.9%). The tissues showing the 
highest maximum concentrations of radioactivity included liver, kidney medulla, spleen, 
adrenal gland(s), kidney(s), lungs, and pituitary gland. Metabolism of berotralstat was 
low across species. About 61% of orally administered berotralstat was excreted as 
unchanged in rats. Less than 10% of berotralstat was metabolized when it was incubated 
with human cryopreserved hepatocytes for 360 minutes.  
5.2 Toxicokinetics 
Bioaccumulation of BCX7353 occurred in rats, but not in monkeys. Table 2 presents 
plasma drug AUC levels in rats and monkeys in chronic toxicity studies. In rats, 
berotralstat AUCs in week 26 were approximately 3 times the AUCs at week 1. The 
monkeys showed no apparent difference in AUCs between Weeks 1 and 39.

7 Genetic Toxicity 
Berotralstat tested negative in the Ames test, a chromosomal aberration assay in human 
peripheral blood lymphocytes in vitro, and an in vivo micronucleus assay in rats.

NDA#2 14094 	 Reviewer : Luqi Pei, PhD 
8 Carcinogenicity 
8.1 	Two-Year Rat Study (806-161) 
Study title: BCX7353: A 104-Week Oral Oncogenicity Study in Rats 
Study no.: 806-161 
Study report location : 4.2.3.4.1 
Conducting laboratory and -------------- ltiHil 
location : 
Date of study initiation: Septem ber 1, 2016 
GLP compliance: Yes 
QA statement: Yes 
Drug, lot #, and % purity: BCX7353.2HCL, 0000007963 , 99.1 % 
CAC concurrence: No 
Key Study Findings 
• 	 Wistar rats dosed by oral gavage with 0 (deionized water), 3, 8, or 20-mg/kg/day 
berotralstat for 104 weeks showed dose-related increases in the incidence of the 
following malignant tumors: hemangiosarcoma (HS) in the mesenteric lymph 
nodes in males and females, undifferentiated sarcoma in the skin in males, and 
endometrial stromal sarcoma in the uterus in females. 
• 	 Only the increase in the incidence of HS in the mesenteric lymph node in HD males 
reached statistical significance in both the trend test (p < 0.005) and pairwise 
analyses (p < 0.01 ). 
• 	 Pairwise analysis for HS in HD males (whole body) was considered marginally 
significant (p=0.0166), although the trend analysis was significant (p < 0.005). 
• 	 Pairwise comparison for HS and hemangioma in combination in the whole body 
was not statistically significant (p=0.041 ), although the trend analysis was 
significant (p < 0.005). 
• 	 Pairwise analysis for neither undifferentiated sarcoma in the skin in males nor 
endometrial stromal sarcoma in the uterus in females was statistically significant. 
• 	 The HD group showed decreases in mean body weights (by approximately 17.6% 
and 13.5% in males and females, respectively). These rats also showed audible 
breathing in the clinical observations. The audible breathing was apparently due 
to presence of excrete (exudate) in the nasal passage. 
• 	 See Table 6 below for the incidence of the above tumors. 
Adequacy of Carcinogenicity Study 
This study is considered adequate. See minutes of the March 10, 2020 Exec CAC 
meeting (DARRTS ID 4576064). The duration of treatment (i.e., 104 weeks) is adequate. 
The dose selection seems to be appropriate. The high dose of 20 mg/kg/day was one­
third to half of the lethal dose of 40 - 60 mg/kg/day. [see Footnote 1 on page 1 O]. Dose­
related responses in both neoplastic and non-neoplastic findings were observed.

NDA #214094 Reviewer: Luqi Pei, PhD 
Neoplastic responses were discussed above in the Key Study Finding section. Non-
neoplastic findings are listed in Table 7. Briefly, treatment-related lesions were observed 
in several organs and tissues in the HD group: adrenal glands, kidney, lung, lymph nodes, 
skeletal muscle, intestine, and spleen.  The lesions were mostly cell vacuolations. 
The Agency did not concur with the dose selection of berotralstat for the 2-year rat study 
because the applicant did not seek the Agency’s input on the dose selection. However, 
the study results as discussed in the review indicated that the dose-selection was 
appropriate.   
Appropriateness of Test Model
The model is appropriate. This strain of rats was used in the repeat-dose toxicity studies 
of berotralstat. The rats showed dose and temporal effects of berotralstat in oral studies 
up to 26 weeks in treatment duration. 
Evaluation of Tumor Findings
Berotralstat is an epigenetic carcinogen in rats. Male rats dosed with 20-mg/kg/day 
berotralstat (HD dose) orally for 2 years showed statistically significant increases in the 
incidence of HS in the mesenteric lymph node (Tables 6 and 7). Hemangiosarcoma is a 
common malignant tumor in rats.  
Dr. Zhuang Miao conducted the statistical analysis of the data. A draft review was 
provided to the DPARP nonclinical team on February 14, 2020. Dr. Miao conducted the 
following two tests in the review: trend test and pairwise comparison. The review uses the 
following criteria below to define a positive result: 
1) Trend test alone: 0.005 and 0.025 for common and rare tumors, respectively 
2) Pairwise comparison alone: 0.01 and 0.05 for common and rare tumors, 
respectively. 
3) Joint tests (trend + pairwise): respective p values for common and rare tumors are 
0.005 and 0.025 in trend test and 0.05 and 0.1 in pairwise comparisons 
HS is considered a common tumor in rats. Only HS in the mesenteric lymph node in high-
dose male rats met all 3 criteria above and was considered positive. 
Pairwise comparison for the incidence of HS between the HD and C groups in whole body 
was marginally significant (p= 0.017). Pairwise comparison for the incidence of HS and 
hemangioma in combination between the HD and Control groups was insignificant 
(p=0.041), although trend analysis showed positive dose responses (p < 0.005).   
There were also dose-related increases in the incidence of HS in HD females, 
undifferentiated sarcoma in the skin in males, and endometrial stromal sarcoma in the 
uterus in females. None of these increases reached statistical significance in pairwise 
comparison. Skin sarcoma and endometrial stromal sarcoma were considered rare 
tumors for this analysis. 
9

The HD group showed decreases in mean body weight, audible breathing, and presence 
of excrete (exudate) in the nasal passage. It is unclear whether these observations had 
any major impact on the tumorigenicity of berotralstat in rats.
